Rickman rugs and intrinsic bilipschitz graphs Thanks: T.O. is supported by the Academy of Finland via the project Quantitative rectifiability in Euclidean and non-Euclidean spaces , grant Nos. 309365, 314172, and via the project Incidences on Fractals , grant No. 321896.
Tuomas Orponen

Abstract
. This paper studies the geometry of bilipschitz maps f : 𝕎 → ℍ f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H} , where ℍ \mathbb{H} is the first Heisenberg group, and 𝕎 ⊂ ℍ \mathbb{W}\subset\mathbb{H} is a vertical subgroup of co-dimension 1 1 . The images f ⁡ ( 𝕎 ) f(\mathbb{W}) of such maps are called Rickman rugs in the Heisenberg group. The main theorem states that a Rickman rug in the Heisenberg group admits a corona decomposition by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs. As a corollary, Rickman rugs are countably rectifiable by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs. Here, an intrinsic bilipschitz graph is an intrinsic Lipschitz graph, which is simultaneously a Rickman rug. General intrinsic Lipschitz graphs need not be Rickman rugs, even locally, by an example of Bigolin and Vittone.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
28A75 (Primary), 28A78 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to introduce the notion of
intrinsic bilipschitz graphs
in the first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}
, and show that bilipschitz images of the parabolic plane inside
\mathbb{H}
are countably rectifiable by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs – in a strong quantitative sense. Before the details, I will explain why such a result might be useful, and how it is connected to the surrounding area of research on
quantitative rectifiability in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, Lipschitz graphs, and domains bounded by Lipschitz graphs, are basic building blocks for performing harmonic analysis on "non-smooth" surfaces and domains. To name a prototypical example, consider the problem of showing that a singular integral operator (SIO) is
L^{2}
-bounded on a surface
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By the works of Calderón, Coifman, David, Macintosh, and Meyer
[
6
,
12
,
11
]
, it is known that SIOs with odd kernels are
L^{2}
-bounded on Lipschitz graphs. With this fundamental result in hand, there are at least two distinct "transference principles" which enable one to treat more general surfaces: David
[
17
,
18
]
developed the notion of
big pieces of Lipschitz graphs
, and David, Jones, and Semmes
[
15
,
26
,
36
]
pioneered the notion of
corona decompositions by Lipschitz graphs
. The rough idea is that surfaces with either big pieces of, or a corona decomposition by, Lipschitz graphs behave just as well as Lipschitz graphs relative to many harmonic analysis problems. For example, SIOs with odd kernels are
L^{2}
-bounded on such surfaces. This paradigm was formalised in the monographs
[
15
,
16
]
of David and Semmes, which gave birth to the vibrant research area of quantitative rectifiability.

There have been recent attempts to work out a theory of quantitative rectifiability in the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Following the train of thought outlined above, one should identify the correct "building blocks" for performing harmonic analysis on surfaces, that is, the "Lipschitz graphs of the Heisenberg group", then establish transference principles to treat more general surfaces, and of course verify that interesting classes of general surfaces satisfy the hypotheses of the transference principles.

Regarding the question of "building blocks", there is an obvious answer: the
intrinsic Lipschitz graphs
(iLGs) introduced by Franchi, Serapioni, and Serra Cassano
[
24
]
in 2006. These share many of the elementary properties of Lipschitz graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
: for example, iLGs are Ahlfors-regular, have approximate tangents almost everywhere by
[
25
]
, are invariant under group automorphisms, and can be extended. There is a growing mountain of evidence showing that iLGs are solid building blocks for a theory of
qualitative rectifiability
in Heisenberg groups: for a few references, see
[
1
,
3
,
14
,
23
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
37
,
39
]
.

There is also evidence, but not so compelling, that intrinsic Lipschitz graphs are suitable building blocks for a theory quantitative rectifiability in Heisenberg groups. In the first Heisenberg group, natural SIOs are known to be
L^{2}
-bounded on
1
-dimensional iLGs by
[
22
]
, but the same problem remains open for
1
-codimensional iLGs. Under an "
\epsilon
" of additional regularity, the answer is positive by
[
8
,
21
]
. Very little is known about other classical topics in harmonic analysis, such as boundary value problems for (sub-elliptic) PDEs, or the behaviour of of (sub-)harmonic measure on domains bounded by iLGs; see the introduction to
[
35
]
for a description of the current state-of-the art on these questions.

We arrive at a point which is centrally relevant to the current paper: it is conceivable that
1
-codimensional iLGs in the first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}
are
not
suitable building blocks for a theory of quantitative rectifiability, but they are suitable in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
for
n\geq 2
. The recent preprint
[
10
]
of Chousionis, Li, and Young shows that
1
-codimensional iLGs in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, for
n\geq 2
, admit approximations by planes in a quantitative sense which precisely matches the Euclidean behaviour of Lipschitz graphs. In contrast, an example in the preprint
[
33
]
by Naor and Young suggests that the same is not true for
1
-codimensional iLGs in
\mathbb{H}
. In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it is known by
[
15
, (C1)
\Longleftrightarrow
(C3)]
that the correct rate of affine approximation is
equivalent
with the
L^{2}
-boundedness of SIOs. So, in the light of current information, one may speculate that natural SIOs are
L^{2}
-bounded on
1
-codimensional iLGs in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, but not in
\mathbb{H}
. This remains a key open problem in the area.

1.1.
Intrinsic bilipschitz graphs

In the sequel, "iLG" refers to a
1
-codimensional iLG in the first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}
. These sets are homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
3
-regular relative to the metric in
\mathbb{H}
. If iLGs are not suitable building blocks for a quantative theory of rectifiability in
\mathbb{H}
– this remains unclear – which sets could take their place? In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the notions of "
m
-dimensional Lipschitz graph" and "bilipschitz image of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
" are fairly interchangeable from the harmonic analysis point of view, at least for the
L^{2}
-theory of SIOs. Indeed, bilipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
inside
\mathbb{R}^{n}
have big pieces of Lipschitz graphs by
[
19
, Theorem 1.14]
(combined with a theorem of Väisälä
[
38
]
as explained in
[
19
, p. 857]
). They also admit corona decompositions by Lipschitz graphs; in fact, big pieces imply corona decompositions, which in turn imply
(
big pieces
)^{2}
, see
[
2
,
5
]
. Conversely, every
(n-1)
-dimensional Lipschitz graph is trivially a bilipschitz image of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

In the first Heisenberg group, analogous questions concern the relationship between iLGs and bilipschitz images of the
parabolic plane

\mathbb{W}:=(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{\mathrm{par}})
, where

(1.1)

d_{\mathrm{par}}((x,t),(\xi,\tau))=\max\{|x-\xi|,|t-\tau|^{1/2}\}.

In the sequel, bilipschitz images of the parabolic plane in
\mathbb{H}
will be called
Rickman rugs
for brevity. In stark contrast to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, iLGs are not Rickman rugs, not even locally. A counterexample is due to Bigolin and Vittone
[
4
]
. This point cannot be over-emphasised: it is, in fact, an open problem to determine if general iLGs can even be covered by countably many Lipschitz images of compact subsets of
\mathbb{W}
, up to an
\mathcal{H}^{3}
null set. The strongest partial result is
[
20
]
, which yields a positive answer under an "
\epsilon
" of extra regularity.

So, iLGs are definitely not Rickman rugs. Are Rickman rugs iLGs? Yes, almost:

Proposition 1.2
.

A Rickman rug has big pieces of intrinsic Lipschitz graphs.

This proposition is not difficult: one verifies that Rickman rugs have
big vertical projections
(see Proposition
5.3
) and satisfy the
weak geometric lemma
(see Corollary
4.20
). Then, the proposition follows from
[
7
, Theorem 1.2]
, which is a Heisenberg variant of
[
19
, Theorem 1.14]
. However, the proposition is as unsatisfactory as it is easy: iLGs are not Rickman rugs, so Proposition
1.2
wastes information. Given the apparent difficulties in using iLGs in the theory of quantitative rectifiability, Proposition
1.2
looks particularly useless. The main theorem has now been sufficiently motivated:

Theorem 1.3
.

A Rickman rug has a corona decomposition by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs.

Here, an
intrinsic bilipschitz graph
is simply an intrinsic Lipschitz graph which is also a Rickman rug. So, in contrast to Proposition
1.2
, one cannot end up from a rock to a hard place by applying Theorem
1.3
; one perhaps remains on the rock, but at least gains an intrinsic graph parametrisation.

The notion of a corona decomposition is defined in Section
1.2
. It is slightly weaker than "big pieces of iLGs", but typically strong enough for applications in harmonic analysis. The recent preprint
[
5
]
shows, in general metric spaces, that corona decompositions are equivalent to
(
big pieces
)^{2}
. In other words, Rickman rugs have big pieces of sets which have big pieces of iLGs. A corona decomposition implies qualitative rectifiability: every Rickman rug can be covered, up to an
\mathcal{H}^{3}
null set, by countably many intrinsic
bi
lipschitz graphs, see Remark
1.10
. The following quantification still remains open:

Question 1
.

Do Rickman rugs have big pieces of intrinsic bilipschitz graphs?

Remark 1.4
.

With Theorem
1.3
in hand, an obvious question concerns the regularity of intrinsic bilipschitz graphs, but this will have to wait until another paper. For readers familiar with the theory of intrinsic Lipschitz graphs, the intrinsic
bi
lipschitz graphs are precisely those whose
characteristic straightening map

(s,t)\mapsto(s,\tau(s,t))
is bilipschitz
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. (This map is commonly denoted
(s,t)\mapsto(s,\chi(s,t))
, see for example
[
3
, p. 3]
or
[
34
, Definition 3.1]
). So, the
characteristics
of an intrinsic bilipschitz function neither converge nor diverge:
|\tau(s,t_{1})-\tau(s,t_{2})|\sim|t_{1}-t_{2}|
for all
s,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Maintaining this property is a challenge in proving Theorem
1.3
, see condition
(\tau_{2})
of Proposition
7.3
.

Since it is not necessary for this paper, and would take a non-trivial amount of space, I omit a discussion on (the existence and uniqueness of) characteristic curves associated with general intrinsic Lipschitz functions; see
[
3
]
or
[
33
, Section 2.3]
.

Remark 1.5
.

The paper
[
33
]
is entitled "Foliated corona decompositions", and its main result,
[
33
, Theorem 1.17]
, states that an iLG in
\mathbb{H}
has a
foliated corona decomposition
. As far as I understand, the definition of "corona decomposition" in
[
33
]
is different than the one used in the present paper (which is the one defined e.g. by David and Semmes in
[
15
]
). Notably, one cannot deduce Theorem
1.3
by concatenating Proposition
1.2
and
[
33
, Theorem 1.17]
, and the arguments here have no overlap with those in
[
33
]
– as far as this is possible for two papers on closely related questions.

1.2.
Corona decompositions

In this section, I define the notion of corona decompositions properly, and also fix metric notations for
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{W}
. Corona decompositions can even be meaningfully defined in metric spaces, see
[
5
]
, and the notion below is just a specialisation to Rickman rugs and intrinsic bilipschitz graphs.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the family of dyadic parabolic rectangles, that is rectangles of the form
Q=I\times J\subset\mathbb{W}
, where
I,J\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}}
are standard dyadic intervals in
\mathbb{R}
, and
|J|=|I|^{2}
. In other words, dyadic parabolic rectangles have the form

Q=[k2^{-n},(k+1)2^{-n})\times[l4^{-n},(l+1)4^{-n}),\qquad k,l,n\in\mathbb{Z}.

The
side-length
of a rectangle
Q=I\times J\in\mathcal{D}
is defined as
\ell(Q):=|I|
. Note that, since the parabolic metric was defined with the
L^{\infty}
-formula (
1.1
), we have
\operatorname{diam}(Q)=\ell(Q)
for
Q\in\mathcal{D}
. For
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, let
c_{Q}
be the centre of
Q
. For
\lambda>0
, define

\lambda Q:=B_{\mathrm{par}}(c_{Q},\lambda\ell(Q)):=\{w\in\mathbb{W}:d_{\mathrm{par}}(w,c_{Q})\leq\lambda\ell(Q)\}.

This notation may appear a little awkward because
1Q\neq Q
, but it has a benefit: we will often define concepts for (parabolic) balls, such as "
\beta(B(w,r))
", and then the same definitions apply without separate comment to
\lambda Q
. Also, since
d_{\mathrm{par}}
was defined as the
L^{\infty}
-metric on
\mathbb{W}
, parabolic balls of radius
r
are rectangles of (Euclidean) dimensions
2r\times 2r^{2}
, and in particular
\lambda Q
is a rectangle of dimensions
2\lambda\ell(Q)\times 2(\lambda\ell(Q))^{2}
.

Corona decompositions involve the notion of
trees
in
\mathcal{D}
, which are defined next:

Definition 1.6

(Tree)
.

A family
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
is a
tree
if the following conditions are met:

(T1)

There is a
root

Q(\mathcal{T})\in\mathcal{T}
such that all rectangles in
\mathcal{T}
are contained in
Q(\mathcal{T})
.

(T2)

Trees are
connected
: if
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\in\mathcal{D}
are rectangles with
Q_{1}\subset Q_{2}\subset Q_{3}
, and
Q_{1},Q_{3}\in\mathcal{T}
, then also
Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}
.

(T3)

Trees are
coherent
: if
Q\in\mathcal{T}
, then either all the
\mathcal{D}
-children of
Q
are contained in
\mathcal{T}
, or then none of them are.

The rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{T}
with no
\mathcal{D}
-children in

\mathcal{D}
are called the
leaves of
\mathcal{T}
, denoted
\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})
.

We may then define corona decompositions:

Definition 1.7

(Corona decomposition)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
. We say that
f
(or its image) has a
corona decomposition by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs
if the following holds for every parameter
\eta>0
. The family
\mathcal{D}
can be partitioned into disjoint collections of
bad rectangles

\mathcal{B}
and
good rectangles

\mathcal{G}
, which may depend on
\eta
, and which have the following properties.

(C1)

The bad rectangles satisfy a Carleson packing condition: there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(M,\eta)>0
such that

\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}|Q|\leq C_{1}|Q_{0}|,\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

(C2)

The good rectangles are partitioned into a countable family of
trees

\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\ldots\subset\mathcal{G}
. Every tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is associated with an intrinsic bilipschitz graph
\Gamma_{j}\subset\mathbb{H}
in such a way that

(1.8)

\operatorname{dist}(f(w),\Gamma_{j})\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q),\qquad w\in 2Q,\,Q\in\mathcal{T}_{j}.

(C3)

The top rectangles
Q(\mathcal{T}_{j})
of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\ldots
satisfy a Carleson packing condition: there exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(M,\eta)>0
such that

\mathop{\sum_{j\geq 1}}_{Q(\mathcal{T}_{j})\subset Q_{0}}|Q(\mathcal{T}_{j})|\leq C_{2}|Q_{0}|,\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

The choice of "
2
" in (
1.8
) is quite arbitrary, and could be replaced by any number
k\geq 2
. In fact, the existence of a corona decomposition with factor "
2
" formally implies the existence of corona decompositions with factor "
k\geq 2
", see
[
15
, p. 20]
.

Notation 1.9
.

The notation "
|A|
" in (
C1
) and (
C3
) refers to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{W}
(or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
), which coincides, up to a multiplicative constant, with the
3
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{3}=\mathcal{H}^{3}_{d_{\mathrm{par}}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q)\sim|Q|\sim\ell(Q)^{3}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}
. The distance in (
1.8
) is defined relative to the metric
d(p,q):=\|q^{-1}\cdot p\|
, where

\|(x,y,t)\|:=\max\{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},\sqrt{|t|}\},

and
(x_{1},y_{1},t_{1})\cdot(x_{2},y_{2},t_{2}):=(x_{1}+x_{2},y_{1}+y_{2},t_{1}+t_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{1}y_{2}-x_{2}y_{1}))
is the Heisenberg group law. This metric
d
will always be used to measure distances in
\mathbb{H}
, and to define metric concepts such as "
\operatorname{diam}
", "
\operatorname{dist}
", closed balls
B(p,r):=\{q\in\mathbb{H}:d(p,q)\leq r\}
, and Hausdorff measures "
\mathcal{H}^{s}
",
s\geq 0
.

Distances and metric concepts in the parabolic plane
\mathbb{W}
are defined relative to the metric
d_{\mathrm{par}}
. With the choices of metrics
d
and
d_{\mathrm{par}}
, the parabolic plane is isometric, and also group-theoretically isomorphic, to the (Abelian) subgroup
\{(0,y,t):y,t\in\mathbb{R}\}\subset\mathbb{H}
equipped with the metric
d
. For this reason, the parabolic plane and this subgroup can be safely identified, and the notation "
\mathbb{W}
" will also be used for
\mathbb{W}=\{(0,y,t):y,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
. In particular, points in
\mathbb{W}
are denoted "
(y,t)
" if
\mathbb{W}
refers to the parabolic plane, and "
(0,y,t)
" if
\mathbb{W}
refers to the
yt
-plane inside
\mathbb{H}
.

Remark 1.10
.

If the reader has not seen a corona decomposition before, start by thinking that
\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
, and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{D}
. In this special situation, there is only one "approximating" intrinsic bilipschitz graph
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathcal{D}}
, and (
1.8
) implies that
f(w)\in\Gamma
for all
w\in\mathbb{W}
. This can be seen by taking a sequence of rectangles in
\mathcal{D}
converging to
w
. In general, the Carleson packing conditions in (
C1
) and (
C3
) imply the following for Lebesgue almost every
w\in\mathbb{W}
: there exists a tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
which contains an infinite "branch" of dyadic rectangles converging to
w
. It follows that
f(w)\in\Gamma_{j}
by (
1.8
). Consequently, the set

\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\{w\in\mathbb{W}:f(w)\notin\Gamma_{j}\}=f^{-1}\Big(f(\mathbb{W})\,\setminus\,\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\Gamma_{j}\Big)

has
\mathcal{H}^{3}
measure zero. Since
\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{f(\mathbb{W})}\ll f_{\sharp}\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{\mathbb{W}}
by the bilipschitz property of
f
, this implies that
f(\mathbb{W})
is countably rectifiable by intrinsic bilipschitz graphs.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

Section
3
contains a number of preliminary result on "dyadic" lines and "corner points" in
\mathbb{W}
, and horizontal lines in
\mathbb{H}
. Section
4
focuses on the approximability of Rickman rugs by vertical planes: the main result is Corollary
4.20
, which implies that Rickman rugs
f(\mathbb{W})
are approximable by vertical planes in
\mathbb{H}
in a weak, but quantitative, sense (the "weak geometric lemma"). The proof is based on the notion of
ruler coefficients
, which measure the linear approximability of
f
along individual horizontal lines. If the ruler coefficients are sufficiently small – and they often are by Proposition
4.4
– then
f(\mathbb{W})
is also approximable by vertical planes, see Corollary
4.25
.

Section
5
verifies that Rickman rugs have
big vertical projections
. The proof is elementary if the rug is assumed to be "
\epsilon
-flat" to begin with, see Proposition
5.4
. But the results in Section
4
verify that Rickman rugs are
\epsilon
-flat "at almost all scales and locations", and these observations combined give the result.

Section
6
constructs the partition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
, and the trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, which appear in the definition of the corona decomposition. This part of the proof of Theorem
1.3
is not difficult, and follows the ideas of David and Semmes
[
15
]
closely. The main novelty is that, unlike in
[
15
]
, the information from Section
5
on big vertical projections is employed to prove the Carleson packing estimate (
C3
) for the top rectangles
Q(\mathcal{T}_{j})
,
j\geq 1
.

The most involved, and novel, part of the paper starts in Section
7
, where we begin the construction of the well-approximating intrinsic bilipschitz graphs
\Gamma_{j}
required by (
C2
) (or (
1.8
)) of the corona decomposition. Fix a tree
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{j}
. The following two facts will be available by this point: (a) for
Q\in\mathcal{T}
, the image
f(Q)
is
\epsilon
-approximable by a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
, and (b) the planes
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
corresponding to various rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{T}
form an angle
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W})\leq\eta
with some fixed vertical plane
\mathbb{W}
, which may be assumed to be
\mathbb{W}=\{(0,y,t):y,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
with no loss of generality. If the reader is familiar with the corona decomposition from
[
15
]
, these are verbatim the same conditions required, there, to construct well-approximating Lipschitz graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If the aim of the current paper was only to prove a corona decomposition with intrinsic Lipschitz (not bilipschitz) graphs, one could probably adapt the argument of
[
15
]
.

The catch is that the conditions (a)-(b) alone are – quite likely – not strong enough to produce an intrinsic
bi
lipschitz graph. There are no recorded counterexamples, but quite likely one can construct an intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{H}
with the following three properties for any
\epsilon,\eta>0
. First,
\Gamma
is
\epsilon
-approximable by vertical planes at all scales and locations. Second, all the the best-approximating vertical planes
\mathbb{V}
satisfy
\angle(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W})\leq\eta
. Third,
\Gamma
is not a Rickman rug. The first two conditions do not seem to be powerful enough to prevent a phenomenon of "crossing characteristics". So, in addition to the properties (a)-(b), we need to keep in mind that the Rickman rug, which we are trying to approximate by an intrinsic bilipschitz graph
\Gamma
, is a bilipschitz image of
\mathbb{W}
. Once this information is married with (a) and (b), it is possible to secure a crucial separation property for the characteristics of
\Gamma
, which guarantees that
\Gamma
is an intrinsic bilipschitz graph over
\mathbb{W}
. The separation property is stated in
(\tau_{2})
of Proposition
7.3
.

2.
Acknowledgements

I’m grateful to Katrin Fässler for extensive discussions during the early stages of this project; these were invaluable in bringing some rigour to the first sketches of the corona decomposition. Also, Katrin helped in proving multiple lemmas Sections
3
and
4
. I am also grateful to Sebastiano Nicolussi Golo and Michele Villa for many discussions during the project; in particular, Sebastiano taught me the idea behind Lemma
3.12
.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Intrinsic Lipschitz functions and graphs

For more information on intrinsic Lipschitz functions and graphs, see
[
24
,
37
]
. The
1
-codimensional vertical subgroups
of
\mathbb{H}
are the
yt
-plane
\mathbb{W}=\{(0,y,t):y,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
, and any rotation of
\mathbb{W}
around the
t
-axis. To every
1
-codimensional vertical subgroup, associate a
complementary horizontal subgroup
, which, as a subset or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, is the orthogonal complement of the vertical subgroup. The complementary horizontal subgroup of the
yt
-plane is, therefore, the
x
-axis
\mathbb{L}=\{(x,0,0):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

Let
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L}
be a pair of complementary subgroups, as above, and let
\phi\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a map. The
intrinsic graph of
\phi
is the set
\Gamma(\phi):=\{w\cdot\phi(w):w\in\mathbb{W}\}\subset\mathbb{H}
. The intrinsic graph
\Gamma(\phi)
can be parametrised by the
intrinsic graph map

\Phi(w):=w\cdot\phi(w)
. If a set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{H}
has a parametrisation
\Gamma=\Phi(\mathbb{W})=\Gamma(\phi)
as the intrinsic graph of a function
\phi\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, we say that
\Gamma
is an
intrinsic graph over
\mathbb{W}
.

Intrinsic graphs over a vertical subgroup
\mathbb{W}
can be characterised as those subsets of
\mathbb{H}
for which the
vertical projection

\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{W}
is a bijection. The vertical projection
\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}
, here, is defined by
\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}(p):=w
, where
p=w\cdot l
is the unique splitting of
p
as a group product of elements
w\in\mathbb{W}
and
l\in\mathbb{L}
. This splitting also gives rise to the
horizontal projection

\Pi_{\mathbb{L}}(p):=l
. When
\mathbb{W}
is the
yt
-plane, one may compute that
\Pi:=\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}
and
\Pi_{\mathbb{L}}
have the explicit expressions

\Pi(x,y,t):=(0,y,t+\tfrac{1}{2}xy)\quad\text{and}\quad\Pi_{\mathbb{L}}(x,y,t)=(x,0,0),\qquad(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H}.

Let
L>0
. An intrinsic graph
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{H}
over
\mathbb{W}
is an
intrinsic
L
-Lipschitz graph over
\mathbb{W}
if
\Gamma
satisfies the cone condition

(3.1)

[p\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L})]\cap\Gamma=\{p\},\qquad p\in\Gamma,\,0<\alpha<\tfrac{1}{L}.

Here
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L})=\{p\in\mathbb{H}:\|\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}(p)\|\leq\alpha\|\Pi_{\mathbb{L}}(p)\|\}
, so for instance
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L})=\mathbb{L}
. It is easy to check that cone condition (
3.1
) can be equivalently written as

(3.2)

\|\Pi_{\mathbb{L}}(q^{-1}\cdot p)\|\leq L\|\Pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot p)\|,\qquad p,q\in\Gamma.

If
\Gamma=\Gamma(\phi)
is an intrinsic
L
-Lipschitz graph over
\mathbb{W}
, we say that
\phi
is an intrinsic
L
-Lipschitz function.

As we already defined after the statement of Theorem
1.3
, an
intrinsic bilipschitz graph over
\mathbb{W}
is an intrinsic Lipschitz graph over
\mathbb{W}
which is bilipschitz equivalent to
\mathbb{W}
. It is worth emphasising that the intrinsic graph map
\Phi\colon\mathbb{W}\to\Gamma(\phi)
is
almost never
a bilipschitz map. For example, if
\phi(0,y,t)\equiv(c,0,0)\neq 0
, then
\Phi(0,y,t)=(0,y,t)\cdot(c,0,0)
is a
right translation
by a non-zero vector, and right translations are not bilipschitz maps in
\mathbb{H}
. For example,
\Phi
here maps every horizontal line on
\mathbb{W}
to a non-horizontal line on
\Phi(\mathbb{W})
(which in this example is a vertical plane parallel to
\mathbb{W}
).

3.2.
Lemmas on dyadic lines and corners

We already introduced the dyadic parabolic rectangles "
\mathcal{D}
" in Section
1.2
, but we continue with a few additional pieces of notation. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
fixed, we write
\mathcal{D}_{n}:=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:\ell(Q)=2^{-n}\}
for the
dyadic parabolic rectangles of generation
n
.
Every parabolic rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
is partitioned by
8
elements of
\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
, and these are known as the
children of
Q
, denoted
\mathbf{ch}(Q)
. The rectangle
Q
is then the
parent
of its children. Every rectangle
Q=I\times J\in\mathcal{D}
has four
corners
, namely
\mathcal{C}(Q)=\partial I\times\partial J
. The set of corners of all parabolic rectangles of generation
n
will be denoted
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{D}_{n})
.

We will need a fairly tedious lemma, below, on the dyadic structure of the corner points
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{D}_{n})
. For this purpose, let
\mathcal{L}
be the set of all horizontal lines in
\mathbb{W}
, and let
\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\{\mathbb{R}\times\{k\cdot 2^{-n}\}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be the
dyadic horizontal lines of generation
n
.

For purposes of "ordering" the lines in
\mathcal{L}
, it will often be convenient to identify them with their second coordinates. Let

\pi_{1}(y,t):=y\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{2}(y,t):=t,\qquad(y,t)\in\mathbb{W},

be the coordinate projections
\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}
. If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
, then
\pi_{2}(\ell)
is a singleton, and we identify
\ell\cong t
, where
\pi_{2}(\ell)=\{t\}
. With this in mind, we will, for example, write
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}
if
\pi_{2}(\ell_{j})=\{t_{j}\}
with
t_{1}>t_{2}
. Also, for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
with
\pi_{2}(\ell)=\{r\}
, and for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the notation
\ell+t
will mean the line
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
with
\pi_{2}(\ell^{\prime})=\{r+t\}
.

Remark 3.3
.

In the next lemma, we will use the following simple observation. If
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
, then the horizontal edges of
Q
lie on consecutive lines in
\mathcal{L}_{2n}
. The horizontal edges of the
8
rectangles in
\mathbf{ch}(Q)\subset\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
span altogether
5
horizontal lines
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ell_{4}<\ell_{4}<\ell_{5}
, which satisfy
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\in\mathcal{L}_{2n}
,
\ell_{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{2n+1}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{2n}
, and
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\in\mathcal{L}_{2n+2}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{2n+1}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and let
w\in\ell\cap\mathcal{C}(Q)
for some
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
. Let
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}
be the parent of
Q
in
\mathcal{D}
.

(a)

If
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, then either

–

both
\ell+2^{-n}
and
\ell-3\cdot 2^{-n}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
, or

–

both
\ell-2^{-n}
and
\ell+3\cdot 2^{-n}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

(b)

If
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
, then both
\ell+2^{-n+1}
and
\ell-2^{-n+1}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

(c)

If
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
, then
\ell
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

(d)

If
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
and
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, then both
\ell+2^{-n}
and
\ell-2^{-n}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

(e)

If
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, then
\ell
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

Proof.

We first prove (a), (b), and (c), which have in common the assumption
n=2m\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. This implies
\lceil n/2\rceil=m
and
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{m-1}
. The rectangles in
\mathbf{ch}(\hat{Q})
have dimensions
2^{-m}\times 4^{-m}
. Let
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ldots<\ell_{5}
be the five horizontal lines which are spanned by
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{ch}(\hat{Q}))
. Thus, the lines
\ell_{j+1}=\ell_{j}+4^{-m}
, and
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}
are spanned by
\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})
. Since
w\in\ell\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{ch}(\hat{Q}))
, we have
\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{5}\}
.

To prove (a), assume that
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. We claim that
\ell\in\{\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\}
. Indeed, since
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{m-1}
, we have
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\in\mathcal{L}_{2m-2}
and
\ell_{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{2m-1}
. Since we assume that
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}=\mathcal{L}_{2m-1}
, we infer that
\ell\notin\{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},\ell_{5}\}
, and hence
\ell\in\{\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\}
. If
\ell=\ell_{2}
, then
\ell-2^{-n}=\ell-4^{-m}
and
\ell+3\cdot 2^{-n}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
. If
\ell=\ell_{4}
, then
\ell+2^{-n}
and
\ell-3\cdot 2^{-n}
contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

To prove (b), assume that
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
. As before,
\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{5}\}
, and since
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\in\mathcal{L}_{2m-2}=\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
, we have
\ell\in\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ell_{4}\}
. But
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\notin\mathcal{L}_{2m-1}=\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, so in fact
\ell=\ell_{3}
. Therefore, the lines
\ell\pm 2\cdot 4^{-m}=2^{-n+1}
contain the horizontal edges of
\hat{Q}
. To prove (c), note that the assumption
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-2}=\mathcal{L}_{2m-2}
forces
\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\}
, which is what we wanted.

Let us then prove (d) and (e), where we assume that
n=2m+1
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence
\lceil n/2\rceil=m+1
, and
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{m}
. This time the children of
\hat{Q}
have dimensions
2^{-m-1}\times 4^{-m-1}
. With the previous notation, we still have
\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{5}\}
. To prove (b), assume that
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Then, we claim that
\ell=\ell_{3}
. Indeed, now
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\in\mathcal{L}_{2m}
, so
\ell\notin\{\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\}
by the assumption
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-1}=\mathcal{L}_{2m}
. Moreover,
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\notin\mathcal{L}_{2m+1}=\mathcal{L}_{n}
, so
\ell\notin\{\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\}
. Hence
\ell=\ell_{3}
, and it follows that the horizontal edges of
\hat{Q}
are contained on
\ell\pm 2\cdot 4^{-m-1}=\ell\pm 2^{-n}
.

To prove (d), assume finally that
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}=\mathcal{L}_{2m}
. Since
\ell_{1},\ell_{5}
are the only lines in
\mathcal{L}_{2m}\cap\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{5}\}
, we infer that
\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{5}\}
, as claimed. ∎

We will use the lemma only via the following corollary:

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ell_{3}<\ell_{4}<\ell_{5}
be consecutive lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
such that
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
and hence
\ell_{1},\ell_{3},\ell_{5}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Assume that one of the lines
\ell_{j}
contains the corner of a rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
. Then, the dyadic parent
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}
has a corner on
\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{4}
.

Proof.

We may assume that the corner "
w
" of
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
is contained on
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{3}
(the cases
w\in\ell_{4}\cup\ell_{5}
are symmetric to
w\in\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
). We then consider the following cases:

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
w\in\ell_{1}
. Since
\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, we are in case (a) of Lemma
3.4
. We infer that either
\ell_{1}+2^{-n}=\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{1}+3\cdot 2^{-n}=\ell_{4}
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
w\in\ell_{2}
. Since
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, we are in case (b) or (c) of Lemma
3.4
, depending on whether
\ell_{2}\notin\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
or
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-2}
. In both cases, either
\ell_{2}
or
\ell_{2}+2^{-n+1}=\ell_{4}
contains an edge, hence a corner, of
\hat{Q}
.

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and
w\in\ell_{3}
. Since
\ell_{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, we are in case (a) of Lemma
3.4
. Consequently, either
\ell_{3}+2^{-n}=\ell_{4}
or
\ell_{3}-2^{-n}=\ell_{2}
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, and
w\in\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Then we are in case (d) of Lemma
3.4
, and we infer that
\ell_{1}+2^{-n}=\ell_{2}
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
and
w\in\ell_{2}
. Then we are in case (e) of Lemma
3.4
, and
\ell_{2}
contains an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

•

Assume
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
, and
w\in\ell_{3}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Then we are in case (d) of Lemma
3.4
, and
\ell_{3}\pm 2^{-n}=\{\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\}
both contain an edge of
\hat{Q}
.

We have covered all the cases, so the proof is complete. ∎

3.3.
Lemmas on horizontal lines

We start with an elementary lemma on horizontal lines, which quantifies the fact that if two horizontal
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}
are "close" to each other, then the coefficients in their parametrisations do not differ very much.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}
be horizontal lines which are not contained in any translate of the
xt
-plane. Then
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
can be written as

L_{j}=\{(a_{j}y+b_{j},y,\tfrac{1}{2}b_{j}y+c_{j}):y\in\mathbb{R}\},\qquad j\in\{1,2\},

for a unique triple
a_{j},b_{j},c_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that

|a_{1}|\leq\Sigma
for some
\Sigma>0
. Assume, moreover, that there exist two points
p,q\in\mathbb{H}
with
d(p,q)=r

such that
\operatorname{dist}(p,L_{j})\leq\epsilon r
and
\operatorname{dist}(q,L_{j})\leq\epsilon r
for some
\epsilon\in(0,\tfrac{1}{64(1+\Sigma)})
. Then,
|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
,

|a_{1}-a_{2}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)^{2}\epsilon,\quad|b_{1}-b_{2}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)\,r\epsilon,\quad\text{and}\quad|c_{1}-c_{2}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)(r\epsilon)^{2}.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
r=1
, since dilating the lines
L_{1},L_{2}
does not change the slopes
a_{j}
, and changes the parameters
b_{j}
and
c_{j}
by multiplicative factors
r
and
r^{2}
, respectively. After left translation (which notably does not affect the hypothesis
|a_{1}|\leq\Sigma
), we may also arrange that
p=0
. We use the following well-known fact: if
p_{1},p_{2}\in L
are points on a common horizontal line, then
|\pi(p_{1})-\pi(p_{2})|=d(p_{1},p_{2})
, where
\pi(z,t):=z
is the
1
-Lipschitz coordinate projection to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The first priority will be to show
|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
. To this end, let
p_{j},q_{j}\in L_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, be the closest points of
p,q
on
L_{j}
, so
\|p_{j}\|\leq\epsilon
and
d(q,q_{j})\leq\epsilon
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Then,

|\pi(p)-\pi(q)|\geq|\pi(p_{1})-\pi(q_{1})|-|\pi(p)-\pi(p_{1})|-|\pi(q)-\pi(q_{1})|=d(p_{1},q_{1})-2\epsilon\geq d(p,q)-4\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Further,

|\pi(p)-\pi(p_{j})|\leq\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad|\pi(q)-\pi(q_{j})|\leq\epsilon,\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.

Since
\epsilon\leq\tfrac{1}{4}
, it follows that

(3.7)

|\pi(p_{2})-\pi(q_{2})|\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad\max\{|\pi(p_{1})-\pi(p_{2})|,|\pi(q_{1})-\pi(q_{2})|\}\leq 2\epsilon.

Note that the point
p
no longer appears in (
3.7
). To use (
3.7
) to infer the estimate
|a_{2}|\leq 2|a_{1}|
, we need to write the points
\pi(p_{j}),\pi(q_{j})\in\pi(L_{j})
in coordinates:

\pi(p_{j})=:(a_{j}x_{j}+b_{j},x_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\pi(q_{j}):=(a_{j}y_{j}+b_{j},y_{j}),\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.

Then, two applications of the second estimate in (
3.7
) implies

\displaystyle 4\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq|[(a_{2}x_{2}+b_{2})-(a_{1}x_{1}+b_{1})]-[(a_{2}y_{2}+b_{2})-(a_{1}y_{1}+b_{1})]|

\displaystyle=|(a_{2}-a_{1})x_{2}-a_{1}(x_{1}-x_{2})-(a_{2}-a_{1})y_{2}+a_{1}(y_{1}-y_{2})|

\displaystyle\geq|a_{2}-a_{1}||x_{2}-y_{2}|-|a_{1}||(x_{1}-x_{2})+(y_{1}-y_{2})|

(3.8)

\displaystyle\geq|a_{2}-a_{1}||x_{2}-y_{2}|-4\epsilon\Sigma.

So, if – contrary to what we claim – it were the case that
|a_{2}|\geq 2\Sigma\geq 2|a_{1}|
, then

(3.9)

4\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|a_{2}||x_{2}-y_{2}|-2\epsilon\Sigma.

On the other hand, by the first estimate in (
3.7
), we have

(3.10)

\max\{|x_{2}-y_{2}|,|a_{2}||x_{2}-y_{2}|\}\geq\tfrac{1}{4}.

If
|x_{2}-y_{2}|\geq\tfrac{1}{4}
, then (
3.9
) and the assumption
64\epsilon(1+\Sigma)<1
yields
4\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\Sigma-2\epsilon\Sigma\geq\tfrac{1}{8}\Sigma
, a contradiction. And if
|a_{2}||x_{2}-y_{2}|\geq\tfrac{1}{4}
instead, then (
3.9
) yields
4\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{8}-2\epsilon\Sigma\geq\tfrac{1}{16}
, another contradiction. So, we conclude that
|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
, as claimed.

The estimates for
|a_{2}-a_{1}|
and
|b_{2}-b_{1}|
are now straightforward. First, a combination of (
3.8
), (
3.10
), and
|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
, yields

|a_{2}-a_{1}|\leq\frac{4\epsilon(1+\Sigma)}{|x_{2}-y_{2}|}\leq 4\epsilon(1+\Sigma)\cdot(4+8\Sigma)\leq 64(1+\Sigma)^{2}\epsilon.

Next, using (
3.7
),
|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
, and
|x_{j}|\leq|\pi(p_{j})|\leq|\pi(p)|+\epsilon=\epsilon
by
p=0
, we have

(3.11)

|b_{j}|\leq|a_{j}x_{j}+b_{j}|+|a_{j}x_{j}|\leq\epsilon+2\Sigma\epsilon\leq 2(1+\Sigma)\epsilon,\qquad j\in\{1,2\},

so in particular
|b_{2}-b_{1}|\leq 4(1+\Sigma)\epsilon
. It remains to check that
|c_{2}-c_{1}|\leq\epsilon^{2}
. This is based on
\max\{\|p_{1}\|,\|p_{2}\|\}\leq\epsilon
. Since

p_{j}=(a_{j}x_{j}+b_{j},x_{j},\tfrac{1}{2}b_{j}x_{j}+c_{j}),\qquad j\in\{1,2\},

it follows that
|\tfrac{1}{2}b_{j}x_{j}+c_{j}|\lesssim\epsilon^{2}
and

|x_{j}|\leq\epsilon
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Combining this with (
3.11
), we find
|c_{j}|\lesssim\epsilon^{2}+|b_{j}x_{j}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)\epsilon^{2}
, and finally
|c_{1}-c_{2}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)\epsilon^{2}
, as claimed. ∎

The next lemma states that two (nearly) equidistant horizontal line segments are roughly contained in a small neighbourhood of a single vertical plane. For
E\subset\mathbb{H}
and
\delta>0
, we use the notation
N(E,\delta):=\{p\in\mathbb{H}:\operatorname{dist}(p,E)\leq\delta\}
for the closed
\delta
-neighbourhood of
E
.

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists an absolute constant
A\geq 10
such that the following holds. Let
0<\epsilon<1
,
H\geq 1
, let
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}
be horizontal lines, let
p\in L_{1}
, and let
r>0
. Assume that

(3.13)

B(p,AH^{3}\epsilon^{-2}r)\cap L_{2}\subset N(L_{1},Hr).

Then, there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}(L_{1})
(the vertical plane spanned by
L_{1}
) with the property

(3.14)

B(p,Hr)\cap L_{2}\subset N(L,\epsilon r).

Proof.

We first reduce to the case
H=1
. Indeed, assume that this case is already known. Then, let
H\geq 1
be arbitrary, and write
r^{\prime}:=Hr
and
\epsilon^{\prime}:=\epsilon/H
. With this notation, the hypothesis (
3.13
) can be rephrased as
\operatorname{dist}(q,L_{1})\leq Hr=r^{\prime}
for all
q\in B(p,AH^{3}\epsilon^{-2}r)\cap L_{2}=B(p,A(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-2}r^{\prime})
. Consequently, the version of the lemma with "
H=1
" implies the existence of
L\subset\mathbb{V}(L_{1})
with the property
\operatorname{dist}(q,L)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}r^{\prime}=\epsilon r
for all
q\in B(p,r^{\prime})\cap L_{2}=B(p,Hr)\cap L_{2}
, as desired. So, we may assume
H=1
. Translating, dilating, and rotating, we may also assume with no loss of generality that
B(p,r)=B(0,1)
,
L_{1}=\{(x,0,0):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
, and
B(0,1)\cap L_{2}\neq\emptyset
(otherwise there is nothing to prove).

Write
\hat{L}_{j}:=\pi(L_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, where
\pi(x,y,t):=(x,y)
is the
1
-Lipschitz coordinate projection to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Write
R:=A\epsilon^{-2}\geq 10
, where the value of the constant
A\geq 10
will become apparent during the proof. Then, it follows from (
3.13
) that
\hat{L}_{2}\cap B_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(0,R)
is contained in the
1
-neighbourhood of
\hat{L}_{1}=\{(x,0):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Since
R\geq 10
, and
\hat{L}_{2}\cap B_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(0,1)\neq\emptyset
, it follows that
\hat{L}_{2}
can be written as a graph over the
x
-axis
\hat{L}_{1}
:

\hat{L}_{2}=\{(x,ax+b):x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Moreover,
|ax+b|\leq 1
for all
|x|\leq R
. In particular,
|b|\leq 1
, and
|a|\leq 2/R
. Since
L_{2}
is a horizontal line with projection
\hat{L}_{2}
, there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(3.15)

L_{2}=\{(x,ax+b,c-\tfrac{1}{2}bx):x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

We will eventually show that (
3.14
) holds with
L:=\{(x,0,c):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
. For this purpose, we first claim that
|c|\lesssim 1
and
|b|\lesssim 1/R
, improving on the previous "trivial" bound
|b|\leq 1
. Since
L_{2}\cap B(0,1)\neq\emptyset
, there exists a point
(x,ax+b,c-\tfrac{1}{2}bx)\in B(0,1)
, whence
|x|\leq 1
, and
|c|\lesssim 1+|bx|\lesssim 1
.

From the estimates
|a|\lesssim R^{-1}
,
|b|\leq 1
and
|c|\lesssim 1
, we can now deduce that, for a suitable small constant
\theta>0
, we have

|x|\leq\theta R\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(x,ax+b,c-\tfrac{1}{2}bx)\in L_{2}\cap B(0,R).

Motivated by this, we define the two points

q_{-}:=(-\theta R,-\theta aR+b,c+\tfrac{\theta}{2}bR)\quad\text{and}\quad q_{+}:=(\theta R,\theta aR+b,c-\tfrac{\theta}{2}bR),

both lying in
L_{2}\cap B(0,R)
. By the assumption (
3.13
) that
\operatorname{dist}(q_{\pm},L_{1})\leq 1
, we now find
\epsilon_{-},\epsilon_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
d((\pm\theta R+\epsilon_{\pm},0,0),q_{\pm})\leq 1
. Expanding what this means,

(3.16)

1\geq d((\theta R+\epsilon_{+},0,0),q_{+})\gtrsim|\epsilon_{+}|+\sqrt{|c-\theta bR-\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}aR^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\theta aR\epsilon_{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{+}b|}

and

(3.17)

1\geq d((-\theta R+\epsilon_{-},0,0),q_{-})\gtrsim|\epsilon_{-}|+\sqrt{|c+\theta bR-\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}aR^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\theta aR\epsilon_{-}-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{-}b|}.

It follows immediately that
|\epsilon_{\pm}|\leq 1
. Consequently (using also the previous bounds
|a|\lesssim R^{-1}
and
|b|\leq 1
), we find that

|\tfrac{1}{2}\theta aR\epsilon_{+}\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\pm}b|\lesssim 1.

Combining this information with the bounds from (
3.16
)-(
3.17
), we deduce that also

2\theta|bR|\leq|\theta bR+c-\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}aR^{2}|+|\theta bR-c+\tfrac{1}{2}\theta^{2}aR^{2}|\lesssim 1,

and hence finally
|b|\lesssim R^{-1}
, as claimed.

Now, we complete the proof of (
3.14
) for
L=\{(x,0,c):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Note that

d((x,0,c),(x,ax+b,c-\tfrac{1}{2}bx))\sim|ax+b|+\sqrt{|bx+\tfrac{1}{2}ax^{2}|},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

For
|x|\lesssim 1
, the bound
\max\{|a|,|b|\}\lesssim 1/R
shows that
d((x,0,c),(x,ax+b,c-\tfrac{1}{2}bx))\lesssim 1/\sqrt{R}
. In particular, if the constant "
A
" in
R=A\epsilon^{-2}
was chosen large enough, and recalling the parametrisation (
3.15
) of the line
L_{2}
, we see that
d(q,L)\leq\epsilon
for all
q\in L_{2}\cap B(0,1)
, as claimed. ∎

3.4.
Quasi-isometries on
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{W}

Quasi-isometric embeddings are maps between metric spaces which behave like bilipschitz maps down to a fixed (typically small) resolution "
\epsilon
". These maps arise in the present paper, roughly speaking, as compositions
\pi_{\mathbb{V}}\circ f
, where
f\colon B(w,r)\to\mathbb{H}
is a bilipschitz map defined a parabolic ball
B(w,r)\subset\mathbb{W}
, and
\pi_{\mathbb{V}}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{V}
is the "closest point projection" to some vertical plane
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{V}_{x,r}\subset\mathbb{H}
, which approximates
f(B(w,r))
up to error "
\epsilon r
".

Definition 3.18

(
(M,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric embedding)
.

A map
f\colon(X,d)\to(Y,d^{\prime})
between two metric spaces is an
(M,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric embedding
(abbreviated
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE) if

M^{-1}d(x,y)-\epsilon\leq d^{\prime}(f(x),f(y))\leq Md(x,y)+\epsilon,\qquad x,y\in X.

Remark 3.19
.

Note that if
f\colon(X,d)\to(Y,d^{\prime})
is an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, and
x,x^{\prime}\in X
are two points with
d(x,x^{\prime})\geq 2M\epsilon
, then
d^{\prime}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\geq\epsilon
. In particular,
f(x)\neq f(x^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.20

(Orderly metric)
.

A metric
d
on
\mathbb{R}
is
orderly
if
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)\to(\mathbb{R},d)
is continuous, and
\max\{d(x,y),d(y,z)\}\leq d(x,z)
for all triples
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}
with
x\leq y\leq z
.

We will mainly use the definition in the form of the following corollary: if
[a,b]\subset[c,d]
, then
d(a,b)\leq d(c,d)
. This follows by chaining
d(a,b)\leq d(c,b)\leq d(c,d)
. The only metrics on
\mathbb{R}
we will actually encounter are the Euclidean one, and the square root metric
\|x-y\|:=\sqrt{|x-y|}
. Both are evidently orderly.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}
, let
d
be a metric on
\mathbb{R}
, let
f\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
be a map, and let
\delta>0
. We say that
f
is
\delta
-monotone relative to
d
if either
f(a)<f(b)
for all
a,b\in E
with
a<b
and
d(a,b)>\delta
, or then
f(a)>f(b)
for all
a,b\in E
with
a<b
and
d(a,b)>\delta
.

The definition can be posed for arbitrary metrics, but most likely is not very useful if
d
is not orderly. We start with a simple lemma on
(M,\epsilon)
-QIEs on the real line.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval (either open or closed), and let
f\colon(I,d)\to(\mathbb{R},d)
be an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, where
d
is an orderly metric. Then
f
is
2\epsilon M
-monotone relative to
d
, and
f([a,b])
is
\epsilon
-dense in
[f(a),f(b)]
for all
a,b\in I
.

The last statement means that
\operatorname{dist}(y,f([a,b]))\leq\epsilon
for all
y\in[f(a),f(b)]
.

Proof of Lemma
3.22
.

We start by proving the second assertion, which is then used to establish the first. Fix
a,b\in I
with
a<b
, and assume without loss of generality that
f(a)<f(b)
(if
f(a)=f(b)
, there is nothing to prove). Pick
f(a)<y<f(b)
, and fix
\epsilon^{\prime}>\epsilon
. Consider the points

c:=\sup\{x\in[a,b]:f(x)\leq y\}\quad\text{and}\quad d:=\inf\{x\in[c,b]:f(x)\geq y\}.

Clearly
c\leq d
by definition, and in fact
c=d
; namely, if
c<d
, any point
x\in(c,d)
would satisfy
f(x)<y
by the definition of
d
, and then
c\geq x>c
by the definition of
c
.

Next, by definitions of
c
and
d
, there are points
x_{1}\leq c=d\leq x_{2}
with
|x_{1}-x_{2}|
arbitrarily small such
that
f(x_{1})\leq y\leq f(x_{2})
. Since
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)\to(\mathbb{R},d)
is continuous, such points can also be
chosen with
d(x_{1},x_{2})<(\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon)/M
. Then,
using the assumption that
d
is orderly, we find that

d(f(x_{1}),y)\leq d(f(x_{1}),f(x_{2}))\leq Md(x_{1},x_{2})+\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}
, as desired.

We then proceed to the first statement of the lemma. Let
a,b,c,d\in I
with
a<b
,
c<d
, and
\min\{d(a,b),d(c,d)\}>2\epsilon M
. Therefore
\min\{d(a,b)/M-\epsilon,d(c,d)/M-\epsilon\}>\epsilon^{\prime}
for some
\epsilon^{\prime}>\epsilon
. By Remark
3.19
, we have
f(a)\neq f(b)
and
f(c)\neq f(d)
, and we may assume that
f(a)<f(b)
. Our task is to prove that also
f(c)<f(d)
. The argument splits into two cases: either
[c,d]\subset[a,b]
or
[c,d]\not\subset[a,b]
.

Assume first that
[c,d]\subset[a,b]
. We first check that
f(d)>f(a)
. If this were not the case, then
f(a)\in[f(d),f(b)]
. Since
f([d,b])
is
\epsilon
-dense in
[f(d),f(b)]
, this implies that there exists a point
x\in[d,b]
with
d(f(x),f(a))\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
. However,
d(a,x)\geq d(c,d)
, hence

(3.23)

d(f(x),f(a))\geq\frac{d(a,x)}{M}-\epsilon\geq\frac{d(c,d)}{M}-\epsilon>\epsilon^{\prime},

a contradiction. So, we already know that
f(d)>f(a)
. Assume, then, to reach another contradiction, that
f(d)<f(c)
. Consequently
f(d)\in[f(a),f(c)]
, and by the reasoning above, there exists a point
x\in[a,c]
with
d(f(x),f(d))\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
. However,
d(x,d)\geq d(c,d)
, which leads to
d(f(x),f(d))>\epsilon^{\prime}
, a contradiction. This settles the case
[c,d]\subset[a,b]
.

Consider then the case
[c,d]\not\subset[a,b]
. Then, either
c<a
, or
d>b
. These sub-cases are similar, and we only treat
c<a
. We first claim that, in this case,
f(c)<f(b)
. If this failed, then
f(b)\in[f(a),f(c)]
, and we may find a point
x\in[c,a]
with
d(f(x),f(b))\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
. However,
d(x,b)\geq d(a,b)
, so
d(f(x),f(b))>\epsilon^{\prime}
by the computation in (
3.23
), a contradiction. Finally, we check that
f(d)>f(c)
by a fourth iteration of the same argument: if this fails, then
f(c)\in[f(d),f(b)]
. Consequently, there exists a point
x\in[d,b]
or
x\in[b,d]
such that
d(f(x),f(c))\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
. If here
d\leq b
, then
d(c,x)\geq d(c,d)
, leading to the familiar contradiction. And if
b\leq d
, then
d(c,x)\geq d(a,b)
, leading once more to the same contradiction. The proof of the lemma is complete. ∎

Corollary 3.24
.

Let
f\colon I:=[a,c]\to\mathbb{R}
be an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, and assume that
b\in(a+2M\epsilon,c-2M\epsilon)
. Then
f(b)\in(f(a),f(c))
or
f(b)\in(f(c),f(a))
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.19
, we have
f(a)\neq f(b)\neq f(c)
. If
f(a)<f(b)
, then Lemma
3.22
implies that also
f(b)<f(c)
, and hence
f(b)\in(f(a),f(c))
. Otherwise,
f(b)\in(f(c),f(a))
by the same argument. ∎

We next prove a similar statement concerning
horizontal

(M,\epsilon)
-QIEs
defined on rectangles in the parabolic plane

\mathbb{W}
; keep in mind that parabolic balls, in particular, are rectangles, since
d_{\mathrm{par}}
was defined as the
L^{\infty}
-metric.

Definition 3.25

(Horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE)
.

Let
Q:=I\times J\subset\mathbb{W}
be a rectangle. A map
F\colon Q\to\mathbb{W}
is called a
horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE
if
F
is an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE in the sense of Definition
3.18
, and moreover, for every
t\in J
there exists a horizontal line
\ell=\ell_{t}\subset\mathbb{W}
such that
F(I\times\{t\})\subset\ell
.

Every horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE
F\colon I\times J\to\mathbb{R}

induces
the following map
f\colon J\to\mathbb{R}
:

f(t):=\pi_{2}(F(y,t)),

where
\pi_{2}
is the projection to the second coordinate, and
y\in J
is arbitrary (the value of
y\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
does not depend on
y\in J
by the horizontality assumption). The projection
\pi_{2}
is a
1
-Lipschitz map
(\mathbb{W},d_{\mathrm{par}})\to(\mathbb{R},\|\cdot\|)
, where
\|x-y\|:=\sqrt{|x-y|}
is the square root metric. From this, it follows quite easily that
f
is also a QIE
(I,\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R},\|\cdot\|)
if the interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is long enough relative to
\sqrt{|J|}
. Details follow:

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\epsilon>0
,
M\geq 1
, and let
I,J\subset\mathbb{R}
be intervals with

|I|>2M^{2}\sqrt{|J|}+4M\epsilon.

If
F\colon Q:=I\times J\to\mathbb{W}
is a horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, then the induced map
f\colon(J,\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R},\|\cdot\|)
is an
(M,2\epsilon)
-QIE.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, assume that
I=[-a,a]
for some
a>M^{2}\sqrt{|J|}+2M\epsilon
. In particular,
0\in I
. Then, fix
t_{1},t_{2}\in J
, and note that
f(t_{j})=\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{j}))
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Using that
F
is an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, we infer that

\|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})\|\leq d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(0,t_{1})),F((0,t_{2}))\leq M\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|+\epsilon,

since the parabolic distance of
(0,t_{1}),(0,t_{2})
is simply
\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|
. Proving the lower bound is a little trickier. The plan is the following. Let
\pi_{1}(y,t):=y
. Consider the auxiliary point

v:=(\pi_{1}(F(0,t_{2})),\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{1})))\in\mathbb{W},

which is vertically aligned with
F(0,t_{2})
, but has the same
\pi_{2}
-projection as
F(0,t_{1})
. Then,

\|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})\|=\|\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{1}))-\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{2})\|=\|\pi_{2}(v)-\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{2}))\|=d_{\mathrm{par}}(v,F(0,t_{2})).

To complete the proof from here, we will for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>\epsilon
find a point
y_{0}\in I
such that

(3.27)

d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(y_{0},t_{1}),v)<\epsilon^{\prime}.

With such a point
y_{0}\in I
, in hand, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})\|

\displaystyle\geq d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(y_{0},t_{1}),F(0,t_{2}))-\epsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d_{\mathrm{par}}((y_{0},t_{1}),(0,t_{2}))}{M}-2\epsilon^{\prime}\geq\frac{\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|}{M}-2\epsilon^{\prime},

and the proof is finished by letting
\epsilon^{\prime}\searrow\epsilon
.

To find the special point
y_{0}\in I
, consider the map
F_{j}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
F_{j}(y):=\pi_{1}(F(y,t_{j}))
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Since
F
maps
I\times\{t_{j}\}
inside a fixed horizontal line, and
\pi_{1}
restricted to this line is an isometry, we see that
F_{1}
is an
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE
I\to\mathbb{R}
(with Euclidean metrics on
I
and
\mathbb{R}
). Moreover, since
-a<-2M\epsilon
and
a>2M\epsilon
, we infer from Corollary
3.24
that
F_{1}(0)\in[F_{1}(-a),F_{1}(a)]
or
F_{1}(0)\in[F_{1}(a),F_{1}(-a)]
. Assume with no loss of generality that
F_{1}(-a)<F_{1}(0)<F_{1}(a)
. Then

(3.28)

F_{1}(a)-F_{1}(0)>\frac{a}{M}-\epsilon>M\sqrt{|J|}+\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad F_{1}(0)-F_{1}(-a)>Mr+\epsilon.

On the other hand, since
t_{1},t_{2}\in J
, we note that

(3.29)

|F_{1}(0)-F_{2}(0)|\leq d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(0,t_{1}),F(0,t_{2}))\leq M\sqrt{|J|}+\epsilon,

so we infer from a combination of (
3.28
)-(
3.29
) that
F_{2}(0)\in[F_{1}(a_{1}),F_{1}(c_{1})]
. Finally, by Lemma
3.22
, the image
F_{1}(I)
is
\epsilon
-dense in
[F_{1}(-a),F_{1}(a)]
, so there exists a point
y_{0}\in I
such that

|\pi_{1}(F(y_{0},t_{1}))-\pi_{1}(F(0,t_{2}))|=|F_{1}(y)-F_{2}(0)|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

Since
\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{1}))=\pi_{2}(F(0,t_{1}))=\pi_{2}(v)
, we find that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(y_{0},t_{1}),v)

\displaystyle\leq|\pi_{1}(F(y,t_{1}))-\pi_{1}(v)|=|\pi_{1}(F(y_{0},t_{1}))-\pi_{1}(F(0,t_{2}))|\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

as desired in (
3.27
). ∎

As the first corollary of the previous lemma, we infer that both
\pi_{j}
-projections,
j\in\{1,2\}
, of horizontal QIEs are QIEs on the real line (relative to two different metrics):

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
F\colon Q=I\times J\to\mathbb{W}
be a horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, where
|I|>2M^{2}\sqrt{|J|}+4M\epsilon
. Then, for
(y_{0},t_{0})\in Q
fixed, the maps

y\mapsto\pi_{1}(F(y,t_{0}))\quad\text{and}\quad t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t))

are
(M,2\epsilon)
-QIEs on
(I,|\cdot|)\to(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)
and
(J,\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R},\|\cdot\|)
, respectively.

Proof.

The claim about
y\mapsto\pi_{1}(F(y,t_{0}))
follows immediately from the horizontality of
F
, and the fact that
\pi_{1}
restricted to any horizontal line is an isometry. The claim about
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t))
is a restatement of Lemma
3.26
. ∎

As a second corollary or Lemma
3.26
, we infer a
vertical monotonicity
property of horizontal QIEs
F\colon Q\to\mathbb{W}
. Let us first define this property:

Definition 3.31
.

Let
Q=I\times J\to\mathbb{W}
be a rectangle. A map
F\colon Q\to\mathbb{W}
is called
vertically
\delta
-monotone
if
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
is
\delta
-monotone on
J
relative to the square root metric for every
y\in I
, and the signature of monotonicity is independent of
y\in I
.

The final condition means that either
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
is
\delta
-increasing on
J
for every
y\in I
, or then
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
is
\delta
-decreasing on
J
for every
y\in I
.

Corollary 3.32
.

Let
F\colon Q:=I\times J\to\mathbb{W}
be a horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE, where
|I|>2M^{2}\sqrt{|J|}+4M\epsilon
. Then
F
is vertically
2M\epsilon
-monotone.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.26
, we know that the induced map
f\colon(J,\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R},\|\cdot\|)
is an
(M,2\epsilon)
-QIE. Since the square root metric is orderly, Lemma
3.22
applies to
f
and shows that
f
is
4\epsilon M
-monotone. Assume, for example, that
f
is
4M\epsilon
-increasing, that is,

t_{1},t_{2}\in J\text{ and }\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|>4M\epsilon\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f(t_{2})>f(t_{1}).

By the horizontality assumption on
F
, this implies that

(y_{1},t_{1}),(y_{2},t_{2})\in Q\text{ and }\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|>4M\epsilon\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\pi_{2}(F(y_{2},t_{2}))>\pi_{2}(F(y_{1},t_{1})).

In particular,
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
is
4\epsilon M
-increasing for all
y\in I
. ∎

For future reference, we formalise the notion of
signature of monotonicity
:

Definition 3.33

(Signature)
.

Let
F\colon Q=I\times J\to\mathbb{W}
be a vertically
\delta
-monotone map. The
signature
of
F
is "
+
" if the maps
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y,t))
are
\delta
-increasing on
J
for all
y\in I
. Otherwise the signature of
F
is "
-
".

We close this section with another simple property of QIEs on the parabolic plane:

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
Q=I\times J\subset\mathbb{W}
be a rectangle, and let
F\colon Q\to\mathbb{W}
be a horizontal
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE. Assume that
w_{1}=(y_{1},t_{1}),w_{2}=(y_{2},t_{2})\in Q
with

(3.35)

|y_{2}-y_{1}|>\max\{8M\epsilon,4M^{2}\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|\}.

Then,

|\pi_{1}(F(w_{2}))-\pi_{1}(F(w_{1}))|\geq\frac{|y_{2}-y_{1}|}{2M}.

Proof.

Consider the point
w_{2}^{\prime}:=(y_{2},t_{1})\in Q
. Then,

|\pi_{1}(F(w_{2}^{\prime}))-\pi_{1}(F(w_{2}))|\leq d_{\mathrm{par}}(F(w_{2}^{\prime}),F(w_{2}))\leq M\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|+\epsilon.

Further, using the
(M,\epsilon)
-QIE property of
\pi_{1}\circ F
on
\{(y,t_{1}):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
, we have

|\pi_{1}(F(w_{2}^{\prime}))-\pi_{1}(F(w_{1}))|\geq\frac{|y_{2}-y_{1}|}{M}-\epsilon.

Therefore, by the triangle inequality,

|\pi_{1}(F(w_{2}))-\pi_{1}(F(w_{1}))|\geq\frac{|y_{2}-y_{1}|}{M}-M\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|-2\epsilon\geq\frac{|y_{2}-y_{1}|}{2M},

using the main hypothesis (
3.35
) (or rather its corollary
|y_{2}-y_{1}|>4M\epsilon+2M^{2}\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|
) in the final inequality. ∎

4.
Ruler coefficients and
\beta
-numbers

The aim of this section is to show that Rickman rugs
f(\mathbb{W})\subset\mathbb{H}
are
\epsilon
-approximable by vertical planes at "almost all scales and locations". The formal statement is Corollary
4.20
. The idea is that
f
restricted to every horizontal line defines a
1
-regular curve in
\mathbb{H}
, and such curves are well-known to admit the correct type of
\epsilon
-approximations by horizontal lines. It follows that for "most"
w\in\mathbb{W}
and
r>0
, the image
f(B(w,r))
is a surface in
\mathbb{H}
which is approximately ruled by horizontal lines. Relying on the assumption that
f
is bilipschitz, and using Lemma
3.12
, this will imply that
f(B(w,r/C))
lies
\epsilon
-close to a vertical plane for a suitable constant
C=C(\epsilon,M)\geq 1
, see Proposition
4.11
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{W}
,
r>0
, and let
f\colon B(w,r)\to\mathbb{H}
be a map. Define
\rho_{f}(B(w,r))
to be the infimum of numbers
\rho>0
with the following property: for every horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{H}
such that

(4.2)

f(\ell\cap B(w,r))\subset N(L,\rho r).

Here
N(E,\delta):=\{p\in\mathbb{H}:\operatorname{dist}(p,E)\leq\delta\}
refers to the closed
\delta
-neighbourhood of
E
.

Remark 4.3
.

Of course, the requirement (
4.2
) may be restricted to those lines
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
with
\ell\cap B(w,r)\neq\emptyset
, and in particular
\pi_{2}(\ell)\cap\pi_{2}(B(w,r))\neq\emptyset
.

The discs
B(w,r)
,
x\in\mathbb{W}
,
r>0
, with
\rho_{f}(B(w,r))\geq\epsilon
satisfy a
Carleson packing condition
, for any
\epsilon>0
fixed. We only formulate a dyadic (and more easily applicable) variant of this result. To this end, recall that if
Q\in\mathcal{D}
and
C>0
, then
CQ:=B(c_{Q},C\ell(Q))
, where
c_{Q}\in Q
is the centre of
Q
, and
\ell(Q)=\operatorname{diam}(Q)
is the side-length of
Q
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
. For any
\epsilon\in(0,1]
and
C\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(C,\epsilon,M)
such that the following holds:

(4.5)

\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}(C,\epsilon)}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}\ell(Q)^{3}\leq C^{\prime}\ell(Q_{0})^{3},\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D},

where
\mathcal{B}(C,\epsilon)=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:\rho_{f}(CQ)\geq\epsilon\}
.

Proof.

The map
f
restricted to an arbitrary horizontal line
\ell_{t}=\{(y,t):y\in\mathbb{R}\}\subset\mathbb{W}
defines a
1
-regular curve
\gamma_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{H}
by
\gamma_{t}(y):=f(y,t)
. The regularity constant of
\gamma_{t}
only depends on
M
. To apply this fact, we record that every
1
-regular curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{H}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition known as the (strong)
geometric lemma
:

(4.6)

\mathop{\sum_{I\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}}}}_{I\subset I_{0}}\beta_{\gamma}(CI)^{4}|I|\leq C^{\prime}|I_{0}|,\qquad I_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}},

where
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the standard dyadic grid on
\mathbb{R}
, and
\beta_{\gamma}(CI)
is the
horizontal
\beta
-number

\beta_{\gamma}(J):=\inf\{\epsilon>0:\gamma(J)\subset N(L,\epsilon|J|)\text{ for some horizontal line }L\subset\mathbb{H}\}.

The geometric lemma (
4.6
) for
1
-regular curves in
\mathbb{H}
is literally contained in
[
9
, Proposition 3.1]
, but as the authors of
[
9
]
also say, it is a consequence of the main theorem in
[
27
]
. The constant
C^{\prime}\geq 1
in (
4.6
) only depends on
C
and the
1
-regularity constant of
\gamma
.

To pass from the condition (
4.6
) concerning individual curves
\gamma_{t}=f(\cdot,t)
to the Carleson packing condition (
4.6
) concerning the ruler coefficients, let us abbreviate

\beta_{t}(CQ):=\beta_{\gamma_{t}}(\pi_{1}(CQ)),\qquad Q\in\mathcal{D},\,t\in\pi_{2}(CQ),

and also define
\beta_{t}(CQ)=0
if
t\notin\pi_{2}(CQ)
. Then, we define the
L^{4}
-averaged coefficient

\alpha_{f}(CQ):=\left(\frac{1}{|\pi_{2}(CQ)|}\int_{\pi_{2}(CQ)}\beta_{t}(CQ)^{4}\,dt\right)^{1/4},\qquad Q\in\mathcal{D}.

It follows rather immediately from (
4.6
), and the relation
|\pi_{2}(CQ)|\sim_{C}\ell(Q)^{2}
, that the
4^{th}
powers of the coefficients
\alpha_{f}(CQ)
satisfy a Carleson packing condition:

\displaystyle\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}\alpha_{f}(CQ)^{4}\ell(Q)^{3}

\displaystyle\sim_{C}\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}\ell(Q)\int_{\pi_{2}(CQ)}\beta_{t}(CQ)^{4}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\pi_{2}(CQ_{0})}\Bigg(\mathop{\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}}_{t\in\pi_{2}(CQ)}\beta_{\gamma_{t}}(\pi_{1}(CQ))^{4}\ell(Q)\Bigg)dt

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}\int_{\pi_{2}(CQ_{0})}\Bigg(\mathop{\sum_{I\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}}}}_{I\subset\pi_{1}(Q_{0})}\beta_{\gamma_{t}}(CI)^{4}|I|\Bigg)\,dt

(4.7)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{form124}}}{{\lesssim_{C,M}}}\int_{\pi_{2}(CQ_{0})}\ell(Q_{0})\,dt\sim\ell(Q_{0})^{3},\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(Q).

In the only non-trivial inequality, we used that if
t\in\pi_{2}(CQ_{0})
is fixed, then any interval
CI
with
I\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{R}}
, has
\lesssim_{C}1
representations as
CI=\pi_{1}(CQ)
with
Q\in\mathcal{D}
and
t\in\pi_{2}(CQ)
.

From the "geometric lemma for the
\alpha_{f}
-coefficients" established in (
4.7
), and Chebyshev’s inequality, it follows for all
\epsilon>0
that

\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(C,\epsilon)}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}\ell(Q)^{3}\lesssim_{C,M}\epsilon^{-4}\cdot\ell(Q_{0})^{3},\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D},

where
\widetilde{B}(C,\epsilon):=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:\alpha_{f}(CQ)\geq\epsilon\}
. Thus, to complete the proof of (
4.5
), hence the proposition, it remains to prove an inclusion of the form

\mathcal{B}(C,\epsilon)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(2C,\tilde{\epsilon}),\qquad C\geq 1,\epsilon>0,

where
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
is only allowed to depend on
C,\epsilon,M
. In other words, we need to show that if
Q\in\mathcal{B}(C,\epsilon)
, hence
\rho_{f}(CQ)\geq\epsilon
, then
\alpha_{f}(CQ)\geq\tilde{\epsilon}
, hence
Q\in\widetilde{B}(2C,\tilde{\epsilon})
. In fact, it is more convenient to prove an equivalent statement: for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
, depending only on
C,\epsilon,M
such that

(4.8)

\alpha_{f}(2CQ)<\tilde{\epsilon}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\rho_{f}(CQ)<\epsilon.

In fact, we claim that (
4.8
) holds with
\tilde{\epsilon}:=(\epsilon/10)\cdot(\delta/C)^{1/2}
, where
\delta:=\epsilon/(2M)
. Assume that
\alpha_{f}(2CQ)<\tilde{\epsilon}
with this choice of
\tilde{\epsilon}
, and fix a horizontal line
\ell=\ell_{t_{0}}\subset\mathbb{W}
with
t_{0}\in\pi_{2}(CQ)
. We claim that there exists

t\in[t_{0}-(\delta\ell(Q))^{2},t_{0}+(\delta\ell(Q))^{2}]\subset\pi_{2}(2CQ)\text{ such that }\beta_{t}(2CQ)<\tfrac{\epsilon}{4}.

Indeed, if this fails, then we note that
|\pi_{2}(2CQ)|=(2C\ell(Q))^{2}
, and hence

\alpha_{f}(2CQ)\geq\left(\frac{1}{|\pi_{2}(2CQ)|}\int_{t_{0}-\delta^{2}\ell(Q)^{2}}^{t_{0}+\delta^{2}\ell(Q)^{2}}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)^{4}\,dt\right)^{1/4}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{4C^{2}}\right)^{1/4}>\tilde{\epsilon},

a contradiction.

To complete the proof, fix
t\in[t_{0}-(\delta\ell(Q))^{2},t_{0}+(\delta\ell(Q))^{2}]
such that
\beta_{t}(CQ)<\tfrac{\epsilon}{4}
. This means that there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{H}
with the property

f(\ell_{t}\cap CQ)=\gamma_{t}(\pi_{1}(2CQ))\subset N(L,2C\tfrac{\epsilon}{4}\cdot\ell(Q))=N(L,\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot C\ell(Q)).

where
\ell_{t}=\{(y,t):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Since
\ell_{t_{0}}\subset N_{\mathrm{par}}(\ell_{t},\delta\ell(Q))\subset N_{\mathrm{par}}(\ell_{t},\tfrac{\epsilon}{2M}\cdot C\ell(Q))
, we may finally infer from the
M
-bilipschitz property of
f
that

f(\ell_{t_{0}}\cap CQ)\subset N(L,\epsilon\cdot C\ell(Q)).

Since
\ell=\ell_{t_{0}}
was an arbitrary horizontal line intersecting
CQ
, we have proven that
\rho_{f}(CQ)<\epsilon
, as claimed in (
4.8
). The proof of Proposition
4.4
is complete. ∎

Proposition
4.4
shows that dyadic rectangles with non-negligible ruler coefficient are not too common. We next record a key geometric property of
f
-images of discs with small ruler coefficients:
\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))<\delta
forces
\beta_{f}(B(w,r))<\epsilon
, where
\beta_{f}(B(w,r))
is the
strong vertical
\beta
-number
:

Definition 4.9

(Strong vertical
\beta
-number)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be a map, and let
w\in\mathbb{W}
,
r>0
. For a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
, define
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};B(w,r))
as the infimum of the numbers
\epsilon>0
with the following property: for all horizontal lines
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, there corresponds a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}
such that
f(\ell\cap B(w,r))\subset N(L,\epsilon r)
. The
strong vertical
\beta
-number
is then defined as

\beta_{f}(B(w,r)):=\inf_{\mathbb{V}}\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};B(w,r)),

where the infimum runs over all vertical planes.

Remark 4.10
.

It is sometimes convenient (but not really necessary) to talk about the
best approximating plane
for
f
at
B(w,r)
. This will refer to a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
such that the infimum in the definition of
\beta_{f}(B(w,r))
is attained. It follows from an easy compactness argument that best approximating planes exist (in verifying this, recall that
N(L,\epsilon r)=\{p\in\mathbb{H}:\operatorname{dist}(p,L)\leq\epsilon r\}
is a closed neighbourhood in this paper).

Second remark: if
\beta_{f}(B(w,r))<\epsilon
, then there exists a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
with
f(B(w,r))\subset N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon r)
. This means that the strong vertical
\beta
-number is at least as large as the commonly used
vertical
\beta
-number
, see
[
7
, Definition 3.3]
. Hence the word "strong".

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
. Then, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, there exist
C=C(\epsilon,M)\geq 10
and
\delta=\delta(C,\epsilon,M)>0
such that the following holds:

\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))<\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\beta_{f}(B(w,r))<\epsilon.

Remark 4.12
.

The proof below shows that
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};B(w,r))<\epsilon
for
any
vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
which arises as follows: take an arbitrary horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
with
\ell\cap B(w,r)\neq\emptyset
. Let
L\subset\mathbb{H}
be some horizontal line with

f(\ell\cap B(w,Cr))\subset N(L,C\delta r),

(which exists by
\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))<\delta
) and let
\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{V}(L)
be the unique vertical plane containing
L
. In the proof below, we will, for the sake of concreteness, pick the line
\ell
which passes through
w
, but any line intersecting
B(w,r)
would work equally well, modulo changing
C
and
\delta
by absolute constant factors.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We choose

(4.13)

C:=C_{0}M^{4}\epsilon^{-2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta:=\epsilon/(C_{0}CM)

for some absolute constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, whose value will be determined during the proof.

Let
\ell_{1}\subset\mathbb{W}
be the horizontal line containing
w
. By definition of
\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))<\delta
, there exists a horizontal line
L_{1}\subset\mathbb{H}
such that

f(\ell_{1}\cap B(w,Cr))\subset N(L_{1},C\delta r)\subset N(\mathbb{V},C\delta r),

where we define
\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{V}(L_{1})
to be the vertical plane spanned by
L_{1}
.

Fix an arbitrary horizontal line
\ell_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}
. The task is to find a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}
such that
f(\ell_{2}\cap B(w,r))\subset N(L,\epsilon r)
. In doing so, we may and will assume that
\ell_{2}\cap B(w,r)\neq\emptyset
. Let
p_{1}\in L_{1}
be the closest point to
f(w)
. Then
d(p_{1},f(w))\leq\epsilon r\leq Mr
. Consequently,

(4.14)

f(\ell_{2}\cap B(w,r))\subset B(f(w),Mr)\subset B(p_{1},2Mr).

Now, by definition of
\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))\leq\delta
, there exists a horizontal line
L_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}
, not necessarily contained on
\mathbb{V}
, such that

f(\ell_{2}\cap B(w,Cr))\subset N(L_{2},C\delta r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{form13}}}{{\subset}}N(L_{2},\epsilon r/2).

Combining this assumption with (
4.14
), we observe that, in particular,

f(\ell_{2}\cap B(w,r))\subset B(p_{1},2Mr)\cap N(L_{2},\epsilon r/2).

So, the proof of the proposition will be complete once we manage to show that there exists a further horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}
such that

(4.15)

B(p_{1},2Mr)\cap L_{2}\subset N(L,\epsilon r/2).

To prove (
4.15
), we apply Lemma
3.12
with the horizontal lines
L_{1},L_{2}
, with
p:=p_{1}\in L_{1}
, and radius
r>0
, with "
\epsilon/2
" in place of "
\epsilon
", and with constant
H:=2M
. In other words, we need to verify that

(4.16)

B(p,AH^{3}(\epsilon/2)^{-2}r)\cap L_{2}\subset N(L_{1},Hr).

Once this has been accomplished, Lemma
3.12
yields a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}(L_{1})
with the property

B(p,2Mr)\cap L_{2}=B(p,Hr)\cap L_{2}\subset N(L,\epsilon r/2),

as required in (
4.15
).

It remains to verify (
4.16
). Abbreviate
R:=AH^{3}(\epsilon/2)^{-2}r\geq r
. We first claim that it suffices to verify the inclusion

(4.17)

B(p,R)\cap L_{2}\subset N(f(\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)),2C\delta r),

which informally says that
L_{2}
is well-approximated by
f(\ell_{2})
. By the definition of
L_{2}
, we know that
f(\ell_{2})
is well-approximated by
L_{2}
, and (
4.17
) is the converse statement: it holds, as we will see, because
f|_{\ell_{2}}
is bilipschitz.

To infer (
4.16
) from (
4.17
), fix
q_{2}\in B(p,R)\cap L_{2}
, and using (
4.17
), pick

v_{2}\in\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)\text{ such that }q_{2}\in B(f(v_{2}),2C\delta r).

Then, noting that
\operatorname{dist}_{\mathrm{par}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\leq r
(since
\ell_{1}
passes through
w
, and
\ell_{2}
intersects
B(w,r)
, as we assumed), we may find a point
v_{1}\in\ell_{1}\cap B(w,Cr)
with the property
d_{\mathrm{par}}(v_{1},v_{2})\leq r
. In particular,
d(f(v_{1}),f(v_{2}))\leq Mr
. In addition, since
v_{1}\in\ell_{1}\cap B(w,Cr)
, the definition of
\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))\leq\delta
yields a point
q_{1}\in L_{1}
with
d(f(v_{1}),q_{1})\leq C\delta r
. Finally, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(q_{2},L_{1})

\displaystyle\leq d(q_{2},q_{1})

\displaystyle\leq d(q_{1},f(v_{1}))+d(f(v_{1}),f(v_{2}))+d(f(v_{2}),q_{2})

\displaystyle\leq Mr+3C\delta r\leq 2Mr,

as claimed in (
4.17
) (here we took
\delta=\delta(C,M)>0
sufficiently small).

Finally, we turn our attention to (
4.17
). We will use Lemma
3.22
. To do so, we identify both
(\ell_{2},d_{\mathrm{par}})
and
(L_{2},d)
with
\mathbb{R}
, and we first use the restriction of
f
to
I:=\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)
to define a QIE
i\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
. Simply, for
v\in I
, let
i(v)
be the closest point on
L_{2}
to
f(v)
. Since
f
is
M
-bilipschitz, and
d(f(v),i(v))\leq C\delta r
for all
v\in I
, we see that
i\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
is an
(M,C\delta r)
-QIE.

Identify
J:=B(p,R)\cap L_{2}
to a sub-interval of
\mathbb{R}
(of length
\leq 2R
). Then, pick an arbitrary point
w_{2}\in\ell_{2}\cap B(w,r)
, and write
p_{2}:=i(w_{2})\in L_{2}
. Noting that
d(p,p_{2})\leq 2C\delta r+d(f(w),f(w_{2}))\leq 2Mr
, it follows that

(4.18)

J\subset[i(w_{2})-R-2Mr,i(w_{2})+R+2Mr]=:[i(w_{2})-C^{\prime}r,i(w_{2})+C^{\prime}r],

where
C^{\prime}:=AH^{3}(\epsilon/2)^{-2}+2M
. Recall here that
H=4M
, so
C^{\prime}\lesssim_{\epsilon,M}1
.

We first claim that there exist two points
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in I=\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)
such that
J\subset[i(a^{\prime}),i(b^{\prime})]
. To see this, write
a:=w_{2}
, and pick an arbitrary point
b\in\ell_{2}\cap B(w,5r)
with
|a-b|=r>2MC\delta r
. Since
i
is an
(M,C\delta r)
-QIE, it follows from Remark
3.19
that
i(a)\neq i(b)
. We assume with no loss of generality that

(4.19)

a<b\quad\text{and}\quad i(a)<i(b).

According to Lemma
3.22
, the map
i
is
2MC\delta r
-monotone, and since
a<b-2MC\delta r
, we infer from (
4.19
) that
i
is
2MC\delta r
-increasing. In particular, if
a^{\prime}\in I
is a further point with
a^{\prime}<a-2MC\delta r
, then
i(a^{\prime})<i(a)
. In particular, this holds if we pick
a^{\prime}\in I=\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)
with
a^{\prime}<a
at distance
\geq Cr/10
from
a
. Then

i(a)-i(a^{\prime})\geq\frac{a-a^{\prime}}{M}-C\delta r\geq\frac{Cr}{10M}-C\delta r\geq\frac{Cr}{20M},

recalling from (
4.13
) that
\delta<1/(100M)
. Similarly, we may find a point
b^{\prime}\in I
with
b^{\prime}>b
at distance
\geq Cr/10
from
b
with the property

i(b^{\prime})-i(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{form11}}}{{>}}i(b^{\prime})-i(b)>Cr/(20M).

Combining these estimates with (
4.18
), and noting that
C>20MC^{\prime}
by (
4.13
), we find that
J\subset[i(a^{\prime}),i(b^{\prime})]
, as required. Next, we infer from Lemma
3.22
that
i(I)
is
(C\delta r)
-dense in
[i(a^{\prime}),i(b^{\prime})]
, in particular in
J
. Unwrapping the definitions, this means that for every
q\in J=B(p,R)\cap L_{2}
, we may find a point
v\in I=\ell_{2}\cap B(w,\tfrac{C}{2}r)
with the property
d(q,i(v))\leq C\delta r
. Moreover,
i(v)
was the closest point of
f(v)
to
L_{2}
, hence
d(i(v),f(v))\leq C\delta r
. It follows that
d(q,f(v))\leq 2C\delta r
, and the proof of (
4.17
) is complete. ∎

As a corollary of Propositions
4.4
and
4.11
, we record that bilipschitz images of
\mathbb{W}
inside
\mathbb{H}
satisfy the
weak geometric lemma
for the strong vertical
\beta
-numbers (hence also for the standard vertical
\beta
-numbers):

Corollary 4.20

(Weak geometric lemma)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
. Then, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1]
and
C\geq 1
, we have

(4.21)

\mathop{\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}}_{\beta_{f}(CQ)\geq\epsilon}\ell(Q)^{3}\lesssim_{C,\epsilon,M}\ell(Q_{0})^{3},\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

Proof.

Fix
C\geq 1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
. Choose
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(C,\epsilon,M)\geq C
and
\delta=\delta(C,\epsilon,M)>0
such that

\rho_{f}(B(w,C^{\prime}r))<\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\beta_{f}(B(w,Cr))<\epsilon,\qquad w\in\mathbb{W},\,r>0.

This is possible by Proposition
4.11
. Thus,

\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:\beta_{f}(CQ)\geq\epsilon\}\subset\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:\rho_{f}(C^{\prime}Q)\geq\delta\}=\mathcal{B}(C^{\prime},\delta).

Now (
4.21
) follows from Proposition
4.4
applied with constants
C^{\prime},\delta
. ∎

4.1.
Ruled and vertical rectangles

We have already learned from Proposition
4.11
that small ruler coefficients imply small vertical
\beta
-numbers. In this section, we formalise the connection a little further, and add the notion of "slope" to the soup.

We start with some notation on parametrising horizontal lines and vertical planes. Let
L\subset\mathbb{H}
be a horizontal line which is not contained on any translate of the
xt
-plane. Then,
L
can be written as

L=\{(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c):y\in\mathbb{R}\}

for some unique triple
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
. The absolute value of the coefficient "
a
" will be called the
slope of
L
, denoted
\angle(L)
, or
\angle(L,\mathbb{W})
if the relation to the
yt
-plane needs emphasising. If
L
is contained in the
xt
-plane, it cannot be expressed as above, and we declare
\angle(L):=\infty
. If
\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{V}(L)
is the vertical plane spanned by
L
, then the
slope of
\mathbb{V}
equals, by definition, the slope of
L
, and we write
\angle(\mathbb{V}(L)):=\angle(L)
.

Definition 4.22

(Ruled rectangle)
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, let
C\geq 10
and
\delta,\Sigma>0
be constants, and let

f\colon B(c_{Q},C\ell(Q))\to\mathbb{H}
be a map. The
rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is called
(\delta,C)
-ruled
if

\rho_{f}(CQ)<\delta/C
, where
CQ=B(c_{Q},C\ell(Q))
as usual. This means, by definition, that for every horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{H}
such that

(4.23)

f(\ell\cap CQ)\subset N(L,\delta\ell(Q)).

The rectangle
Q
is called
(\delta,C,\Sigma)
-ruled if it is

(\delta,C)
-ruled, and there exists a horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
such that
\ell\cap Q\neq\emptyset
, and such that

some
horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{H}
satisfying (
4.23
) has
slope
\angle(L)\leq\Sigma
.

Definition 4.24

(Vertical rectangle)
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, let
H\geq 1
,
\epsilon\in(0,1]
,

\Sigma>0
be constants and let
f:HQ\to\mathbb{H}
be a map.
The rectangle
Q
is called
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical

if there exists a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}\subset\mathbb{H}

with
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q})\leq\Sigma
such that
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V}_{Q};HQ)<\epsilon/H
.

To avoid over-indexing, we omit reference to
f
in the notation for ruled and vertical rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{D}
. We next record a straightforward corollary of Proposition
4.11
, which says that ruled rectangles are vertical rectangles. The only additional information is that the slopes of the ruling control the slopes of well-approximating vertical planes.

Corollary 4.25

(to Proposition
4.11
)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}

be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
, and let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
H\geq 1
, and
\Sigma>0
.
Then, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\epsilon,H,M)\geq 10
and
\delta_{0}=\delta(\epsilon,C_{0},H,M)>0
such that the following holds
for all
C\geq C_{0}
and
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
. If
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is

(\delta,CH,\Sigma)
-ruled, then it is
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical.

Proof.

Recall that
HQ:=B(c_{Q},H\ell(Q))
. We apply Proposition
4.11
with centre
w=c_{Q}
and radius
r=H\ell(Q)
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}:=\epsilon/H
in place of
\epsilon
. The conclusion is that if

\rho_{f}(B(w,Cr))=\rho_{f}(CHQ)<\delta

for sufficiently large
C\geq 1
and sufficiently small
\delta>0
,
depending on
\epsilon,H,M
, then there exists a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
with
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V}_{Q};HQ)<\epsilon/H
. Moreover, as explained in Remark
4.12
, the plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
can be chosen as
\mathbb{V}_{Q}=\mathbb{V}(L)
for any horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{H}
with the following property: there is a horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
with
\ell\cap HQ=\ell\cap B(w,r)\neq\emptyset
