such that

(4.26)

f(\ell\cap CHQ)\subset N(L,\delta CH\ell(Q)).

Now we use that
Q
is
(\delta,CH,\Sigma)
-ruled: there exists a horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
with
\ell\cap Q\neq\emptyset
such that (
4.26
) holds, and such
that
\angle(L)\leq\Sigma
. Hence,
\angle(\mathbb{V}(L))\leq\Sigma
, and the choice

\mathbb{V}_{Q}:=\mathbb{V}(L)
satisfies all the requirements needed to show
that
Q
is
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical.
∎

4.2.
The local quasi-isometry
F_{Q}

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
,
and fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
H\geq 1
, and
\Sigma>0
. Then, if
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, Definition
4.24
implies that
f|_{HQ}
composed with
closest-point-projection to
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
is an
(M,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE.
However, it will be convenient to "normalise" this QIE in such a way that its target is always
\mathbb{W}
, instead of the changing vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
.
For this purpose, we will next construct a horizontal QIE

F_{Q}:HQ\to\mathbb{W}
of the form
F_{Q}=\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}\circ\iota_{Q}
, where
\iota_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{V}_{Q}
is the composition of
f|_{HQ}
with (almost) the
closest-point-projection to
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
,
and
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}:\mathbb{V}_{Q}\to\mathbb{W}
is a bilipschitz map defined in (
4.31
) below.

We first define the map
\iota:=\iota_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{V}_{Q}
, where
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical
rectangle (in fact, we may define
\iota_{Q}
on
\mathbb{W}
, but it is useless outside
HQ
). Recall from Definition
4.24
that for every horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, there exists a horizontal line
L=L_{Q,\ell}\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
with the property

(4.27)

f(\ell\cap HQ)\subset N(L,\epsilon\ell(Q)).

Since
the
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q})\leq\Sigma
, there exist
constants
a_{Q},b_{Q},c_{Q,\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a_{Q}|\leq\Sigma
such that

(4.28)

L_{Q,\ell}=\{(a_{Q}y+b_{Q},y,\tfrac{1}{2}b_{Q}y+c_{Q,\ell}):\,y\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The following lemma will be used to define the map
\iota_{Q}
.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
L:=\{(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c):\,y\in\mathbb{R}\}
be a horizontal line with finite slope. For
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H}
, define the map

i_{L}(x,y,t):=(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c),

which picks the unique point on
L
with which has the same second coordinate as
(x,y,t)
. Then,

d((x,y,t),\iota_{L}(x,y,t))\leq A(1+|a|)\,\mathrm{dist}((x,y,t),L),\qquad(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H},

for some absolute constant
A\geq 1
.

Proof.

Given
p:=(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H}
, let
p^{\prime}:=(ay^{\prime},y^{\prime},\frac{1}{2}by^{\prime}+c)
be a point on the horizontal line
L
that realizes the
distance to
(x,y,t)
. Then,

d(p,i_{L}(p))\leq d(p,p^{\prime})+d(p^{\prime},i_{L}(p))\lesssim\mathrm{dist}(p,L)+(1+|a|)|y-y^{\prime}|.

Here the upper bound for the second summand is an easy consequence
of the fact that
i_{L}(p)=(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c)
and

p^{\prime}=(ay^{\prime}+b,y^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{2}by^{\prime}+c)
lie on the same horizontal line

L
, and
L
is a left translate of the line
\{(ay,y,0):\,y\in\mathbb{R}\}
by
(b,0,c)
.
∎

With the lemma above in mind, we define
\iota_{Q}|_{\ell}\colon\ell\to L\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
by setting
\iota_{Q}(w):=i_{L_{Q,\ell}}(f(w))
, that is

(4.30)

\iota_{Q}(w):=\left(a_{Q}f_{2}(w)+b_{Q},f_{2}(w),\tfrac{1}{2}b_{Q}f_{2}(w)+c_{Q,\ell}\right),\quad w\in\ell.

The constants
a_{Q},b_{Q},c_{Q,\ell}
are defined in
(
4.28
). As
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
ranges over horizontal lines,
this defines
\iota_{Q}
on
\mathbb{W}
. Since
f
is
M
-bilipschitz and
(
4.27
) holds, and
|a_{Q}|\leq\Sigma
, Lemma

4.29
implies that
\iota_{Q}
is an

(M,A(1+\Sigma)\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE on
HQ
(of course not necessarily on
\mathbb{W}
), which maps each horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
inside the horizontal line
L_{Q,\ell}\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
. Thus, identifying
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
with
\mathbb{W}
in some abstract way, one can already view
\iota_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{V}_{Q}
as a horizontal QIE in the sense of Definition
3.25
. It pays off to make the identification
\mathbb{V}_{Q}\cong\mathbb{W}
more explicit, and this is what we do next.

Let
\mathbb{V}:=\{(ay+b,y,t):\,(y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
be a vertical plane with
\angle(\mathbb{V})<\infty
. Let
\Pi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{W}
be the standard vertical projection
\Pi(x,y,t)=(y,t+\tfrac{xy}{2})
, and set

\pi_{\mathbb{V}}:=\psi_{\mathbb{V}}\circ\Pi
, where
\psi_{\mathbb{V}}(y,t):=(y,t-\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}-by)
. In other words,

(4.31)

\pi_{\mathbb{V}}(x,y,t)=\left(y,t+\tfrac{1}{2}xy-\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}-by\right),\qquad(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H}.

Lemma 4.32
.

The map
\pi_{\mathbb{V}}
maps horizontal lines in
\mathbb{V}
onto horizontal lines in

\mathbb{W}
, and is
A(1+|a|)
-bilipschitz for some absolute constant
A\geq 1
.

Proof.

We first observe that

(4.33)

\pi_{\mathbb{V}}\left(ay+b,y,c+\tfrac{1}{2}by\right)=(y,c),\quad y,c\in\mathbb{R}.

Since every horizontal line in
\mathbb{V}
is of the form

L\cdot(0,0,c)=\{(ay+b,y,c+\tfrac{1}{2}by):\,y\in\mathbb{R}\}

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
, formula (
4.33
) proves that

\pi_{\mathbb{V}}
maps horizontal lines in
\mathbb{V}
to horizontal
lines in
\mathbb{W}
. To prove the bilipschitz continuity of

\pi_{\mathbb{V}}
, let

p=\left(ay+b,y,c+\tfrac{b}{2}y\right)\quad\text{and}\quad p^{\prime}=\left(ay^{\prime}+b,y^{\prime},c^{\prime}+\tfrac{b}{2}y^{\prime}\right)

be two points in
\mathbb{V}
. It follows from (
4.33
)
that

d_{\mathrm{par}}(\pi_{\mathbb{V}}(p),\pi_{\mathbb{V}}(p^{\prime}))=d_{\mathrm{par}}((y,c),(y^{\prime},c^{\prime}))\sim|y-y^{\prime}|+\sqrt{|c-c^{\prime}|}.

On the other hand, it is easy to check that

d(p,p^{\prime})\sim(1+|a|)|y-y^{\prime}|+\sqrt{|c-c^{\prime}|}.

Hence
d(p,p^{\prime})/(1+|a|)\lesssim d_{\mathrm{par}}(\pi_{\mathbb{V}}(p),\pi_{\mathbb{V}}(p^{\prime}))\lesssim(1+|a|)d(p,p^{\prime})
, as claimed.
∎

Combined with the properties
of
\iota_{Q}
, which we stated below
Lemma
4.29
, Lemma
4.32
yields the
following corollary (and definition).

Corollary 4.34
.

Let
A\geq 1
be an absolute
constant as in Lemma
4.29
and Lemma
4.32
.
Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
,
and fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
H\geq 1
, and
\Sigma>0
.
If
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is an

(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle,
then

F_{Q}:=\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}\circ\iota_{Q}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{W}

is a horizontal
(A(1+\Sigma)M,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE on
HQ
which has the explicit expression

(4.35)

F_{Q}(w)=(f_{2}(w),c_{Q,\ell}),\qquad w\in\ell,

for any horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
. Here
c_{Q,\ell}
is the coefficient defined in (
4.28
).

So, the map
F_{Q}
may be defined on the whole plane
\mathbb{W}
, but it is only a horizontal QIE restricted to
HQ
. For this reason, we will often treat
F_{Q}
as if it only were defined on
HQ
.

Proof of Corollary
4.34
.

As we discussed below Lemma
4.29
,
with these choices of parameters, the maps
\iota_{Q}
associated
to
(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangles are

(M,A^{-1}(1+\Sigma)^{-1}\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIEs
HQ\to\mathbb{V}_{Q}

which send horizontal line segments to horizontal line segments.
Moreover, since
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}\colon\mathbb{V}_{Q}\to\mathbb{W}
is
A(1+\Sigma)
-bilipschitz by Lemma
4.32
, and sends horizontal
lines in
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
to horizontal lines in
\mathbb{W}
, the first claim follows. The formula (
4.35
) follows directly from the definitions of
\iota_{Q}
in (
4.30
), and
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}\circ\Pi
in (
4.31
):

\displaystyle F_{Q}(w)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}(\Pi[a_{Q}f_{2}(w)+b_{Q},f_{2}(w),\tfrac{1}{2}b_{Q}f_{2}(w)+c_{Q,\ell}])

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q}}(f_{2}(w),b_{Q}f_{2}(w)+c_{Q,\ell}+\tfrac{1}{2}a_{Q}f_{2}(w)^{2})

\displaystyle=(f_{2}(w),\tfrac{1}{2}b_{Q}f_{2}(w)+c_{Q,\ell}+\tfrac{1}{2}a_{Q}f_{2}(w)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}a_{Q}f_{2}(w)^{2}-b_{Q}f_{2}(w))

\displaystyle=(f_{2}(w),c_{Q,\ell}),\qquad w\in\ell\subset\mathbb{W}.

This completes the proof of the corollary – and the definition of
F_{Q}
. ∎

To end this section, we quantify how much the maps
F_{Q}
and
F_{\hat{Q}}
can
differ from each other if
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}
is the parent of
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, and both
Q,\hat{Q}
are
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
Q,\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}
be
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangles such that
\hat{Q}
is
the parent of
Q
. Assume moreover that
\epsilon<cM^{-1}(1+\Sigma)^{-1}
for a small absolute constant
c>0
. Then,

d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{Q}(w),F_{\hat{Q}}(w))\lesssim(1+\Sigma)^{\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon\ell(Q),\qquad w\in HQ.

Proof.

Write
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
, let
w\in HQ
, and let
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
be the unique horizontal line containing
w
. From the expression (
4.35
), we deduce that

F_{Q}(w)=(f_{2}(w),c_{Q,\ell})\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\hat{Q}}(w)=(f_{2}(w),c_{\hat{Q},\ell}),

so
d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{Q}(w),F_{\hat{Q}}(w))=|c_{Q,\ell}-c_{\hat{Q},\ell}|
. Let
L_{Q,\ell},L_{\hat{Q},\ell}\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
be the horizontal lines familiar from (
4.27
), with
\angle(L_{Q,\ell}),\angle(L_{\hat{Q},\ell})\leq\Sigma
, and which satisfy

f(\ell\cap HQ)\subset N(L_{Q},\epsilon\ell(Q))\quad\text{and}\quad f(\ell\cap HQ)\subset N(L_{\hat{Q}},2\epsilon\ell(Q)).

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\ell\cap Q
with

|w_{1}-w_{2}|=\ell(Q)
. Then
d(f(w_{1}),f(w_{2}))\geq M^{-1}\ell(Q)
, and

\operatorname{dist}(f(w_{j}),L_{Q})\leq\epsilon\ell(Q)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{dist}(f(w_{j}),L_{\hat{Q}})\leq 2\epsilon\ell(Q)

for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Now, since

\epsilon\ll M^{-1}(1+\Sigma)^{-1}
, Lemma
3.6
can be
applied with
r=M^{-1}\ell(Q)
and
2M\epsilon
in place of

\epsilon
. The conclusion is that

|c_{Q,\ell}-c_{\hat{Q},\ell}|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)(2M\epsilon\cdot M^{-1}\ell(Q))^{2}\sim(1+\Sigma)(\epsilon\ell(Q))^{2},

and hence

d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{Q}(w),F_{\hat{Q}}(w))\lesssim(1+\Sigma)^{1/2}\epsilon\ell(Q)
, as claimed. ∎

4.3.
Approximate quadrics

Recall that if
Q\in\mathcal{D}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, then
f(\ell\cap HQ)\subset N(L,\epsilon\ell(Q))
for a certain horizontal line
L=L_{Q,\ell}\subset\mathbb{H}
with
\angle(L)\leq\Sigma
. If we write
L=\{(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
, as usual, then the image

\mathcal{Q}_{Q,\ell}:=\Pi(L_{Q,\ell})\subset\mathbb{W}

of
L_{Q,\ell}
under the vertical projection
\Pi
is the quadric
\mathcal{Q}_{Q,\ell}=\{(y,q_{Q,\ell}(y)):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
parametrised by

q_{Q,\ell}(y):=\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c,\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

We will call
q_{Q,\ell}
the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric
. This notion will be central in the proof of Theorem
1.3
: the intrinsic bilipschitz graphs
\Gamma_{j}
appearing in (
1.8
) of the corona decomposition are constructed, roughly speaking, by gluing together
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadrics for various rectangles
Q
and lines
\ell
.

Since the graph
\mathcal{Q}_{Q,\ell}
is the
\Pi
-projection of the horizontal line
L_{Q,\ell}
which approximates
f(\ell\cap HQ)
well in
\mathbb{H}
, one expects
\mathcal{Q}_{Q,\ell}
to approximate
\Pi(f(\ell\cap HQ))
well in
\mathbb{W}
. The next two lemmas quantify this heuristic.

Lemma 4.37
.

Let
L=\{(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
be a horizontal line. Write
q_{L}(y):=\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c
and
\mathcal{Q}_{L}(y):=(y,q_{L}(y))
. Then,

\max\{d_{\mathrm{par}}(\Pi(p),\mathcal{Q}_{L}(y)),|x-\dot{q}_{L}(y)|\}\lesssim(1+|a|)\mathrm{dist}(p,L),\qquad p=(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{H}.

Proof.

Since
\Pi(p)=(y,t+\frac{xy}{2})
and the points

\Pi(p),\mathcal{Q}_{L}(y)\in\mathbb{W}
have the same
y
-coordinate, and
\dot{q}_{L}(y)=ay+b
,
we find that

(4.38)

d_{\mathrm{par}}(\Pi(p),\mathcal{Q}_{L}(y))=\left|\left(t+\tfrac{xy}{2}\right)-\left(\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c\right)\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

and

(4.39)

|x-\dot{q}_{L}(y)|=|x-(ay+b)|.

Both (
4.38
) and (
4.39
) are bounded from above by (a constant times)

d(p,(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c))=d(p,i_{L}(p))\leq A(1+|a|)\mathrm{dist}(p,L),

where we used Lemma
4.29
in the final estimate. This completes the proof. ∎

Corollary 4.40
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, let
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
be a horizontal line, and let
q:=q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric. Then,

(4.41)

|(f_{3}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{1}(w)f_{2}(w))-q(f_{2}(w))|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)^{2}\epsilon^{2}\ell(Q)^{2},\qquad w\in\ell\cap HQ,

and

(4.42)

|f_{1}(w)-\dot{q}(f_{2}(w))|\lesssim(1+\Sigma)\epsilon\ell(Q),\qquad w\in\ell\cap HQ.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
4.37
to the point
p:=f(w)=(f_{1}(w),f_{2}(w),f_{3}(w))=:(x,y,t)
for
w\in\ell\cap HQ
, which satisfies
\operatorname{dist}(p,L)\leq\epsilon\ell(Q)
by the assumption that
Q
is
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical. The horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
appearing here has slope
|a|\leq\Sigma
. Now, the left hand side of (
4.41
) is the parabolic distance squared between
\Pi(p)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{L}(y)
. Consequently, both (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) follow directly from the estimate in Lemma
4.37
. ∎

To emphasise the obvious, (
4.41
)-(
4.42
) say that the values of
q
and
\dot{q}
evaluated at
f_{2}(y,t)
– and
not
at
y
! – are essentially determined by
f(y,t)
for
(y,t)\in\ell\cap HQ
.

4.4.
Vertical trees

Recall the definition of
trees
in
\mathcal{D}
from Section
1.2
. Before introducing concrete trees, we make the following choices of constants: for given parameters
M\geq 1
(the bilipschitz constant of
f
) and
\Sigma>0

(the "maximal slope" constant), we set

(4.43)

N:=A(1+\Sigma)M\quad\text{and}\quad 0<\epsilon<cN^{-10},

where
A\geq 1
is the absolute constant from Corollary
4.34
, and
c\in(0,\tfrac{1}{100})
is another absolute constant, whose size will have to be adjusted in upcoming arguments. Corollary
4.34
verifies that if
Q
is an
(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, then
F_{Q}
is a horizontal
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE
HQ\to\mathbb{W}
.

Definition 4.44

(Vertical tree)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map
M\geq 1
,
and fix parameters
\epsilon>0
,
H\geq 1
, and
\Sigma>0
. A
tree
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
is called a

(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree
(or just a
vertical
tree
) if it consists of
(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangles.

We next verify, as a consequence of Corollary
3.32
, that the QIEs
F_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{W}
are vertically monotone on a smaller parabolic ball
BQ\subset HQ
whenever
H\gg(1+\Sigma)^{2}M^{2}B
.

Lemma 4.45
.

Let
B,M\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
.
Let
H:=A(1+\Sigma)^{2}M^{2}B
for a sufficiently large absolute constant
A\geq 1
.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be an
(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle. Then, the horizontal
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE
F_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{W}
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-monotone on
BQ
with
\epsilon^{\prime}:=2N\epsilon
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.32
, the vertical
2N\epsilon\ell(Q)
-monotonicity of
F_{Q}
on
BQ
will follow from the
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE property of
F_{Q}
on
HQ
, provided that
H\geq AN^{2}B\sim(1+\Sigma)^{2}M^{2}B
for some absolute constant
A\geq 1
. Indeed,
BQ
fits in a parabolic rectangle
I\times J
of dimensions approximately
B\ell(Q)\times(B\ell(Q))^{2}
. To conclude that
F_{Q}
is vertically
2N\epsilon\ell(Q)
-monotone on such a rectangle, Corollary
3.32
needs
F_{Q}
to be an
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE on a concentric parabolic rectangle of dimensions approximately

[N^{2}B\ell(Q)+N\epsilon\ell(Q)]\times(B\ell(Q))^{2}.

Evidently
N\epsilon\leq N^{2}B
, so this his bigger rectangle fits inside
HQ
for some
H\sim N^{2}B
. ∎

Motivated by the lemma above, we fix a parameter
B\geq 5
, and in the sequel we will always require

(4.46)

H\geq A(1+\Sigma)^{2}M^{2}B

for a sufficiently large absolute constant
A\geq 10
such that the conclusion of Lemma
4.45
holds: whenever
Q
is a rectangle in a vertical tree, then the restriction of
F_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{W}
to
BQ
is vertically
2N\epsilon\ell(Q)
-monotone. In the end, the choice
B:=5
works, but we will keep the special notation "
B
" to emphasise the "meaning of the constant
5
".

Definition 4.47

(Signature of
Q
)
.

The
signature of
Q
is the signature (recall Definition
3.33
) of the vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-monotone map
F_{Q}\colon BQ\to\mathbb{W}

defined in Corollary
4.34
.

Recall, above, that
\epsilon^{\prime}=2N\epsilon<N^{-9}
by
(
4.43
). We then arrive at the main result of this section, which states that signature within
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical trees are constant:

Proposition 4.48
.

Let
B\geq 5
, and let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
be an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree, where
H
is given by (
4.46
). Then, all the rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{T}
have the same signature.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the signatures of
Q
and
\hat{Q}
are the same for all
Q,\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}
, where
\hat{Q}
is the
\mathcal{D}
-parent of
Q
. Fix
Q\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}
, and assume for example that the signature of
\hat{Q}
, hence
F_{\hat{Q}}\colon B\hat{Q}\to\mathbb{W}
, is
+
. This means, by definition, that the maps
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t))
are
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(\hat{Q})=2\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-increasing for all
y\in\pi_{1}(B\hat{Q})
. The task is to show that the maps
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y_{0},t))
are
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-increasing for all
y\in\pi_{1}(BQ)
. For this, we will use Lemma
4.36
to compare
F_{Q}
to
F_{\hat{Q}}
.

Let us prove first the
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-increasing property of
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y,t))
. Since we already know that
F_{Q}
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-monotone on
BQ
, it suffices to "test" the signature with a parameter
y\in\pi_{1}(BQ)
of our choosing. We choose
y
so that the vertical line
\{(y,t):t\in\mathbb{R}\}
intersects
Q
. Then, we may pick
t_{1}<t_{2}
such that
(y,t_{1}),(y,t_{2})\in Q
and
d_{\mathrm{par}}((y,t_{1}),(y,t_{2}))=\ell(Q)
, or in other words
t_{2}=t_{1}+\ell(Q)^{2}
. Since certainly
(y,t_{j})\in HQ
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, Lemma
4.36
is applicable and shows that

(4.49)

d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{Q}(y,t_{j}),F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{j}))\leq A(1+\Sigma)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\epsilon\ell(Q),\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.

On the other hand, since
F_{Q}
is a horizontal
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE on
HQ
, Lemma
3.30
implies that the map
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t))
is an
(N,2\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE on
([t_{1},t_{2}],\|\cdot\|)
(the constant
H\geq AM^{2}
is certainly large enough for this purpose), and consequently

(4.50)

\|\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{2}))-\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{1}))\|\geq\frac{\ell(Q)}{N}-2\epsilon\ell(Q)\geq\frac{\ell(Q)}{2N},

recalling from (
4.43
) that
\epsilon<cN^{-10}
. Since, moreover,
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t))
is
2\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-increasing, and
\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|=\ell(Q)>2\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
, we may upgrade (
4.50
) to

\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{2}))-\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{1}))>\frac{\ell(Q)^{2}}{(2N)^{2}}.

Combining this separation property with (
4.49
), and noting that
A^{2}(1+\Sigma)\epsilon^{2}<(4N)^{-2}
by (
4.43
) shows that

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y,t_{2}))-\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y,t_{1}))

\displaystyle>\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{2}))-\pi_{2}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{1}))

\displaystyle\quad-d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{1}),F_{Q}(y,t_{1}))^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-d_{\mathrm{par}}(F_{\hat{Q}}(y,t_{2}),F_{Q}(y,t_{2}))^{2}

(4.51)

\displaystyle>\frac{\ell(Q)^{2}}{(2N)^{2}}-2\cdot\frac{\ell(Q)^{2}}{(4N)^{2}}>0.

Hence, we have found two (more than)
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-separated points
(y,t_{1}),(y,t_{2})\in Q\subset BQ
such that
\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y,t_{2}))>\pi_{2}(F_{Q}(y,t_{1}))
. Since
F_{Q}
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-monotone on
BQ
, this implies that the signature of
F_{Q}
is "
+
", as claimed. ∎

By the previous proposition, we may talk about the
signature of a vertical tree
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
. Since the treatments of the two possible signatures
+
or
-
are similar – except that a large number of inequality signs need to be inverted – we will typically assume that the signature of a vertical tree is
+
; vertical trees with this signature will be called
positive
.

We close the section by recording the following global bilipschitz property of
f_{2}
:

Proposition 4.52
.

Let
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
, and let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
be a vertical tree
associated with
f
.
1
1

1

In other words, the rectangles in

\mathcal{T}
are
(A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical, where
H=A(1+\Sigma)^{2}M^{2}B
, with
B\geq 5
, and the verticality is defined relative to
f

via Definition
4.24
.
Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
,
and let
w_{j}=(y_{j},t_{j})\in\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, be
points with the properties
y_{1}<y_{2}
and

(4.53)

|y_{1}-y_{2}|>\max\{\tfrac{1}{100}\min\{\ell(Q_{1}),\ell(Q_{2})\},4N^{2}\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|\},

where
N=A(1+\Sigma)M
. In particular,
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})=|y_{1}-y_{2}|
. Then,

(4.54)

\tfrac{1}{2N}d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq|f_{2}(w_{2})-f_{2}(w_{1})|\leq Md_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2}).

Proof.

The upper bound in (
4.54
) only uses the fact that
f
is
M
-Lipschitz, so it suffices to prove the lower bound. We start by choosing
Q\in\mathcal{T}
such that

(4.55)

w_{1},w_{2}\in HQ\quad\text{and}\quad\tfrac{1}{100}\ell(Q)\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|<\tfrac{2}{100}\ell(Q).

To this
end, let
i\in\{1,2\}
be such that
\min\{\ell(Q_{1}),\ell(Q_{2})\}=\ell(Q_{i})
and let
Q\in\mathcal{T}

be the largest rectangle containing
Q_{i}
with the property

\tfrac{1}{100}\ell(Q)\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|
. Such
Q
exists since
\tfrac{1}{100}\ell(Q_{i})\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\ell(Q(\mathcal{T}))
. Then
w_{i}\in\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{i}\subset\tfrac{1}{2}HQ
, and
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})=|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{2}{100}\ell(Q)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{diam}(HQ)
, so in fact both
w_{1},w_{2}\in HQ
, as desired.

Next we consider the horizontal
(N,\epsilon\ell(Q))
-QIE
F_{Q}\colon HQ\to\mathbb{W}
. We apply Lemma
3.34
to the map
F_{Q}
and
the rectangle
Q
. The main hypothesis (
3.35
) of
the lemma is valid by (
4.53
) and (
4.55
), noting that
8N\epsilon\ell(Q)\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|
as long as
8N\epsilon<\tfrac{1}{100}
, and this follows from the choices made at (
4.43
). Therefore,
Lemma
3.34
implies

|\pi_{1}(F_{Q}(w_{2}))-\pi_{1}(F_{Q}(w_{1}))|\geq\frac{|y_{1}-y_{2}|}{2N}.

To conclude the proof of (
4.54
), we just need to note that

\pi_{1}(F_{Q}(w))=f_{2}(w)
for all
w\in HQ
; this follows from see (
4.35
). ∎

5.
Big projections

In this section, we show that bilipschitz images of
\mathbb{W}
inside
\mathbb{H}
have
big vertical projections
(BVP) in the following sense: if
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
is an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
, and
w\in\mathbb{W}
,
r>0
, then there exists
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
, and a constant
\delta>0
depending only on
M
, such that

\mathcal{H}^{3}[\Pi_{\theta}(f(B(w,r))])\geq\delta r^{3}\sim\mathcal{H}^{3}(B(w,r)).

Here
\Pi_{\theta}
is the
vertical projection
to the subgroup
\mathbb{W}_{\theta}:=R_{\theta}\mathbb{W}
, where
R_{\theta}(z,t):=(e^{i\theta}z,t)
is a rotation around the
t
-axis by angle
\theta
. In particular,
\Pi_{0}(x,y,t)=\Pi(x,y,t)=(y,t+\tfrac{1}{2}xy)
and
\mathbb{W}_{0}=\mathbb{W}
. We will check in the next remark that
\Pi_{\theta}
has the explicit expression

(5.1)

\Pi_{\theta}=R_{\theta}\circ\Pi\circ R_{\theta}^{-1}.

Remark 5.2
.

We recap standard definition of
vertical projection to
\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
: for
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
fixed, every point
p\in\mathbb{H}
can be decomposed uniquely as
p=w_{\theta}\cdot v_{\theta}
, where
w_{\theta}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
and
v_{\theta}\in\mathbb{L}_{\theta}=\{(e^{i\theta}x,0,0):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
. This gives rise to the vertical projection
p\mapsto\Pi_{\theta}(p):=w_{\theta}
. To see that
\Pi_{\theta}
has the expression (
5.1
), one observes that
R_{\theta}^{-1}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
is a group homomorphism, so
R_{\theta}^{-1}p=R_{\theta}^{-1}(w_{\theta}\cdot v_{\theta})=(R_{\theta}^{-1}w_{\theta})\cdot(R_{\theta}^{-1}v_{\theta})
. Since
R_{\theta}^{-1}w_{\theta}\in\mathbb{W}
, this yields
\Pi(R^{-1}_{\theta}(p))=R_{\theta}^{-1}w_{\theta}=R_{\theta}^{-1}(\Pi_{\theta}(p))
, hence
\Pi_{\theta}=R_{\theta}\circ\Pi\circ\mathbb{R}_{\theta}^{-1}
.

Before heading to the results of the section, let us generalise the notion of slope (introduced in Section
4.1
) as follows: if
L
is a horizontal line, and
\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
is a vertical subgroup,
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
, we define the
slope of
L
relative to
\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
as

\angle(L,\mathbb{W}_{\theta}):=\angle(R_{\theta}^{-1}L).

Similarly, for vertical planes
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
, we define
\angle(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\theta}):=\angle(R^{-1}_{\theta}\mathbb{V})
.

Here is the main result of this section:

Proposition 5.3
.

Rickman rugs in
\mathbb{H}
have BVP.

We will prove Proposition
5.3
with the aid of the following more technical statement:

Proposition 5.4
.

For every
M\geq 1
, there exist
H\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
such that the following holds. Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map, let
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
, and let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be a rectangle such that
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};HQ)<\epsilon/H
for some vertical subgroup
\mathbb{V}
with
\angle(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\theta})=0
. Then

(5.5)

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(HQ)])\geq\ell(Q)^{3}.

The proof of Proposition
5.4
uses winding numbers, so we briefly recall them. There exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function
\gamma\mapsto\mathfrak{w}(\gamma,0)
defined on the set of loops (that is: closed paths)
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{0\}
with the following properties:

•

If
\gamma(t)=e^{2\pi int}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\mathfrak{w}(\gamma,0)=n
.

•

If
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are homotopic in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{0\}
, then
\mathfrak{w}(\gamma_{1},0)=\mathfrak{w}(\gamma_{2},0)
.

The value
\mathfrak{w}(\gamma,0)
is the
winding number of
\gamma
with respect to
0
. The winding number with respect to an arbitrary point
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
can be defined by
\mathfrak{w}(\gamma,z)=\mathfrak{w}(\gamma-z,0)
, whenever
\gamma
is a loop in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{z\}
. For us, winding numbers will be useful via the following lemma:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
Q=[0,a]\times[0,b]\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a closed rectangle, and let
f\colon Q\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a continuous map. Write
\gamma:=f(\partial Q)
, where
\partial Q\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is parametrised as a loop in any such way that
\partial Q(0)=0=\partial Q(1)
. If
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\gamma
is a point with
\mathfrak{w}(\gamma,z)\neq 0
, then
z\in f(Q)
.

Proof.

Consider the homotopy
H(s,t):=f((1-s)\partial Q(t))
,
(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}
, which deforms
\gamma
to the constant path
\gamma_{0}(t)\equiv f(0)
. Note that
H
is well-defined, because
(1-s)\partial Q(t)\in[0,(1-s)a]\times[0,(1-s)b]\subset Q
for all
(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}
. If
z\notin f(Q)
, then
\mathrm{Im\,}H\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{z\}
, so
\gamma
and
\gamma_{0}
are homotopic in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{z\}
. Hence
\mathfrak{m}(\gamma,z)=\mathfrak{m}(\gamma_{0},z)=0
, contrary to the assumption. ∎

We are then armed to prove Proposition
5.4
. Let the reader be warned that the letter "
H
" will refer both to a constant, and occasionally a homotopy; the correct interpretation should always be clear from the context.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

First, we may assume that
\theta=0
, since

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(HQ)])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{form130}}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}^{3}(R_{\theta}(\Pi[(R_{\theta}^{-1}\circ f)(HQ)]))=\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi[(R_{\theta}^{-1}\circ f)(HQ)]),

and
f_{\theta}:=R_{\theta}^{-1}\circ f
is an
M
-bilipschitz map with the property
\beta_{f_{\theta}}(R_{\theta}^{-1}(\mathbb{V}),HQ)<\epsilon/H
, where
\angle(R_{\theta}^{-1}(\mathbb{V}))=\angle(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\theta})=0
.

So, assuming
\theta=0
, there exists a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
with
\angle(\mathbb{V})=0
such that
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};HQ)\leq\epsilon/H
. In other words, for every horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}
such that

(5.7)

f(\ell\cap HQ)\subset N(L,\epsilon\ell(Q)).

By pre- and post-composing
f
with left translations and dilations, it is easy to reduce the proof to the following situation:

•

\ell(Q)=1
and
HQ=[0,H)\times[0,H^{2})
,

•

f(0)=0
.

The second point in particular implies, by (
5.7
), that
\operatorname{dist}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{V})\leq\operatorname{dist}(f(0),\mathbb{V})\leq\epsilon
. Therefore, if we replace "
\mathbb{V}
" by "
\mathbb{W}
", the inclusion (
5.7
) continues to hold with "
2\epsilon
" in place of "
\epsilon
". So, we may additionally assume that

•

\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{W}=\{(0,y,t):y,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

Even though the
(\epsilon,H,0)
-verticality assumption would control the behaviour of
f
on
HQ
, it is useful to restrict attention to a slightly smaller rectangle, namely

\hat{Q}:=[0,H]\times[0,1]\subset\overline{HQ}.

For
j\in\{0,1\}
, let
h_{j}:=[0,H]\times\{j\}
and
v_{j}:=\{jH\}\times[0,1]
be the horizontal and vertical edges of
\hat{Q}
, respectively, and let
\partial\hat{Q}=h_{0}v_{1}h_{1}v_{0}
be the loop which travels clockwise around
\partial\hat{Q}
, starting and ending at
0
. We parametrise
\partial\hat{Q}
by the interval
[0,2H+2]
, so the four corners of
\hat{Q}
are
\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})=\{\partial\hat{Q}(0)=\partial\hat{Q}(2H+2),\partial\hat{Q}(H),\partial\hat{Q}(H+1),\partial\hat{Q}(2H+1)\}
. Then
\pi:=\Pi\circ f\circ\partial\hat{Q}\colon[0,2H+2]\to\mathbb{W}
is a loop which starts and ends at
\Pi\circ f(0)=0
.
2
2

2

The ”coordinate projections”
\pi_{1}(y,t)=y
and
\pi_{2}(y,t)=t
of
\mathbb{W}
will not be used in this section, so the notation should not cause confusion.
We plan to show that
\pi
winds (positively or negatively) around all the points in a large rectangle
R\subset\mathbb{W}
, and then infer from Lemma
5.6
that
R\subset\Pi(f(\hat{Q}))
.

We first decompose
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}
in the natural way, where

(5.8)

\begin{cases}\pi_{1}=\Pi\circ f\circ h_{0}\colon[0,H]\to\mathbb{W},\\
\pi_{2}=\Pi\circ f\circ v_{1}\colon[H,H+1]\to\mathbb{W},\\
\pi_{3}=\Pi\circ f\circ h_{1}\colon[H+1,2H+1]\to\mathbb{W},\\
\pi_{4}=\Pi\circ f\circ v_{0}\colon[2H+1,2H+2]\to\mathbb{W},\end{cases}

and

(5.9)

\pi_{i}(t)=\pi_{j}(t),\qquad t\in\mathrm{dom\,}\pi_{i}\cap\mathrm{dom\,}\pi_{j},\,1\leq i,j\leq 4.

In the sequel, we abbreviate
I_{j}:=\mathrm{dom\,}\pi_{j}
. We will fit
\pi
(or rather its trace) inside a union of four slabs, that is: neighbourhoods of lines,
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}\subset\mathbb{W}
, two of which are horizontal, and two vertical, see Figure
1
.

\begin{overpic}[Image6.pdf]
\put(-2.0,25.0){$S_{4}$}
\put(48.0,0.0){$S_{1}$}
\put(95.0,25.0){$S_{2}$}
\put(48.0,48.0){$S_{3}$}
\put(48.0,25.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1.
The black wiggly loop is
\pi
, and the red segments form
\eta\cong\pi
.

The horizontal slabs
S_{1},S_{3}
containing
\pi_{1},\pi_{3}
are neighbourhoods of the horizontal lines (say)
L_{1},L_{3}\subset\mathbb{W}
which best approximate
f(h_{1})
and
f(h_{2})
, respectively. Since

f(h_{j})\subset N(L_{j},\epsilon)\subset N(\mathbb{W},\epsilon)

it is not hard to check that

\pi_{j}=\Pi(f(h_{j}))\subset N(L_{j},A\epsilon),\qquad j\in\{1,3\},

for some absolute constant
A\geq 1
, and we set
S_{j}:=N(L_{j},A\epsilon)
for
j\in\{1,3\}
. (For this argument, "
o(\epsilon)
" would work just as well as "
A\epsilon
".) On the other hand, since
f
is
M
-bilipschitz, the lines
L_{1},L_{3}\subset\mathbb{W}
are reasonably well separated:

\operatorname{dist}(L_{1},L_{3})\geq\operatorname{dist}(f(0,0),f(0,1))-2\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{M}\cdot d_{\mathrm{par}}((0,0),(0,1))-2\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{2M},

choosing
\epsilon<1/(2M)
. Choosing also
\epsilon\leq 1/(8AM)
, the slabs
S_{1}
and
S_{3}
inherit the separation of their central lines:

(5.10)

\operatorname{dist}(S_{1},S_{3})\geq\tfrac{1}{4M}.

Figure
1
depicts the scenario where
S_{3}
is "above"
S_{1}
, but it could also happen that
S_{3}
is "below"
S_{1}
. To define the vertical slabs containing
\pi_{2},\pi_{4}
, first recall that the second coordinate map
w\mapsto f_{2}(w)
is
M
-Lipschitz. It follows that

f_{2}(v_{j})\subset[f_{2}(0,jH)-M,f_{2}(0,jH)+M],\qquad j\in\{0,1\}.

Since
f_{2}
coincides with the second coordinate of
\Pi\circ f
, we infer that
\pi_{2}=\Pi(f(v_{1}))
and
\pi_{4}=\Pi(f(v_{0}))
are contained in vertical slabs
S_{2}
and
S_{4}
of width
2M
around the vertical lines
V_{2}:=\{f_{2}(H,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}
and
V_{4}:=\{f_{2}(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}
, respectively.

Just like
S_{1},S_{3}
, the vertical slabs
S_{2}
and
S_{4}
are also well-separated if the parameter
H
is chosen larger than
20M^{2}
, and
\epsilon
is smaller than
H/(AM)
. In fact, we claim that

(5.11)

|f_{2}(H,0)-f_{2}(0,0)|\geq H/(2M).

To see this, note that
d(f(H,0),f(0,0))\geq H/M
by the
M
-bilipschitz property of
f
, and on the other hand
\operatorname{dist}(f(0,0),L_{0})\leq\epsilon
and
\operatorname{dist}(f(H,0),L_{0})\leq\epsilon
. Then, use Lemma
4.29
to deduce that
f(0,0)
and
f(H,0)
lie at distance
\lesssim\epsilon
from the points
p_{0},p_{H}\in L_{0}
whose second coordinates match those of
f(0,0)
and
f(H,0)
. The lower bound (
5.11
) follows by estimating
|f_{2}(H,0)-f_{2}(0,0)|=d(p_{0},p_{H})\geq d(f(H,0),f(0,0))-2A\epsilon\geq H/(2M)
.

Taking
H:=20M^{2}
, it follows from the definitions of
S_{2},S_{4}
, and the lower bound (
5.11
), that

(5.12)

\operatorname{dist}(S_{2},S_{4})\geq H/(4M).

Of course
S_{4}
can either be to the "left" or "right" from
S_{2}
; Figure
1
shows the latter scenario. Combining (
5.10
) and (
5.12
), we deduce that the area of the open rectangle "
R
" bounded by the four slabs
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}
is
\geq H/(16M^{2})\geq 1
. So, to conclude the proof of (
5.5
), hence the proposition, it remains to show that
R\subset\Pi(f(\hat{Q}))
.

By Lemma
5.6
, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{w}(\pi,z)\neq 0
for all
z\in R
. This hopefully seems quite obvious:
R
is "enclosed" by the union
S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{4}
, and
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}
is a loop with
\pi_{j}\subset S_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq 4
. The details are not much harder: we will deform
\pi
inside
S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{4}
into a polygonal loop
\eta=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}\eta_{4}
with
4
linearly parametrised edges
\eta_{j}=[p_{j},p_{j+1}]\subset S_{j}
. Finally, we observe that if
z\in R
, then
z
in particular lies in the quadrilateral bounded by
\eta
, see Figure
1
. It follows that
\mathfrak{w}(\pi,z)=\mathfrak{w}(\eta,z)\in\{-1,1\}
, since
\eta
is evidently homotopic to either
z+e^{2\pi it}
or
z+e^{-2\pi it}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\setminus\,\{z\}
.

Let us fill in the details. We define
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{4}
by the formulae

\begin{cases}\eta_{1}=[\pi_{1}(0),\pi_{1}(H)]_{I_{1}}\subset S_{1},\\
\eta_{2}=[\pi_{2}(H),\pi_{2}(H+1)]_{I_{2}}\subset S_{2},\\
\eta_{3}=[\pi_{3}(H+1),\pi_{3}(2H+1)]_{I_{3}}\subset S_{3},\\
\eta_{4}=[\pi_{4}(2H+1),\pi_{4}(2H+2)]_{I_{4}}\subset S_{4},\end{cases}

where
I_{j}=\mathrm{dom\,}\pi_{j}=\mathrm{dom\,}\eta_{j}
, and
[p_{1},p_{2}]_{[a,b]}
refers, in general, to the linear parametrisation of the segment
[p_{1},p_{2}]\subset\mathbb{W}
by the interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
, namely

[p_{1},p_{2}]_{[a,b]}(t):=(1-\tfrac{t-a}{b-a})\cdot p_{1}+\tfrac{t-a}{b-a}\cdot p_{2},\qquad t\in[a,b].

Then, we define the polygonal path
\eta\colon[0,2H+2]\to S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{4}
by setting
\eta(t):=\eta_{j}(t)
for
t\in I_{j}
. This definition is consistent by (
5.9
), and
\eta
is continuous.

Next, let
H_{j}\colon[0,1]\times I_{j}\to S_{j}
be the convex homotopy between
\pi_{j}
and
\eta_{j}
, namely
H_{j}(s,t):=(1-s)\pi_{j}(t)+s\eta_{j}(t)
for
(s,t)\in[0,1]\times I_{j}
. Then
H_{j}|_{\{0\}\times I_{j}}=\pi_{j}
,
H_{j}|_{\{1\}\times I_{j}}=\eta_{j}
, and
H_{j}(s,\partial I_{j})\equiv\pi_{j}(\partial I_{j})\equiv\eta_{j}(\partial I_{j})
for all
s\in[0,1]
.

Finally, we define the homotopy
H\colon[0,1]\times[0,2H+2]\to S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{4}
as a concatenation of the homotopies
H_{j}
, namely

H(s,t):=H_{j}(s,t),\qquad s\in[0,1],\,t\in I_{j},\,1\leq j\leq 4.

This definition is well-posed, and
H
is continuous, since

H_{i}(s,t)=H_{j}(s,t),\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq 3,\,s\in[0,1],\,t\in I_{i}\cap I_{j},

by (
5.9
) (for
t\in I_{j}\cap I_{j}
simply
H_{j}(s,t)=\pi(t)=\eta(t)
for all
s\in[0,1]
). Since
\mathrm{Im\,}H\subset S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{4}\subset\mathbb{W}\,\setminus\,R
, the winding numbers of
\pi
and
\eta
with respect to every point in
R
coincide. Clearly
\mathfrak{w}(\eta,z)\in\{-1,1\}
for all
z\in R
, so we conclude that
\mathfrak{w}(\pi,z)\neq 0
for all
z\in R
. Lemma
5.6
now implies that
R\subset\Pi(f(\hat{Q}))
, and the proof of the proposition is complete. ∎

Proposition
5.4
also yields information about the projections of
f(Q)
instead of
f(HQ)
:

Corollary 5.13
.

For every
M\geq 1
, there exist
\delta,\epsilon>0
, depending only on
M
, such that that the following holds. Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
, let
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
, and let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be a rectangle with
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};Q)<\epsilon
for some vertical plane
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
with
\angle(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\theta})=0
. Then,

(5.14)

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}(f(Q)))\geq\delta\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q).

Proof.

Choose a sub-rectangle
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
such that
HQ^{\prime}\subset Q
and
\ell(Q^{\prime})\geq\ell(Q)/(AH)
, where
H\sim_{M}1
is the parameter from Proposition
5.4
, and
A\geq 1
is an absolute constant. If
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
is a horizontal line, then by the assumption
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};Q)<\epsilon
, there exists a horizontal line
L\subset\mathbb{V}
such that

f(\ell\cap HQ^{\prime})\subset f(\ell\cap Q)\subset N(L,\epsilon\ell(Q))=N(L,AH\epsilon\cdot\ell(Q^{\prime})).

In other words,
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};HQ^{\prime})\leq A\epsilon
. Now, if
\epsilon>0
is chosen sufficiently small, depending on
H\sim_{M}1
, Proposition
5.4
shows that

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q)])\geq\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(HQ^{\prime})])\geq\ell(Q^{\prime})^{3}\sim_{M}\ell(Q)^{3}.

Thus (
5.14
) is satisfied for
\delta=\delta(M)>0
small enough. ∎

We then complete the proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

The BVP property clearly follows from its dyadic variant: for every
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
such that
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}(f(Q)))\gtrsim_{M}\ell(Q)^{3}
. To prove this, fix
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, and let
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
be the largest (or one of the largest) sub-rectangle(s) of
Q
with
\beta_{f}(Q^{\prime})<\epsilon
, where
\epsilon=\epsilon(M)>0
is the constant from Corollary
5.13
. Since the rectangles
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
with
\beta_{f}(Q^{\prime})\geq\epsilon
satisfy a Carleson packing condition by Corollary
4.20
, we have
\ell(Q^{\prime})\sim_{M}\ell(Q)
. Now, Corollary
5.13
yields
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
such that

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q)])\geq\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q^{\prime})])\geq\delta\ell(Q^{\prime})^{3}\sim_{M}\ell(Q)^{3},

as desired. ∎

6.
Construction of trees

In this section, we construct the decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
and the trees
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\ldots
which are required by the corona decomposition, Theorem
1.3
, recall also Section
1.2
. This is not too difficult, and the most involved part of the paper only begins in the next section, where each tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is associated with an intrinsic bilipschitz graph
\Gamma_{j}\subset\mathbb{H}
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map,
M\geq 1
. The construction of
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T}_{j})
is very similar to the one described in
[
15
, §7]
: for the reader familiar with
[
15
]
, the only significant difference lies in checking the Carleson packing condition for the top rectangles, see (
D4
) below. As in
[
15
]
, this task requires controlling the total "turning" of certain best-approximating planes; whereas in
[
15
]
the amount of "turning" is controlled by hypothesis on either singular integrals or
\beta
-numbers, the present argument rather relies on the "big projections" property, Proposition
5.3
.

We claim that for every
H\geq 1
, and for every
\epsilon>0
which is sufficiently small in a manner depending only on
M
, there exist constants
\delta>0
(depending on
M
),
\Sigma\geq 1
(depending on
\delta
), and a partition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
with the following properties:

(D1)

The rectangles in
\mathcal{B}
satisfy a Carleson packing condition, that is,

\mathop{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}}}_{Q\subset Q_{0}}|Q|\lesssim_{\epsilon,H,M}|Q_{0}|,\qquad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

(D2)

The rectangles in
\mathcal{G}
are partitioned into countably many trees "
\mathcal{T}
" with top rectangles
Q(\mathcal{T})
. For each tree
\mathcal{T}
, there exists a vertical subgroup
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{W}_{\theta(\mathcal{T})}\subset\mathbb{H}
such that

(6.1)

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta(\mathcal{T})}[f(Q(\mathcal{T}))])\geq\delta\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q(\mathcal{T})).

Further, for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
, there exists a vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q}\subset\mathbb{H}
with
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\leq\Sigma
with the property
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};HQ)<H^{-1}A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon
. If
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{W}
, these properties imply that
\mathcal{T}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree.

(D3)

Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})
if and only if either
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\geq\Sigma/2
, or then at least one child
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbf{ch}(Q)
has
\beta_{f}(HQ^{\prime})\geq H^{-1}A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon
.

(D4)

The top rectangles of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\ldots
satisfy a Carleson packing condition with constants depending only on
\epsilon,H,M
.

The conditions (
D1
) and (
D4
) are exactly the Carleson packing conditions required by (
C1
) and (
C3
) in the definition of corona decompositions – at least if eventually
\epsilon,H\sim_{M,\eta}1
, and this will be the case. The condition (
D3
) is merely an auxiliary condition which helps verify (
D4
) below. The condition (
D2
) is the one to remember: it contains the properties of individual trees which are needed, starting in the next section, to build the
\eta
-approximating intrinsic bilipschitz graphs required by condition (
C2
). In this construction, we will want to choose
H\geq 1
large enough, and
\epsilon>0
small enough, in a manner depending on
M,\eta
, so the decompositions
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}\cup\ldots
will have to be performed with such parameters.

It will soon be apparent that
M
determines
\delta
, then a small "
\delta
" forces us to choose "
\Sigma
" large, and eventually small "
\delta
" and large "
\Sigma
" force us to choose "
\epsilon
" small. So, the decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
can be performed with any
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small to meet these requirements, which eventually just depend on
M
.

Before attempting a "global" decomposition of
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}\cup\mathcal{B}
, we perform a localised version. Fix
\bar{Q}\in\mathcal{D}
arbitrary, and write
\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q}):=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}:Q\subset\bar{Q}\}
. We claim that
\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q})
can be partitioned to collections
\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})
and
\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
with the properties (
D1
)-(
D4
).

We simply let
\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})
consist of all the rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q})
with

(6.2)

\beta_{f}(HQ)\geq H^{-1}A^{-2}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon.

Let
\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q}):=\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q})\,\setminus\,\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})
. For
Q\in\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
, let
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
be the best approximating plane for
f
in
HQ
, so
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V}_{Q};HQ)<H^{-1}A^{-1}(1+\Sigma)^{-2}\epsilon
. In particular,
\beta_{f}(\mathbb{V};Q)<\epsilon
, so if
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
is the unique parameter with
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\theta})=0
, and
\epsilon,\delta>0
are small enough, depending only on
M
, Corollary
5.13
implies
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q))]\geq\delta\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q)
. We also define
\mathbb{W}_{Q}:=\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
for
Q\in\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
, so

(6.3)

\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{Q})=0,\qquad Q\in\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q}).

We now perform the tree decomposition of
\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
in the only possible way. Pick some maximal rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
which has not yet been assigned to any tree. If either the
\mathcal{D}
-parent "
\hat{Q}
" of
Q
lies in
\mathcal{D}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
, or
\hat{Q}\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\hat{\mathcal{T}})
for some existing tree
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
, then
Q
becomes the top rectangle of a new tree
\mathcal{T}
, and we set
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathbb{W}_{Q}=\mathbb{W}_{Q(\mathcal{T})}
. Otherwise,
\hat{Q}\in\hat{\mathcal{T}}\,\setminus\,\mathbf{Leaves}(\hat{\mathcal{T}})
for some existing tree
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
, and we add
Q
to
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
. Now that
Q
has been assigned to some tree
\mathcal{T}
, we declare when, exactly,
Q
is a minimal rectangle in
\mathcal{T}
:

Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{ch}(Q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})\neq\emptyset\quad\text{or}\quad\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\geq\Sigma/2.

This completes the construction of the trees. Note that, for every tree
\mathcal{T}
, the family
\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})
is a union of the collections

\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T}):=\{Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T}):\mathbf{ch}(Q)\cap\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})\neq\emptyset\}

and

\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}):=\{Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T}):\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\geq\Sigma/2\}.

Let us mention that, while it is possible that
Q(\mathcal{T})\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T})
, we always have
Q(\mathcal{T})\in\mathcal{T}\,\setminus\,\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
, because
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q(\mathcal{T})},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})=0
by (
6.3
) and the choice
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathbb{W}_{Q(\mathcal{T})}
.

Which conditions among (
D1
)-(
D4
) are satisfied? The Carleson packing condition for
\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})
required in (
D1
) is satisfied by Corollary
4.20
(also because
\Sigma\sim_{M}1
, as we will soon see). The property (
D3
) of
\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})
is evidently satisfied by construction. The projection condition (
6.1
) and the strong
\beta
-number bound in (
D2
) are also satisfied by construction (and because
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
), so the only part of (
D2
) in doubt is the slope bound
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\leq\Sigma
for
Q\in\mathcal{T}
. We handle this in the next lemma before turning to the most difficult property (
D4
).

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be one of the trees constructed above. If the constant
\epsilon>0
is chosen small enough, depending on
M,\Sigma
, then
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\leq\Sigma
for all
Q\in\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

The inequality is only in doubt for
Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
, so fix
Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
. Note that the
\mathcal{D}
-parent
\hat{Q}
of
Q
lies in
\mathcal{T}\,\setminus\,\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
, hence satisfies
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})<\Sigma/2
, because
Q(\mathcal{T})\notin\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
.

To show that
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\leq\Sigma
, pick two points
w_{1},w_{2}\in Q\subset\hat{Q}
which lie on a common horizontal line and satisfy
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\ell(Q)
. Since
\beta_{f}(Q)<\epsilon
and
\beta_{f}(\hat{Q})<\epsilon
, there exist horizontal lines
L_{Q}\subset\mathbb{V}_{Q}
and
L_{\hat{Q}}\subset\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}}
with the property

\{f(w_{1}),f(w_{2})\}\subset N(L_{Q},\epsilon\ell(Q))\cap N(L_{\hat{Q}},2\epsilon\ell(Q)),

and moreover
\angle(L_{\hat{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})=:\angle(\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})<\Sigma/2
. So, it now suffices to prove the following geometric statement for
r:=2\ell(Q)>0
. Assume that
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\mathbb{H}
are horizontal lines,
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
is a vertical subgroup, and
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{H}
are points satisfying

(a)

\angle(L_{1},\mathbb{W}^{\prime})<\Sigma/2
,

(b)

r/M\leq d(p_{1},p_{2})\leq Mr
,

(c)

\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\subset N(L_{1},\epsilon r)\cap N(L_{2},\epsilon r)
.

Then
\angle(L_{2},\mathbb{W}^{\prime})\leq\Sigma
if
\epsilon>0
is chosen small enough, depending on
M,\Sigma
. After rotating
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{W}
, if necessary, this statement follows directly from Lemma
3.6
(more precisely the first part about "
|a_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma
"). ∎

We finally onsider condition (
D4
). Let
\kappa\sim_{M}\delta\sim_{M}1
be a suitable small constant to be determined soon, and classify the trees according to the following three types:

(i)

Those with
|Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})|\geq\kappa\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|
.

(ii)

Those with
|\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T})|\geq\kappa\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|
.

(iii)

Those with
|\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})|\geq(1-2\kappa)\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|
.

Every tree has one of the types (i)-(iii), because

Q(\mathcal{T})\subset[Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})]\cup[\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T})]\cup[\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})].

We next claim that
trees of type (iii) do not exist
if the "approximation parameter"
\epsilon>0
is chosen small enough, and and the "slope parameter"
\Sigma\geq 1
is chosen large enough, depending only on
\delta\sim_{M}1
. To see this, let
\mathcal{T}
be a hypothetical tree of type (iii), and recall from (
6.1
) that

(6.5)

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q(\mathcal{T}))])\geq\delta\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q(\mathcal{T}))

for the unique parameter
\theta\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
such that
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{W}_{\theta}
. We claim that

(6.6)

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q)])=o(\epsilon,\Sigma)\cdot\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q),\qquad Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}),

where
o(\epsilon,\Sigma)
denotes a constant which tends to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
and
\Sigma\to\infty
. Let us complete the non-existence proof of trees of type (iii), assuming that (
6.6
) has already been established. In addition to (
6.6
), we will need the following estimate:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}))])

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{3}(f(Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})))

(6.7)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M}\kappa\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|.

The first inequality follows from the well-known fact that vertical projections do not increase
3
-dimensional Hausdorff measure by more than a constant, see
[
7
, Lemma 3.6]
. The second inequality follows from the the
M
-bilipschitz property of
f
, and the hypothesis that
\mathcal{T}
has type (iii). By (
6.7
), we may choose
\kappa\sim_{M}\delta
so small that
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}))])<(\delta/2)\cdot\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q(\mathcal{T}))
. Using this estimate, (
6.5
), and the disjointness of the rectangles in
\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})
, we have

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q(\mathcal{T}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}))])+\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T}))])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})}\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi_{\theta}[f(Q)])+\frac{\delta}{2}\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|

\displaystyle\leq o(\epsilon,\Sigma)\sum_{Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})}\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q)+\frac{\delta}{2}\cdot|Q(\mathcal{T})|<\delta|Q(\mathcal{T})|,

and choosing
\epsilon>0
so small and
\Sigma\geq 1
so large, depending on
\delta\sim_{M}1
, that
o(\epsilon,\Sigma)<\delta/2
. This contradiction will show that trees of type (iii) exist, once (
6.6
) has been established.

To prove (
6.6
), fix
Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
. Applying the rotation
R_{\theta}^{-1}
, we may assume
\theta=0
, so
\Pi_{\theta}=\Pi
,
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{W}
, and
\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q})=\angle(\mathbb{V}_{Q},\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{T}})\geq\Sigma/2
, where
\mathbb{V}_{Q}
is a vertical plane with
f(Q)\subset N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon\ell(Q))
. Of course we also have
f(Q)\subset B(f(c_{Q}),M\ell(Q))
. Putting this information together, (
6.6
) will follow from a geometric estimate of the form

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi[B(p,r)\cap N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon r)])=o(\epsilon,\Sigma)\cdot r^{3},\qquad\angle(\mathbb{V})\geq\Sigma/2,\,p\in\mathbb{H},\,r>0.

The claim is invariant under scaling and left translation, so we may assume that
\mathbb{V}
is a vertical subgroup, and
r=1
. Also, since we only need to consider the situation where
B(p,r)\cap N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon r)\neq\emptyset
, we may assume that
p\in\mathbb{V}
, replacing "
r
" by "
2r
" if necessary. After this reduction, we may apply one more left translation which keeps the subgroup
\mathbb{V}
fixed, but sends
p
to
0
; in other words, it suffices to show

\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi[B(0,1)\cap N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon)])=o(\epsilon,\Sigma),\qquad\angle(\mathbb{V})\geq\Sigma/2.

This is so obvious that we leave further calculations to the reader (if
\epsilon=0
and
\angle(\mathbb{V})=\infty
, the projection
\Pi(B(0,1)\cap\mathbb{V})
is just a segment of length
\sim 1
on the
t
-axis).

We have now proven that trees of type (iii) do not exist. So, to verify the Carleson packing condition for the tops
Q(\mathcal{T})
in property (
D4
), it suffices to consider trees of type (i)-(ii). First, the sets
Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})\subset Q(\mathcal{T})
are disjoint for distinct trees, so

\mathop{\sum_{\mathcal{T}\text{ type (i)}}}_{Q(\mathcal{T})\subset Q_{0}}|Q(\mathcal{T})|\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\mathop{\sum_{\mathcal{T}\text{ type (i)}}}_{Q(\mathcal{T})\subset Q_{0}}|Q(\mathcal{T})\,\setminus\,\cup\mathbf{Leaves}(\mathcal{T})|\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\cdot|Q_{0}|\sim_{M}|Q_{0}|,\quad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

To handle trees of type (ii), we use the Carleson packing condition for the strong vertical
\beta
-numbers in Corollary
4.20
, recalling that every leaf
Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T})
has a child
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbf{ch}(Q)
with
\beta_{f}(HQ^{\prime})\gtrsim_{M}\epsilon
:

\mathop{\sum_{\mathcal{T}\text{ type (ii)}}}_{Q(\mathcal{T})\subset Q_{0}}|Q(\mathcal{T})|\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\mathop{\sum_{\mathcal{T}\text{ type (ii)}}}_{Q(\mathcal{T})\subset Q_{0}}\sum_{Q\in\mathbf{Leaves}_{1}(\mathcal{T})}|Q|\lesssim\frac{1}{\kappa}\mathop{\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}}_{\beta(HQ)\gtrsim_{M}\epsilon}|Q|\lesssim_{\epsilon,H,M}|Q_{0}|,\quad Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

This completes the proofs of (
D1
)-(
D4
) for the decomposition
\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q})=\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q})\cup\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q})
, where
\bar{Q}\in\mathcal{D}
was an arbitrary "starting rectangle".

To infer, from this special case, a decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
satisfying properties (
D1
)-(
D4
), one follows
[
15
, p. 38]
verbatim. Choose a sequence of disjoint "starting rectangles"
\bar{Q}_{1},\bar{Q}_{2},\ldots
with the following property: every rectangle in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is contained in
\bigcup\bar{Q}_{j}
for
n\geq 0
, and for
n<0
, the same is still true for every rectangle in
\mathcal{D}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{E}_{n}
, where
\operatorname{card}\mathcal{E}_{n}\lesssim 1
. Such a sequence
\bar{Q}_{1},\bar{Q}_{2}\ldots
is easy to pick by hand, or see
[
15
, p. 38]
for a more abstract construction; the idea is roughly to cover
B(0,1)
by disjoint rectangles of side-length
1
, then
B(0,2)\,\setminus\,B(0,1)
by disjoint rectangles of side-length
2
(without overlapping the unit rectangles), and so on. Once this is done properly, all the rectangles in
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
with
\operatorname{dist}(Q,0)\gg 2^{-n}
will be contained in the union
\bigcup\bar{Q}_{j}
, and of course
\operatorname{card}\{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{n}:\operatorname{dist}(Q,0)\lesssim 2^{-n}\}\lesssim 1
.

Now, we define
\mathcal{G}:=\cup\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q}_{j})
and
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{D}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{G}
. The tree partition of
\mathcal{G}
is given as the union of the tree partitions of the families
\mathcal{G}(\bar{Q}_{j})\subset\mathcal{D}(\bar{Q}_{j})
. The conditions (
D2
)-(
D3
) concerning individual trees are valid without problems, and the Carleson packing condition (
D4
) follows from the disjointness of the rectangles
\bar{Q}_{1},\bar{Q}_{2},\ldots
The Carleson packing condition for
\mathcal{B}
postulated in (
D1
) is also valid, because

\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{j\geq 1}\mathcal{B}(\bar{Q}_{j})\cup\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{E}_{n}.

This completes the construction of the trees satisfying conditions (
D1
)-(
D4
).

7.
Constructing intrinsic bilipschitz graphs

Recalling the the decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{G}
achieved in the previous section, the proof of Theorem
1.3
is only one component short: to every tree
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{j}
, satisfying the properties stated in (
D2
), we need to associate an intrinsic bilipschitz graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
which
\eta
-approximates
f
in the sense specified in (
1.8
). Performing a rotation around the
t
-axis if necessary, we may assume that the tree
\mathcal{T}
is
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical, where
\epsilon,H
may be chosen freely (as long as
\epsilon>0
was taken small enough, depending on
M
), and
\Sigma
is a parameter determined by
M
. The existence of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
is the content of the following proposition:

Proposition 7.1
.

For every
M\geq 1
and
\eta,\Sigma>0
, there exist constants
\epsilon>0
and
H\geq 10
such that the following holds. Let
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
be an
M
-bilipschitz map, and let
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{D}
be an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree associated with
f
. Then, there exists an intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}\subset\mathbb{H}
over the plane
\mathbb{W}
such that

(7.2)

\operatorname{dist}(f(w),\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}})\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q),\qquad w\in 2Q,\,Q\in\mathcal{T}.

The intrinsic Lipschitz constant of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
only depends on
M
and
\Sigma
. The graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
can be chosen so that it admits a
C
-bilipschitz parametrisation by
\mathbb{W}
, where
C
also only depends on
M
and
\Sigma
.

The intrinsic graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
will be constructed by specifying its
characteristic curves
, but any general theory of these curves is not required to understand the following proposition:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
M\geq 1
,
\Sigma,\eta>0
,
f\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
, and
\mathcal{T}
have the same meaning as in Proposition
7.1
. Then, the following holds if
H,K\geq 1
are chosen large enough depending on
M,\Sigma,\eta
, and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
is chosen small enough depending on all the previous parameters. There exists a map
\tau=\tau_{\mathcal{T}}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the following properties.

(
\tau_{1}
)

For every
t\in\mathbb{R}
fixed,
y\mapsto\tau(y,t)\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})
, and
a fortiori

\partial_{y}\tau(y,t)
is
2\Sigma
-Lipschitz:

|\partial_{y}\tau(y_{2},t)-\partial_{y}\tau(y_{2},t)|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{2}-y_{1}|,\qquad y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

(
\tau_{2}
)

There exists a constant
\theta>0
, depending only on
M,\Sigma
, such that

\theta\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leq|\tau(y,t_{2})-\tau(y,t_{1})|\leq\theta^{-1}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\qquad t_{1},t_{2},y\in\mathbb{R}.

In particular, the graphs
\{(y,\tau(y,t)):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, foliate
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(
\tau_{3}
)

For every
y\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, the map
t\mapsto\partial_{y}\tau(y,t)
is
\tfrac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous with constant
\eta
:

|\partial_{y}\tau(y,t_{2})-\partial_{y}\tau(y,t_{1})|\leq\eta\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|},\qquad t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

(
\tau_{4}
)

Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}
be a rectangle with corner
w:=(x,t)\in\mathcal{C}(Q)
, and let
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
be the unique horizontal line containing
w
. Let
q:=q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric introduced in Section
4.3
. Then,

|\tau(y,t)-q(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}(y,t)-\dot{q}(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q)

for all
y\in B(f_{2}(w),K\ell(Q))
, where
f_{2}
is the second component of
f
.

Remark 7.4
.

Property (
\tau_{4}
) captures, roughly speaking, that the values of
\tau
are dictated by approximate quadrics associated with rectangles
Q\in\mathcal{T}
. This statement encodes the relation between
\tau
and
f
, because, by Corollary
4.40
, the values of the approximate quadrics evaluated at
f_{2}(w)
are nearly determined by
f(w)
. The property (
\tau_{4}
) is eventually used to verify the
\eta
-approximation in (
7.2
), and for this purpose, we will need to choose
K\geq A(1+\Sigma)M
for a sufficiently large absolute constant
A\geq 1
.

During the remainder of this section, we will reduce the proof of Proposition
7.1
(hence Theorem
1.3
) to Proposition
7.3
, which we now take for granted. The proof of Proposition
7.3
is contained in the next section. To prove Proposition
7.1
, we define a function
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
implicitly by

\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,y,\tau(y,t)):=\dot{\tau}(y,t),\qquad(y,t)\in\mathbb{W},

where
\dot{\tau}(y,t):=\partial_{y}\tau(y,t)
, here and always. The definition of
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is well-posed by properties (
\tau_{1}
)-(
\tau_{2}
). We also define the
intrinsic graph map

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,y,\tau(y,t))

\displaystyle:=(0,y,\tau(y,t))\cdot(\dot{\tau}(y,t),0,0),\qquad(y,t)\in\mathbb{W},

and the intrinsic graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}:=\{\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,y,t):(0,y,t)\in\mathbb{W}\}
.

Remark 7.5
.

I explain, briefly and informally, how the properties (
\tau_{1}
)-(
\tau_{4}
) of the map "
\tau
" translate into geometric properties of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
. The property (
\tau_{1}
) says nothing beyond "
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is an intrinsic Lipschitz function". Property (
\tau_{2}
) is the deepest property, and allows us to find a bilipschitz parametrisation for
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
; more on this very soon. Property
(\tau_{3})
means that the graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
looks everywhere, and at every scale, like a "vertical Lipschitz flag", up to an error quantified by the parameter
\eta
. Namely, if
\eta=0
, then
\dot{\tau}(y,t)
is independent of
t
, and therefore also
(0,y,t)\mapsto\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,y,t)
is independent of
t
. And the intrinsic graph of an intrinsic Lipschitz function, which does not depend on the
t
-variable, has the form
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{H}
, where
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}_{2}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a Lipschitz graph.

Finally, condition (
\tau_{4}
) means that the "Lipschitz flag" mentioned above looks like a vertical plane near all points of the form
\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,f_{2}(w),t)
, where
w=(x,t)\in\mathcal{C}(Q)
and
Q\in\mathcal{T}
; to see this, try replacing "
\tau
" and "
\dot{\tau}
" by "
q
" and "
\dot{q}
" in the definition of
\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}
, and notice (or check form (
7.15
)) that the image of
\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is a vertical plane in this case.

The map
\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is the "standard" intrinsic graph map of
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
, and is almost never a bilipschitz map
\mathbb{W}\to\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
, as explained in Section
3.1
. To obtain a
bi
lipschitz parametrisation of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
, one instead needs to consider
\Psi\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
defined by

(7.6)

\Psi(y,t):=\Phi_{\mathcal{T}}(0,y,\tau(y,t)),\qquad(y,t)\in\mathbb{W}.

It is not a new idea that the map "
\Psi
" above is a good candidate for bilipschitz parametrising
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
by
\mathbb{W}
; the same map was used earlier in, at least,
[
13
]
and
[
4
]
in a scenario where
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is assumed
a priori
Euclidean Lipschitz regular. This need not be true in our setting, but condition (
\tau_{2}
) is an adequate substitute.

Proposition 7.7
.

The function
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is an intrinsic Lipschitz function. Further, the map
\Psi
is a bilipschitz homeomorphism
(\mathbb{W},d_{\mathrm{par}})\to\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
. The intrinsic Lipschitz, and bilipschitz constants, here, depend only on
\eta,\Sigma,\theta
in the properties
(\tau_{1})
-
(\tau_{3})
.

Recalling that
\theta\sim_{M,\Sigma}1
, and choosing
0<\eta<\min\{\Sigma,\theta\}
, Proposition
7.7
implies the claims in Proposition
7.1
about the intrinsic Lipschitz constant of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
, and the bilipschitz constant of its
\mathbb{W}
-parametrisation
\Psi
.

Proof of Proposition
7.7
.

Fix
y_{1},y_{2},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, and write
p_{j}:=\Psi(y_{j},t_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. These are two arbitrary points on
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
. We record immediately that
p^{-1}\cdot p_{2}=\Psi(y_{1},t_{1})^{-1}\cdot\Psi(y_{2},t_{2})=(a,b,c)
, where

(7.8)

\begin{cases}a=\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1}),\\
b=y_{2}-y_{1},\\
c=\tau(y_{2},t_{2})-\tau(y_{1},t_{1})-\tfrac{1}{2}(y_{2}-y_{1})\cdot[\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})+\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})].\end{cases}

To prove that
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
is intrinsic Lipschitz, we need to show (recall (
3.2
)) that

(7.9)

|\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})|\lesssim_{\eta,\Sigma,\theta}\|\Pi(p_{1}^{-1}\cdot p_{2})\|,

and to prove that
\Psi
is bilipschitz, we need to show that

(7.10)

d(p_{1},p_{2})=\|p_{1}^{-1}\cdot p_{2}\|\sim_{\eta,\Sigma,\theta}\max\{|y_{1}-y_{2}|,\sqrt{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}\}.

We start with the latter task. The third component "
c
" in (
7.8
) simplifies if either
y_{1}=y_{2}
or
t_{1}=t_{2}
. First, if
y_{1}=y=y_{2}
, then

(7.11)

|c|=|\tau(y,t_{2})-\tau(y,t_{1})|\in[\theta|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\theta^{-1}|t_{2}-t_{1}|]

by condition (
\tau_{2}
). Second, if
t_{1}=t=t_{2}
, then

(7.12)

|c|=\left|\int_{y_{1}}^{y_{2}}\frac{2\dot{\tau}(y,t)-[\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t)+\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t)]}{2}\,dy\right|\lesssim\Sigma|y_{2}-y_{1}|^{2}

by condition (
\tau_{1}
). Since moreover, by conditions (
\tau_{1})
and (
\tau_{3}
),

|a|\leq|\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{2})|+|\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{2}-y_{1}|+\eta\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|},

we can now use the triangle inequality to conclude from (
7.11
)-(
7.12
) that

d(p_{1},p_{2})\lesssim\max\{\Sigma,\sqrt{\Sigma},\eta,\theta^{-1/2}\}\cdot d_{\mathrm{par}}((y_{1},t_{1}),(y_{2},t_{2})).

Hence
\Psi\colon\mathbb{W}\to\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
is Lipschitz. To prove a lower bound, observe first that

d(\Psi(y_{1},t_{1}),\Psi(y_{2},t_{2}))=d(p_{1},p_{2})\geq|b|=|y_{2}-y_{1}|.

So, if
\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\lesssim_{\Sigma,\theta}|y_{2}-y_{1}|
, the lower bound is clear. If, on the other hand
|y_{2}-y_{1}|\ll\sqrt{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}
, or more precisely
(1+10\max\{\Sigma,\sqrt{\Sigma}\})|y_{2}-y_{1}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}
, then we use (
7.11
)-(
7.12
), and the upper bound for
|a|
(with
t_{1}=t_{2}
) to deduce

\displaystyle d(p_{1},p_{2})

\displaystyle\geq d(\Psi(y_{1},t_{1}),\Psi(y_{1},t_{2}))-d(\Psi(y_{1},t_{2}),(y_{2},t_{2}))

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}-(1+10\max\{\Sigma,\sqrt{\Sigma}\})|y_{2}-y_{1}|

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\gtrsim\sqrt{\theta}\cdot d_{\mathrm{par}}((y_{1},t_{1}),(y_{2},t_{2})).

This completes the proof of the bilipschitz property (
7.10
).

We then turn to the proof of the intrinsic Lipschitz condition (
7.9
). First, we spell out

\Pi(p_{1}^{-1}\cdot p_{2})=(0,y_{2}-y_{1},\tau(y_{2},t_{2})-\tau(y_{1},t_{1})-(y_{2}-y_{1})\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})).

Denote the third component "
h
", and estimate it from below as follows:

\displaystyle|h|

\displaystyle\geq|\tau(y_{2},t_{2})-\tau(y_{2},t_{1})|-|\tau(y_{2},t_{1})-\tau(y_{1},t_{1})-(y_{2}-y_{2})\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})|

\displaystyle\geq\theta\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|-\left|\int_{y_{1}}^{y_{2}}\dot{\tau}(y,t_{1})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})\,dy\right|\geq\theta\cdot|t_{2}-y_{1}|-2\Sigma\cdot|y_{2}-y_{1}|^{2}.

We used here properties (
\tau_{1}
)-(
\tau_{2}
). Consequently, if
2\Sigma\cdot|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{2}\leq\tfrac{\theta}{2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|
, then
\|\Pi(p_{1}^{-1}\cdot p_{2})\|\geq\sqrt{|h|}\gtrsim_{\theta}\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}
. In the opposite case,

\|\Pi(p^{-1}\cdot p_{2})\|\geq|y_{2}-y_{1}|\gtrsim_{\Sigma,\theta}\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}.

In both cases,
\|\Pi(p^{-1}\cdot p_{2})\|\gtrsim_{\Sigma,\theta}|y_{2}-y_{1}|+\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}
.

Next, we use properties (
\tau_{2}
)-(
\tau_{3}
):

\displaystyle|\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|\dot{\tau}(y_{2},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{2})|+|\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(y_{1},t_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Sigma\cdot|y_{2}-y_{1}|+\eta\cdot\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}.

Comparing this upper bound with the lower bound
\|\Pi(p^{-1}\cdot p_{2})\|\gtrsim_{\Sigma,\theta}|y_{2}-y_{1}|+\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}
completes the proof of (
7.9
), and the proof of the proposition. ∎

The next proposition shows that the intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
satisfies the main requirement of Proposition
7.1
, namely (
7.2
). This will be based on properties (
\tau_{3}
)-(
\tau_{4}
).

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}
, and assume that the constant
K
in (
\tau_{4}
) satisfies
K\geq A(1+\Sigma)M
. Then,
\operatorname{dist}(f(w),\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}})=o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)
for all
w\in 2Q
.

The notation
o(\eta)
here refers to a constant which can be chosen arbitrarily small by taking
\eta>0
in Proposition
7.3
sufficiently small,
depending only on
M
and
\Sigma
. Hence, Proposition
7.13
implies (
7.2
), modulo the effort of re-naming small constants.

Proof of Proposition
7.13
.

The idea is simple: since
Q\in\mathcal{T}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, we know that
f(2Q)
is approximated by the vertical plane
\mathbb{V}:=\mathbb{V}_{Q}
up to an error
\epsilon\ell(Q)
. So, choosing
0<\epsilon\leq\eta
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{V}
is further approximated by
\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}}
in a sufficiently large neighbourhood of
f(Q)
, up to an error
o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)
.

For this purpose, let
w_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(Q)
be an arbitrary corner. Then,
2Q\subset B(w_{0},5\ell(Q))
, so

f(2Q)\subset B(f(w_{0}),5M\ell(Q))\cap N(\mathbb{V},\epsilon\ell(Q))\subset N(\mathbb{V}\cap B(f(w_{0}),6M\ell(Q)),\epsilon\ell(Q)),

using
M\geq 1\geq\epsilon
in the last inclusion. Abbreviate
B(f(w_{0}),6M\ell(Q))=:B_{0}
. It remains to prove

(7.14)

\mathbb{V}\cap B_{0}\subset N(\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}},o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)),

because then
f(2Q)\subset N(\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}},o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q))
.

To prove (
7.14
), let
\ell=\{(y,t_{0}):y\in\mathbb{R}\}\subset\mathbb{W}
be the unique horizontal line containing
w_{0}
, and let
q:=q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric. Thus
q(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c
for some
|a|\leq\Sigma
and
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
. With this notation, we parametrise
\mathbb{V}
with the map

(7.15)

V(y,t)=(0,y,q(y)+t)\cdot(\dot{q}(y),0,0)=(ay+b,y,\tfrac{1}{2}by+c+t),

which has the special property
V(y,t)=V(y,0)\cdot(0,0,t)
. The map
V
is an analogue of the bilipschitz parametrisation "
\Psi
" for
\mathbb{V}
; replace
\tau(y,t)
by
q(y)+t
in (
7.6
) to see the connection). It is easy to see that
V
is a bilipschitz embedding with constant
\lesssim 1+\Sigma
; one can either use the formula (
7.15
) directly, or repeat the proof of Proposition
7.7
, noting that
t\mapsto q(y)+t
is
1
-bilipschitz, and
t\mapsto\partial_{y}[q(y)+t]=\dot{q}(y)
is independent of
t
.

We next wish to parametrise
\mathbb{V}\cap B_{0}
by
V
, and we claim the following: there exists an absolute constant
A\geq 1
such that

(7.16)

\mathbb{V}\cap B_{0}\subset V(B[(f_{2}(w_{0}),0),AM(1+\Sigma)\ell(Q)]),

The proof of (
7.16
) is based on recalling that
q=q_{Q,\ell}
is the approximate quadric associated with the horizontal line
\ell\ni w_{0}
. Recalling the definition of the map
\iota_{Q}
from (
4.30
), we in fact have
V(f_{2}(w_{0}),0)=\iota_{Q}(w_{0})
(use the formula (
7.15
) to verify this), and hence

(7.17)

d(f(w_{0}),V(f_{2}(w_{0}),0))=d(f(w_{0}),\iota_{Q}(w_{0}))\lesssim(1+\Sigma)\epsilon\ell(Q)

by Lemma
4.29
(or see the discussion below (
4.30
)). Since
V
is an
A(1+\Sigma)
-bilipschitz parametrisation of
\mathbb{V}
, and
f(w_{0})
is the centre of
B_{0}
, (
7.16
) follows from (
7.17
).

With (
7.16
) in hand, (
7.14
) will evidently follow from

(7.18)

V(B[(f_{2}(w_{0}),0),AM(1+\Sigma)\ell(Q)])\subset N(\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}},o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)).

To prove (
7.18
), pick
(y,h)\in B[(f_{2}(w_{0}),0),AM(1+\Sigma)\ell(Q)]
, hence

(7.19)

|y-f_{2}(w_{0})|\leq A(1+\Sigma)M\ell(Q)\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{|h|}\leq A(1+\Sigma)M\ell(Q).

We need to prove that
\operatorname{dist}(V(y,t),\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}})=o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)
. We start by verifying

(7.20)

d(V(y,0),\Psi(y,t_{0}))=o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q).

Recall that
t_{0}
is second coordinate of
w_{0}
. To prove (
7.20
), compute explicitly

\Psi(y,t_{0})^{-1}\cdot V(y,0)=(\dot{q}(y)-\dot{\tau}(y,t_{0}),0,q(y)-\tau(y,t_{0})).

Property (
\tau_{4}
), combined with the first part of (
7.19
) and
K\geq A(1+\Sigma)M
, now implies

|\tau(y,t_{0})-q(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}(y,t_{0})-\dot{q}(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q),

and (
7.20
) follows. As a consequence,

d(V(y,h),\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,h))=d(V(y,0)\cdot(0,0,h),\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,h))\leq o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q).

Now
\operatorname{dist}(V(y,h),\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}})=o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q)
, and hence (
7.18
), will follow as soon as we prove

(7.21)

\operatorname{dist}(\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,h),\Gamma_{\mathcal{T}})=o(\eta)\cdot\ell(Q).

To see this, let us spell out the definition:

\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,t)=(\dot{\tau}(y,t_{0}),y,\tau(y,t_{0})+t+\tfrac{1}{2}y\cdot\dot{\tau}(y,t_{0})).

By property (
\tau_{2}
), the map
t\mapsto\tau(y,t)
is a
\theta^{-1}
-bilipschitz homeomorphism
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, for any fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\theta\sim_{M,\Sigma}1
. Recalling from (
7.19
) that
|h|\lesssim_{M,\Sigma}\ell(Q)^{2}
, there consequently exists
t_{h}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|t_{h}-t_{0}|\lesssim_{M,\Sigma}\ell(Q)^{2}
such that
\tau(y,t_{h})=\tau(y,t_{0})+h
. To complete the proof of (
7.21
), and the proposition, we claim that

d(\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,h),\Psi(y,t_{h}))\leq_{M,\Sigma}\eta\cdot\ell(Q).

This follows immediately from computing explicitly

[\Psi(y,t_{0})\cdot(0,0,h)]^{-1}\cdot\Psi(y,t_{h})=(\dot{\tau}(y,t_{h})-\dot{\tau}(y,t_{0}),0,0),

recalling that
|t_{h}-t_{0}|\lesssim_{M,\Sigma}\ell(Q)^{2}
, and using the
\tfrac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuity of
t\mapsto\dot{\tau}(y,t)
with constant
\eta
, recall property (
\tau_{3}
). The proof of Proposition
7.13
is complete. ∎

8.
Reduction to an inductive construction

The purpose of this section is to reduce Proposition
7.3
to yet another result, Proposition
8.3
below. We first describe this proposition, and the details of the reduction are contained in Section
8.1
. With no loss of generality, we assume that the top rectangle of the given
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree
\mathcal{T}
is
Q(\mathcal{T})=[0,1)^{2}\subset\mathbb{W}
. Also, with no loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{T}
is a positive vertical tree, that is, the signature of
\mathcal{T}
is
+
. The case of negative signature could be reduced to the positive case, but in fact it would be easier to make minor changes in the argument whenever the positive signature is used.

Instead of constructing
\tau\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
directly, we will prove, inductively, the existence of a sequence of maps
\tau_{n}
,
n\geq 0
, defined on the dyadic horizontal lines of generation
n
:

\mathcal{L}_{n}=\{\mathbb{R}\times\{k\cdot 2^{-n}\}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

The lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
will be in one-to-one correspondence with
characteristics of generation
n
, denoted
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, each of which is a graph of a
C^{2}
-function, with
2\Sigma
-Lipschitz first derivative. For
n\geq 0
, we will construct a map
\tau_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\to C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus, the
\tau_{n}
-image of every line in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
will be a real-valued
C^{2}
-function defined on
\mathbb{R}
. The relationship between
\tau_{n}
and
\tau
will, eventually, be that if
\ell=\mathbb{R}\times\{k\cdot 2^{-n}\}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, then

\tau(y,k\cdot 2^{-n})\approx\tau_{n}(\ell)(y),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R},

where the error tends geometrically to
0
as
n\to\infty
.

The construction of the maps
\tau_{n}
involves a number of constant parameters; we gather here some reminders and information. We have already encountered the bilipschitz constant
M
, the "slope" constant
\Sigma
, and the constants
B\geq 5
(such that
F_{Q}
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(Q)
-increasing on
BQ
by Lemma
4.45
),
H\geq A(1+\Sigma)M^{2}B
, and
N=A(1+\Sigma)M
. Also, the constant
K\geq 1
already appeared in condition (
\tau_{4}
) of Proposition
7.3
, and we mentioned in Remark
7.4
that we need to choose
K\geq A(1+\Sigma)M
for the purpose of verifying (
7.2
). Given such a constant "
K
", we will below need to assume

(8.1)

H\geq A\max\{N^{2},KN\}.

The letter "
A
" will continue to denote a generic large absolute constant, whose value may change without separate mention. In addition to all the previous constants, there will be a new small constant
\delta>0
, which may be specified by the "user", but must be sufficiently small in a manner depending on
M,\Sigma
, and
\eta
(as in property (
\tau_{3}
) of Proposition
7.3
). The constant "
\delta
" here has nothing to do with the "projection constant" which appeared in (
6.1
); this previous "
\delta
" will no longer be used.

Finally, the constant
\epsilon>0
in the definition of
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangles needs to be chosen small in a manner depending on all the previous constants. All in all, the relations between the constants are given by the following informal sequence:

(8.2)

1\lesssim_{\delta,K,M,\Sigma}\epsilon\ll\{\delta,M,\Sigma\}\ll K\ll H\lesssim_{\delta,K,M,\Sigma}1.

After this introduction, here is the statement containing the properties of the maps
\tau_{n}
:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
M\geq 1
,
\Sigma>0
, let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small relative to
M,\Sigma
, let
K\geq 1
be sufficiently large depending on
\delta,M,\Sigma
, let
H\geq 1
be sufficiently large depending
K,M,\Sigma
, and let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small depending on
\delta,H,K,M,\Sigma
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree with top
Q(\mathcal{T})=[0,1)^{2}
. Then, for every
n\geq 0
, there exists a map
\tau_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\to C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the following axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
).

The axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
) closely mirror the properties (
\tau_{1}
)-(
\tau_{4}
) listed in Proposition
7.3
; in fact, (
X1
)-(
X4
) correspond directly to (
\tau_{1}
)-(
\tau_{4}
), and (
X5
) is an additional axiom which ensures that the maps
\tau_{n}
"form a Cauchy sequence in
C^{1}
" – a deliberately vague statement to be clarified in Section
8.1
. We move to the axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
).

The first axiom requires that
\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)
is
2\Sigma
-Lipschitz for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
:

(X1)

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{1}-y_{2}|,\qquad y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\,n\geq 0,\,\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}.

The characteristics will have the same "vertical ordering" as the lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. Since
\pi_{2}(\ell)\in\mathbb{R}
is a singleton for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, we may define
\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}:=\pi(\ell_{2})-\pi(\ell_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
. We write
\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
if
\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}>0
. If
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
, the second axiom states that there exists a constant
\theta=\theta(M,\Sigma)>0
such that

(X2)

\theta(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})\leq\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\leq\theta^{-1}(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

This is the only axiom where the positive signature of
\mathcal{T}
