is visible. If the signature were negative, one would simply replace
\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)
by
\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)
.

The slopes of neighbouring characteristics do not differ much: the third axiom states that if
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and
\bar{\ell}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\}
, then

(X3)

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\bar{\ell})(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

Here
\ell^{\uparrow}
and
\ell_{\downarrow}
refer to the
\mathcal{L}_{n}
-neighbours of
\ell
: by definition,
\ell^{\uparrow}:=\ell+2^{-n}
and
\ell_{\downarrow}:=\ell-2^{-n}
.

The fourth axiom nails the connection between the characteristics in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and the map
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{H}
via the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadrics, recall Section
4.3
. For
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, recall the notation
\mathcal{C}(Q)
for the four corners of
Q
.

Definition 8.4

(
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}_{m}
)
.

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write

\mathcal{C}_{m}:=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}}\mathcal{C}(Q)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C}:=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{m}.

For a horizontal line
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
, we also write
\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell):=\mathcal{C}_{m}\cap\ell
.

So, the set
\mathcal{C}
consists of the corners of rectangles
only in
\mathcal{T}
. Since we assume that the top of
\mathcal{T}
is
[0,1)^{2}
, we have
\mathcal{T}_{m}=\emptyset
, hence
\mathcal{C}_{m}=\emptyset
, for all
m<0
, and
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
. The axiom (
X4
), below, together with the "stability" axiom (
X5
), implies that the characteristics in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
resemble
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadrics in large neighbourhoods of points of the form
f_{2}(w)
, where
w\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(\ell)
is a corner of
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
.

Fix
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Let

w\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(\ell_{\downarrow})

be a corner point such that
y\in B(f_{2}(w),K2^{-n/2})
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
be any rectangle with corner
w
, and let
q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric. We require that

(X4)

\begin{cases}|\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)-q_{Q,\ell}(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n},\\
|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{q}_{Q,\ell}(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n/2},\\
|\ddot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\ddot{q}_{Q,\ell}(y)|=o(\epsilon).\end{cases}

The
o(\epsilon)
-notation here means the following: we write
|g(y)-h(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{const}
if for all
\bar{\epsilon}>0
, there exists
\epsilon>0
, depending only on
K,M,\Sigma
, such that
|g(y)-h(y)|\leq\bar{\epsilon}\cdot\mathrm{const}
. The "triple control" in (
X4
) is such a common notion in the proof below that we give it a separate abbreviation: for
g,h\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
g(y)\approx_{n}h(y)
if

(8.5)

\begin{cases}|g(y)-h(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n},\\
|\dot{g}(y)-\dot{h}(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n/2},\\
|\ddot{g}(y)-\ddot{h}(y)|=o(\epsilon).\end{cases}

Thus, (
X4
) is abbreviated to
\tau_{n}(y)\approx_{n}q_{Q,\ell}(y)
, for appropriate
Q,\ell,y
.

Characteristics of generation
n-1
are generally not characteristics of generation
n
, for
n\geq 1
, but this is almost true: the final axiom states that

(X5)

|\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)-\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell)(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}

for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
and all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 8.6
.

The appearance of
\lceil n/2\rceil
in axiom (
X4
) may appear arbitrary and awkward, but there is a reason for the numerology: for
n\geq 0
, we, informally speaking, want
\tau_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\to C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to keep records of all the data of the tree
\mathcal{T}
down to a certain depth
d(n)
. We choose
d(n)
to be the
smallest integer such that that all the lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
contain corner points of rectangles in
\mathcal{D}_{d(n)}
. This means that we have to take
d(n):=\lceil n/2\rceil
; see Figure
2
for an illustration of the case
n=2
. Note, for example, that the horizontal line
\ell
in the middle lies in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, and the smallest generation of rectangles with corners on
\ell
is
\lceil 1/2\rceil=1
. Most likely any fixed choice
d(n)\sim n/2
would work equally well!

\begin{overpic}[Image5.pdf]
\put(8.0,1.0){$(0,0)$}
\put(72.0,1.0){$(1,0)$}
\put(8.0,80.0){$(0,1)$}
\put(72.0,80.0){$(1,1)$}
\put(84.0,9.0){$n=0$}
\put(84.0,72.0){$n=0$}
\put(84.0,41.0){$n=1$}
\put(84.0,25.0){$n=2$}
\put(84.0,57.0){$n=2$}
\put(5.0,41.0){$\ell$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2.
The points in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
depicted are in red, and the points in
\mathcal{C}_{1}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{C}_{0}
are depicted in orange. The generations of the horizontal lines are indicated with "
n=j
" for
j\in\{0,1,2\}
.

8.1.
From Proposition
8.3
to Proposition
7.3

Let us take the maps
\tau_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\to C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for granted, and use them to complete the proof of Proposition
7.3
. We start by quantifying how the maps
\tau_{n}
"form a Cauchy sequence in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
". We claim that if
N>n\geq 0
,
\ell_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and
\ell_{N}\in\mathcal{L}_{N}
satisfy
|\ell_{n}-\ell_{N}|\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
, then

(8.7)

\|\tau_{n}(\ell_{n})-\tau_{N}(\ell_{N})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\max\{\delta,\theta\}\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})-\dot{\tau}_{N}(\ell_{N})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

To see this, choose a sequence
\ell_{n+1},\ldots,\ell_{N}
of horizontal lines such that
\ell_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{j}
, and
\ell_{j-1}\in\{\ell_{j},\ell_{j}^{\uparrow},\ell_{j\downarrow}\}
for all
j\in\{n+2,\ldots,N\}
. Then
|\ell_{n+1}-\ell_{N}|\leq 2^{-n}
, so
|\ell_{n}-\ell_{n+1}|\leq 101\cdot 2^{-n}
. It follows that
\ell_{n}
is
\leq 101
steps away in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
from
\ell_{n+1}
(if
\ell_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
) or one of the
\mathcal{L}_{n+1}
-neighbours of
\ell_{n+1}
(if
\ell_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n+1}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n}
). For example, if
\ell_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, then

\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})-\dot{\tau}_{N}(\ell_{N})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n+1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}+\sum_{j=n+2}^{N}\|\dot{\tau}_{j}(\ell_{j})-\dot{\tau}_{j-1}(\ell_{j-1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}.

The first term can be estimated by
\leq 101
applications of axiom (
X3
), and the second term can be estimated using both axioms (
X3
) and (
X5
). After summing a geometric series, the result is
\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})-\dot{\tau}_{N}(\ell_{N})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}
. The other cases, and the first inequality in (
8.7
), are proven such a similar manner (also using axiom (
X2
)) that we omit the details.

We now construct the map
\tau\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and choose a sequence of integers
\{k_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
|t-k_{n}\cdot 2^{-n}|\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, writing
\ell_{n}:=\mathbb{R}\times\{k_{n}\cdot 2^{-n}\}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, the inequalities (
8.7
) imply that
\{\tau_{n}(\ell_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Hence the limit

\tau(\cdot,t):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}(\ell_{n})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})

exists in the
C^{1}
-norm, and

\dot{\tau}(y,t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})(y),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

In particular, we see that
y\mapsto\dot{\tau}(y,t)=\partial_{y}\tau(y,t)
is
2\Sigma
-Lipschitz by axiom (
X1
), which gives property (
\tau_{1}
). We remark that, as a corollary of (
8.7
), we have

(8.8)

\|\tau_{n}(\ell)-\tau(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\max\{\delta,\theta\}\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)-\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}

whenever
n\geq 0
,
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
|\pi_{2}(\ell)-t|\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
. This implies that the definition of
\tau(\cdot,t)
does not depend on the choice of the sequence
\{k_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, as long as
|t-k_{n}\cdot 2^{-n}|\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This leads to an observation, which will strengthen first inequality in (
8.8
). Namely, if
t=\pi_{2}(\ell)
for some
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, i.e.
t
is a dyadic rational, then
\tau_{m}(\ell)
is defined for all
m\geq n
, and
\tau(\cdot,t)
can be expressed as
\tau(\cdot,t)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\tau_{m}(\ell)
. It follows from (
X5
) that

(8.9)

\|\tau_{n}(\ell)-\tau(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sum_{m\geq n}\|\tau_{m+1}(\ell)-\tau_{m}(\ell)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-n}.

We then turn to the task of verifying properties (
\tau_{2}
)-(
\tau_{4}
) for
\tau
. The separation property (
\tau_{2}
) follows immediately from axiom (
X2
):

\tau(y,t_{2})-\tau(y,t_{1})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}(\ell^{1}_{n})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell^{2}_{n})(y)\in[\theta(t_{2}-t_{1}),\theta^{-1}(t_{2}-t_{1})],

where
\{\ell_{n}^{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\{\ell_{n}^{2}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are sequences of horizontal lines with
|\pi_{2}(\ell_{n}^{1})-t_{1}|\leq 2^{-n}
and
|\pi_{2}(\ell_{n}^{2})-t_{2}|\leq 2^{-n}
. The Hölder continuity property (
\tau_{3}
) follows easily from (
8.8
) if we choose
\delta\leq c\eta
for a sufficiently small absolute constant
c>0
. To see this, let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. The case
|t_{1}-t_{2}|\geq 1
is a little special: then one needs to check from the case
n=0
of the construction of
\tau_{n}
that the characteristics
\tau_{0}(\ell)
,
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
, only differ by a constant, hence
\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{1})\equiv\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{2})
for
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
. This will indeed be so, see (
9.1
)-(
9.2
), and the discussion below (
9.3
). Hence, if
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
satisfy
|\pi_{2}(\ell_{j})-t_{j}|\leq 1
, we have

\|\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{2})-\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{1})-\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\eta\sqrt{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}.

by (
8.8
). Consider then the case
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<1
, and pick
n\geq 0
such that
2^{-(n+1)}<|t_{1}-t_{2}|<2^{-n}
. Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
be lines with
|\pi_{2}(\ell_{j})-t_{j}|\leq 2^{-n}
. Then
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are neighbours, or perhaps two steps away, in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and hence axiom (
X3
) implies

\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}\lesssim\delta\cdot\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}.

It then follows from (
8.8
), and the triangle inequality, that
\|\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{2})-\dot{\tau}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\eta\sqrt{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}
. The proof of property (
\tau_{3}
) is complete.

Finally, to verify property (
\tau_{4}
), let
Q\in\mathcal{T}
. More precisely, let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
, so
\ell(Q)=2^{-m}
. Let
w=(x,t)\in\mathcal{C}(Q)
be a corner, and let
\ell=\pi_{2}^{-1}\{t\}\subset\mathbb{W}
be the unique horizontal line containing
w
. Since the horizontal edges of all rectangles in
\mathcal{D}_{m}
are contained on lines in
\mathcal{L}_{2m}
, we have
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
w\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(\ell)
with
n:=2m
. Next, let
q=q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric, and fix

y\in B(f_{2}(w),K\ell(Q))=B(f_{2}(w),K2^{-n/2}).

According to axiom (
X4
), we have

(8.10)

|\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)-q(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{q}(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

Choosing
\delta\leq c\eta
as before,
\epsilon>0
small depending on
\eta,K,M,\Sigma
, and combining (
8.8
)-(
8.9
) with (
8.10
), we find

|\tau(y,t)-q(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}(y,t)-\dot{q}(y)|\leq\eta\cdot\ell(Q),

as desired in property (
\tau_{4}
). This completes the proof of Proposition
7.3
, modulo the proof of Proposition
8.3
.

9.
Constructing the characteristics

In this section, we prove Proposition
8.3
.

9.1.
Case
n=0

The construction of the characteristics in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is inductive, and incorporates information about the characteristics already constructed in
\mathcal{X}_{n-1}
. The only exception is the case
n=0
, where we now begin; then
\lceil n/2\rceil=0
, and

\mathcal{L}_{0}=\{\mathbb{R}\times\{k\}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}=:\{\ell_{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Let
Q_{0}:=[0,1)^{2}
be the only rectangle in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. For
k\in\{-1,0,1,2\}
, let
q_{k}:=q_{Q_{0},\ell_{k}}
be the
(Q_{0},\ell_{k})
-approximate quadric. For the same indices
k\in\{-1,0,1,2\}
, we also define

(9.1)

\tau_{0}(\ell_{k})(y):=q_{k}(y).

For the indices
k\notin\{-1,0,1,2\}
, the definition of
\tau_{0}(\ell_{k})
is a bit arbitrary: let us set

(9.2)

\tau_{0}(\ell_{k}):=\tau_{0}(\ell_{0})(y)+C_{M,\Sigma}k,\qquad k\notin\{-1,0,1,2\},

where
C_{M,\Sigma}\sim N^{1/2}
is a constant to be determined shortly.

This completes the definition of
\tau_{0}\colon\mathcal{L}_{0}\to C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})
, and now we need to check the axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
). Axiom (
X1
) is easy: since
\mathcal{T}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical tree, every
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric
q_{Q,\ell}
with
Q\in\mathcal{T}
satisfies
\|\ddot{q}_{Q,\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\Sigma
. In particular,

(9.3)

\|\ddot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{k})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\Sigma,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

which is even better than (
X1
). Axiom (
X2
) requires some work, so we postpone it for a moment. Axiom (
X3
) is a triviality: the quadrics
q_{k}
are associated to the same rectangle
Q_{0}
, hence only differ by a constant (the graphs of every
q_{k}
arise as
\Pi
-projections of certain horizontal in the vertical plane
\mathbb{V}_{Q_{0}}
). Hence
\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{k})\equiv\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{k+1})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

There is very little to verify in axiom (
X4
), but let us do this over-formally to get some familiarity with the axiom. Let
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
and fix
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that there exists a rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
with corner
w\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\ell_{\downarrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{0}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{0}(\ell^{\uparrow})
such that
y\in B(f_{2}(w),K)
. The claim is that
\tau_{0}(\ell)(y)\approx_{0}q_{Q,\ell}(y)
, where
q_{Q,\ell}
is the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric.

Since
Q_{0}
is the only rectangle in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
, we have
Q=Q_{0}
, and
\ell\in\{\ell_{-1},\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
. For such lines, in (
9.1
), we defined
\tau_{0}(\ell)
to be the
(Q_{0},\ell)
-approximate quadric. Hence
\tau_{0}(\ell):=q_{Q_{0},\ell}\approx_{0}q_{Q_{0},\ell}
, and the verification of axiom (
X4
) is complete.

Axiom (
X5
) is meaningless for the time being, since it compares
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
with
\tau_{n}(\ell)
for
n\geq 1
. So, we finally turn to axiom (
X2
). We first claim that for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,

(9.4)

\tau_{0}(\ell_{k})(y)-\tau_{0}(\ell_{k-1})(y)=q_{k}(y)-q_{k-1}(y)\in[cN^{-1/2},CN^{1/2}],\qquad y\in\mathbb{R},

where
0<c\leq C<\infty
are absolute constants. To see this, we recall that the signature of
\mathcal{T}
, and hence
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{T}
is
+
, which meant that the
(N,\epsilon)
-QIE

F:=F_{Q_{0}}:=\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q_{0}}}\circ\iota_{Q_{0}}=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q_{0}}}\circ\Pi\circ\iota_{Q_{0}}\colon HQ_{0}\to\mathbb{W}

is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}
-increasing on the rectangle
BQ_{0}\subset HQ_{0}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}=2N\epsilon
. This fact was established in Lemma
4.45
, assuming that
H\geq A(1+\Sigma)^{2}MB
. The quadric straightening map
\psi_{0}:=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{Q_{0}}}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{W}
appearing in the definition of
F
was introduced just above (
4.31
). It had the form

\psi_{0}(y,t)=(y,t-\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}-by),

where
a=a_{Q_{0}}
and
b=b_{Q_{0}}
are the common coefficients of approximate
(Q_{0},\ell)
-quadrics, all of which have the form
q_{\ell}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c_{\ell}
. In particular, the quadrics
q_{-1},q_{0},q_{1},q_{2}
can be written in this general form for certain coefficients
c_{j}:=c_{\ell_{j}}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q_{k}-q_{k-1}\equiv c_{k}-c_{k-1}
. So, proving (
9.4
) is equivalent to showing that
c_{k}-c_{k-1}\sim_{N}1
. For this purpose, we define
w_{k}:=(0,k)\in\mathbb{W}
. Since
F
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}
-increasing on
BQ_{0}
, and
B\geq 5
, in particular the map
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(0,t))
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-increasing on
[k-1,k]
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
. Since
\|\pi_{2}(w_{k})-\pi_{2}(w_{k-1})\|>\epsilon^{\prime}
(recall from (
4.43
) that
\epsilon\ll N^{-10}
), and
(\pi_{2}\circ F)|_{\ell}\equiv c_{\ell}
by the expression (
4.35
), this implies

c_{k}=\pi_{2}(F(w_{k}))>\pi_{2}(F(w_{k-1}))>c_{k-1},\qquad k\in\{0,1,2\}.

To establish the more quantitative estimate needed for (
9.4
), we use Lemma
3.30
, which implies that the map
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(0,t))
is an
(N,2\epsilon)
-QIE on
([k-1,k],\|\cdot\|)
(for applying Lemma
3.30
, we also use that
F
is a QIE on
HQ_{0}
, a large neighbourhood of
[-1,2]^{2}
). Hence, for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,

\displaystyle(2N)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}-2\epsilon\leq\|\pi_{2}(F(w_{k}))-\pi_{2}(F(w_{k-1}))\|=\|c_{k}-c_{k-1}\|\leq N+2\epsilon\leq 2N.

This completes the proof of (
9.4
). To complete the proof of axiom (
X2
), we recall the definition

\tau_{0}(\ell_{k})(y):=\tau_{0}(\ell_{0})(y)+C_{M,\Sigma}k=q_{0}(y)+C_{M,\Sigma}k,\qquad k\notin\{-1,0,1,2\}.

We see from (
9.4
), and recalling that all the quadrics
q_{k}
only differ by a constant, that axiom (
X2
) is satisfied if
C_{M,\Sigma}\sim N^{1/2}
is chosen sufficiently large. This completes the proof of Proposition
8.3
in the case
n=0
.

9.2.
Case
n\geq 1

We next assume that the map
\tau_{n-1}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\to C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})
has already been constructed for some
n\geq 1
, and satisfies axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
). We now describe the construction of
\tau_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}_{n}\to C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})
. Fix
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The construction is a little different in the cases where also
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
(so
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
is defined), and where
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. The constructions can, however, be carried out simultaneously.

Write
m:=\lceil n/2\rceil
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{+}(\ell):=\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
. We would like to define

\mathcal{I}(\ell):=\{I_{n}(w):w\in\mathcal{C}^{+}_{m}(\ell)\},\quad\text{where}\quad I_{n}(w):=\bar{B}(f_{2}(w),K2^{-n/2}),

but the intervals
I_{n}(w)
,
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}^{+}(\ell)
, may have plenty of overlap, which leads to technical issues. To avoid these, we prune
\mathcal{I}(\ell)
as follows. Pick
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
arbitrarily and see if it is covered by the intervals in
\mathcal{I}(\ell)\,\setminus\,\{I\}
. If it is, discard it; otherwise keep it. The process terminates in
\leq\operatorname{card}\mathcal{I}(\ell)<\infty
steps. The union of the intervals never changed during the process, and the remaining intervals have overlap bounded by
2
: if three intervals of the same length meet at a common point, then one is covered by the two others.

We keep the notation
\mathcal{I}(\ell)
for the sub-collection obtained above. The key facts are these:

(9.5)

\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}^{+}(\ell)}I_{n}(w)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}I\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\mathbf{1}_{I}\leq 2.

From the second property in (
9.5
), we infer that also

(9.6)

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\mathbf{1}_{2I}\leq 4,

because if
y\in 2I_{1}\cap\cdots\cap 2I_{5}
, then
I_{j}\subset[x-|I|,x+|I|]
for all
1\leq j\leq 5
, hence
5|I|\leq\int_{x-|I|}^{x+|I|}\sum\mathbf{1}_{I}\leq 4|I|
by (
9.5
), a contradiction.

With (
9.5
)-(
9.6
) in hand, we construct a partition of unity for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
fixed. Define the (possibly empty) closed sets

(9.7)

U(\ell):=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}I=\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)}I_{n}(w)\quad\text{and}\quad U^{\sharp}(\ell):=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\kappa_{0}I=\bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)}\kappa_{0}I_{n}(w),

where
\kappa_{0}\in(1,\tfrac{4}{5}\sqrt{2})
is an arbitrary fixed constant; let us for concreteness set

\kappa_{0}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\tfrac{4}{5}\sqrt{2}).

The last set equation in (
9.7
) requires a little argument, since the selection of the intervals in
\mathcal{I}(\ell)
had, in principle, nothing to do with the enlargements
\kappa_{0}I
. Fortunately, the same selection process simultaneously yields the last equation in (
9.7
). Indeed, if some interval
\kappa_{0}I_{n}(w)
,
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)
, were not covered by the intervals
\kappa_{0}I
with
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
, then in particular
I_{n}(w)\notin\mathcal{I}(\ell)
. Hence
I_{n}(w)\subset I_{1}\cup\ldots\cup I_{k}
for certain intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
, and it is easy to check that consequently
\kappa_{0}I_{n}(w)\subset\kappa_{0}I_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\kappa_{0}I_{k}
.

Let
\{\varphi_{I,\ell}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a family of functions satisfying the following properties:

\mathbf{1}_{U(\ell)}\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}\leq\mathbf{1}_{U^{\sharp}(\ell)}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}\subset\kappa_{0}I\text{ for all }I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell).

Define also

\varphi_{\ell}=1-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell},\quad\text{hence}\quad\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,U^{\sharp}(\ell)}\leq\varphi_{\ell}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,U(\ell)}.

Since
\kappa_{0}\leq 2
, the overlap condition (
9.6
) holds, and
|I|=2K2^{-n/2}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
, the functions
\varphi_{I,\ell}
and
\varphi_{\ell}
can be easily chosen to satisfy the following bounds for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
:

(9.8)

|\dot{\varphi}_{\ell}(y)|+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}|\dot{\varphi}_{I,\ell}(y)|\lesssim\tfrac{1}{K}\cdot 2^{n/2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\ddot{\varphi}_{\ell}(y)|+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}|\ddot{\varphi}_{I,\ell}(y)|\lesssim\tfrac{1}{K^{2}}\cdot 2^{n}.

Before defining
\tau_{n}(\ell)
, we associate a quadric to each interval
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
. Recall that every interval
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
has the form
I=I_{n}(w)
for some
w_{I}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
. Let
Q_{I}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be any rectangle with corner
w_{I}
, and let

q_{I,\ell}:=q_{Q_{I},\ell}

be the
(Q_{I},\ell)
-approximate quadric. There may be several (up to
4
) admissible choices of
Q_{I}
, and they might produce different quadrics
q_{I,\ell}
. However, we will see in Proposition
9.16
below that the different quadrics nearly agree, so the particular choice does not matter. We are now prepared to define
\tau_{n}(\ell)
. If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, set

(9.9)

\tau_{n}(\ell):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}\cdot q_{I,\ell}+\varphi_{\ell}\cdot\tau_{n-1}(\ell).

If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, set instead

(9.10)

\tau_{n}(\ell):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}\cdot q_{I,\ell}+\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi_{\ell}\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})+\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})],

observing that
\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
.

9.2.1.
Preparatory lemmas

Before beginning to verify axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
), we prove a few lemmas, which will be needed in the verification of multiple axioms. In these lemmas, the definitions of, and relationships between, the constants
\epsilon,H,K,M,N,\Sigma
are as stated before. We recall from (
8.2
) that
\epsilon\ll\{M,\Sigma\}\ll K\ll H
.

The first lemma uses nothing but the
M
-Lipschitz property of
f
, hence
f_{2}
.

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{W}
with
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n/2}
. Then, assuming that
K\geq 100AM
for
A>(\tfrac{4}{5}\sqrt{2}-\kappa_{0})^{-1}
, we have

B(f_{2}(w_{2}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\tfrac{4}{5}K2^{-(n-1)/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-(n-1)/2}).

Proof.

Since
f_{2}
is
M
-Lipschitz, and
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n/2}
, we have

B(f_{2}(w_{2}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2}+100\cdot M2^{-n/2}).

Since
K\geq 100AM
, and
\kappa_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\tfrac{4}{5}\sqrt{2})
, here
\kappa_{0}K+100M\leq(\kappa_{0}+A^{-1})K<\tfrac{4}{5}\sqrt{2}\cdot K
, hence

B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2}+M2^{-(n-1)/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\tfrac{4}{5}K2^{-(n-1)/2}).

The second inclusion simply follows from
\tfrac{4}{5}\leq\kappa_{0}
. ∎

The next lemma implies that the "
\approx_{n}
"-notation introduced in (
8.5
) interacts well with partitions of unity. For technical purposes, we record a slightly more quantitative result:

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
\{\varphi_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\subset C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be a family of functions satisfying

(9.13)

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\varphi_{I}(y_{0})=1,\quad\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}|\dot{\varphi}_{I}(y_{0})|\leq c_{1}\cdot 2^{n/2},\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}|\ddot{\varphi}_{I}(y_{0})|\leq c_{2}\cdot 2^{n}

for some fixed
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\{p_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\cup\{q_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\subset C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be further families of functions satisfying

|p_{I}(y_{0})-q_{I}(y_{0})|\leq\epsilon_{1}\cdot 2^{-n},\quad|\dot{p}_{I}(y_{0})-\dot{q}_{I}(y_{0})|\leq\epsilon_{2}\cdot 2^{-n/2},\quad\text{and}\quad|\ddot{p}_{I}(y_{0})-\ddot{q}_{I}(y_{0})|\leq\epsilon_{3}

for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
with
y_{0}\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I}
. Here
c_{1},c_{2},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}>0
are constants. Then, writing
Q_{p}:=\sum\varphi_{I}p_{I}
and
Q_{q}:=\sum\varphi_{I}q_{I}
, we have

(9.14)

\begin{cases}|Q_{p}(y_{0})-Q_{q}(y_{0})|\leq\epsilon_{1}\cdot 2^{-n},\\
|\dot{Q}_{p}(y_{0})-\dot{Q}_{q}(y_{0})|\leq(c_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})\cdot 2^{-n/2},\\
|\ddot{Q}_{p}(y_{0})-\ddot{Q}_{q}(y_{0})|\leq c_{2}\epsilon_{1}+2c_{1}\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Write
Q_{p}(y_{0})-Q_{q}(y_{0})=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\varphi_{I}(y_{0})\cdot[p_{I}(y_{0})-q_{I}(y_{0})]
, and note that the sum can be restricted to those
I\in\mathcal{I}
with
y_{0}\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I}
. Then (
9.14
) follows by direct computation. ∎

Quite often the following corollary is enough:

Corollary 9.15
.

Let
\{\varphi_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
be a partition of unity satisfying (
9.13
) with
c_{1},c_{2}\lesssim 1
. Let
\{q_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\subset C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Assume that, for some
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, there exists a function
q=q_{y_{0}}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

q_{I}(y_{0})\approx_{n}q(y_{0})\quad\text{for all}\quad I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)\quad\text{with}\quad y_{0}\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}.

Then,
q(y_{0})\approx_{n}Q(y_{0})
, where
Q=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\varphi_{I}q_{I}
.

The next proposition gives a condition for checking that the hypothesis of Corollary
9.15
is valid. The notation
q_{Q,\ell}
will refer to the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric associated with an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition 9.16
.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
be corners of
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
. Assume that
w_{1}\in\ell_{1}
and
w_{2}\in\ell_{2}
, where
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}
are horizontal lines with
|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
. Then, if
\ell\subset\mathbb{W}
is an additional horizontal line with
|\ell-\ell_{1}|\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
, we have

(9.17)

q_{Q_{1},\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q_{Q_{2},\ell}(y),\qquad y\in B(f_{2}(w_{1}),100K2^{-n/2})\cap B(f_{2}(w_{2}),100K2^{-n/2}).

Proof.

Write
m:=\lceil n/2\rceil
. Then
2^{-m}\leq\ell(Q_{j})\leq 2^{-m+1}
for both
j\in\{1,2\}
. If the intersection in (
9.17
) is empty, there is nothing to prove. In the opposite case, we have

(9.18)

|f_{2}(w_{1})-f_{2}(w_{2})|\leq 200K\cdot 2^{-m}.

Based on this, we claim that

(9.19)

d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\lesssim\max\{KN,N^{2}\}\cdot 2^{-m}.

Writing
w_{j}:=(y_{j},t_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, the hypothesis of the lemma implies that
\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|\lesssim 2^{-m}
. Therefore, if

(9.20)

|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\max\{\tfrac{1}{100}\cdot 2^{-m},4N^{2}\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|\},

the estimate (
9.19
) follows immediately. In the opposite case, we rely on Proposition
4.52
, which we recall here for the reader’s convenience (with terminology slightly adapted to the present situation):

Proposition 9.21
.

Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
,
and let
w_{j}=(y_{j},t_{j})\in\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, with

|y_{1}-y_{2}|>\max\{\tfrac{1}{100}\cdot\min\{\ell(Q_{1}),\ell(Q_{2})\},4N^{2}\|t_{1}-t_{2}\|\}.

Then,

|f_{2}(w_{2})-f_{2}(w_{1})|\geq\tfrac{1}{2N}d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2}).

With the proposition in hand, recalling (
9.18
), and assuming that (
9.20
) fails, we estimate

d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq 2N|f_{2}(w_{1})-f_{2}(w_{2})|\lesssim KN2^{-m},

and (
9.19
) follows. The main consequence of (
9.19
) is that
\operatorname{dist}_{\mathrm{par}}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\leq d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},w_{2})\lesssim\max\{KN,N^{2}\}\ell(Q_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, recalling
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
. Therefore, if
H\gg\max\{KN,N^{2}\}
is sufficiently large, then
\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{1}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{2}\cap\ell\neq\emptyset
, and we may even pick two "horizontally aligned" points

v_{1},v_{2}\in\ell\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{1}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{2}\quad\text{with}\quad d_{\mathrm{par}}(v_{1},v_{2})=2^{-m}.

These points will help us to show that the quadrics
q_{1}:=q_{Q_{1},\ell}
and
q_{2}:=q_{Q_{2},\ell}
are almost the same, which is what we claimed. To make this precise, we will appeal to the following
quadric comparison lemma
, whose proof will be postponed to Appendix
A
:

Lemma 9.22

(Quadric comparison lemma)
.

Let
q\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadric, and let
I=[a_{1},a_{2}]\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval. Write

m:=\max\{|q(a_{1})|,|q(a_{2})|\}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{m}:=\max\{|\dot{q}(a_{1})|,|\dot{q}(a_{2})|\}.

Then, for all
r\geq 1
and
t\in rI
,

(9.23)

|q(t)|\lesssim(m+|I|\dot{m})r^{2},\quad|\dot{q}(t)|\lesssim(\dot{m}+\tfrac{m}{|I|})r,\quad\text{and}\quad|\ddot{q}(t)|\lesssim\tfrac{m}{|I|^{2}}+\tfrac{\dot{m}}{|I|}.

We aim to apply the lemma to the quadric
q:=q_{1}-q_{2}
on the interval
I:=[a_{1},a_{2}]:=[f_{2}(v_{1}),f_{2}(v_{2})]
(or
I=[f_{2}(v_{2}),f_{2}(v_{1})]
), where
v_{1},v_{2}\in\ell\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{1}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{2}
were chosen above. To this end, we need to have upper and lower bounds for
|I|
, plus estimates for
|q(a_{j})|,|\dot{q}(a_{j})|
,
j\in\{1,2\}
. Let us start by estimating
|I|
. Since
v_{1},v_{2}\in\ell\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{1}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}HQ_{2}
, and
d_{\mathrm{par}}(v_{1},v_{2})=\ell(Q_{1})=\ell(Q_{2})
, Proposition
9.21
can be applied, once more:

|I|=|f_{2}(v_{2})-f_{2}(v_{1})|\geq\tfrac{1}{2N}d_{\mathrm{par}}(v_{1},v_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2N}\cdot 2^{-m}.

Evidently
|I|\leq M2^{-m}
, since
f_{2}
is
M
-Lipschitz.

To estimate
|q(a_{j})|,|\dot{q}(a_{j})|
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, we observe that
v_{1},v_{2}\in\ell\cap HQ_{1}\cap HQ_{2}
, and then apply Corollary
4.40
to both quadrics
q_{i}
at both points
a_{j}=f_{2}(v_{j})
:

|f_{t}(v_{j})-q_{i}(a_{j})|\lesssim_{\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad|f_{x}(v_{j})-\dot{q}_{i}(a_{j})|\lesssim_{\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-m},\qquad i,j\in\{1,2\}.

Using the triangle inequality, and recalling that
q=q_{1}-q_{2}
, we infer

m:=\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}|q(a_{j})|\lesssim_{\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{m}:=\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}|q(a_{j})|\lesssim_{\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-m}.

Now, combining these bounds with the length estimate
|I|\sim_{N}2^{-m}\sim 2^{-n/2}
, we may apply the quadric comparison lemma: for any
r\geq 1
and
t\in rI
,

(9.24)

\begin{cases}|q_{1}(t)-q_{2}(t)|\lesssim_{r,N,\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n},\\
|\dot{q}_{1}(t)-\dot{q}_{2}(t)|\lesssim_{r,N,\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-m}\\
|\ddot{q}_{1}(t)-\ddot{q}_{2}(t)|\lesssim_{N,\Sigma}\epsilon.\end{cases}

The bound for the second derivatives is naturally independent or
r
, since the second derivative of a quadric is constant. As for the first two estimates, the point "
t
" that we are really interested in is the point
y\in B(f_{2}(w_{1}),100K2^{-n/2})\cap B(f_{2}(w_{2}),100K2^{-n/2})
given by (
9.17
). Since
d_{\mathrm{par}}(v_{j},w_{j})\lesssim H2^{-m}
, it follows from the
M
-Lipschitz property of
f_{2}
that

\operatorname{dist}(y,I)\leq|y-f_{2}(w_{1})|+|f_{2}(v_{1})-f_{2}(w_{1})|\lesssim(K+HM)2^{-m}.

Since
|I|\sim_{N}2^{-m}
, and
M\leq N
, we have
y\in rI
for some
r\sim_{K,N}1
, and it finally follows from (
9.24
) that

|q_{1}(y)-q_{2}(y)|\lesssim_{K,N,\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{q}_{1}(y)-\dot{q}_{2}(y)|\lesssim_{K,N,\Sigma}\epsilon\cdot 2^{-m}.

The correct bound for
|\ddot{q}_{1}(y)-\ddot{q}_{2}(y)|
already follows from (
9.24
). Since
N\sim_{M,\Sigma}1
, this completes the proof of the proposition. ∎

The final result in this section is virtually a restatement of Corollary
3.5
;

Corollary 9.25
.

Let
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ell_{3}<\ell_{4}<\ell_{5}
be consecutive lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, such that
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Assume that one of the lines
\ell_{j}
contains the corner "
w
" of a rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
. Then, the dyadic parent of
Q
has a corner
\widehat{w}\in\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{4}
which lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
.

Proof.

Only the last statement was not part of Corollary
3.5
. If
n
is odd, then
\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil=\lceil n/2\rceil-1
, and the corners of the dyadic parent
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}
of
Q
lie in
\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
by definition (here we used
n\geq 1
, so the dyadic parent of
Q
still lies in
\mathcal{T}
). Moreover, by the coherence axiom (
T3
), all the children of
\hat{Q}
lie in
\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
, and consequently the corners of
\hat{Q}
are corners of some rectangles in
\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
.

If
n
is even, then
\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil=\lceil n/2\rceil
, hence the siblings of
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
lie simultaneously in
\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
. In particular, the corners of
\hat{Q}
lie in
\mathcal{C}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
. ∎

Lemma 9.26
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
. Then at least one of the horizontal edges of
Q
is contained on a line in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
.

Proof.

The height of the rectangle
Q
is
4^{-\lceil n/2\rceil}\in\{2^{-n},2^{-n-1}\}
, depending on the parity of
n
. If the height is
2^{-n}
, then both horizontal edges of
Q
are contained on lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. If the height is
2^{-n-1}
, then both horizontal edges of
Q
are contained on lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n+1}
, and one observes that every second line in
\mathcal{L}_{n+1}
is contained in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. ∎

We then begin to verify the axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
) in order. While verifying axiom (Xi) for
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
and
\tau_{n}
, we may inductively use all of the axioms (Xj) for
\tau_{n-1}
. Moreover, to verify axioms (
X1
) for
\tau_{n}
, we will use axioms (
X3
) and (
X5
) for
\tau_{n}
. However, the proofs of axioms (
X3
),(
X5
) for
\tau_{n}
will not use axiom (
X1
) for
\tau_{n}
, so there is no circular reasoning.

9.2.2.
Verifying axiom (
X1
)

Fix
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The claim is, then, that

(9.27)

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{1}-y_{2}|,\qquad y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R},

and we assume inductively that this holds for
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
,
\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})
, and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})
. An easy case occurs if
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\subset\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,U^{\sharp}(\ell)
, so
\varphi_{\ell}\equiv 1
on a neighbourhood of
y_{1},y_{2}
(this is why we defined
U,U^{\sharp}
as closed sets). Then, depending on whether
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
or
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, we have either

\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})=\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell)(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell)(y_{2})

or

\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2}[\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y_{2})]+\tfrac{1}{2}[\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell^{\downarrow})(y_{1})-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell^{\downarrow})(y_{2})].

In both cases, (
9.27
) follows from induction and the triangle inequality.

Let us then assume that
y_{1}\in U^{\sharp}(\ell)
, for example. Then, it does not matter if
y_{2}\in U^{\sharp}(\ell)
or
y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,U^{\sharp}(\ell)
, and the verification of (
9.27
) will not use the inductive assumption (
X1
). Since
y_{1}\in U^{\sharp}(\ell)
, we have

(9.28)

y_{1}\in\kappa_{0}I_{n}(w)=B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})

for some
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
. This means that
w\in\ell\cup\ell^{\uparrow}\cup\ell_{\downarrow}
and
w\in\mathcal{C}(Q)
for some
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
. There are three different cases to consider:

(9.29)

|y_{1}-y_{2}|\geq\tfrac{1}{10}K\quad\text{or}\quad\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}<|y_{1}-y_{2}|<\tfrac{1}{10}K\quad\text{or}\quad|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

We handle these cases in order. First, assume
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\geq\tfrac{1}{10}K
. Let
\ell=:\ell_{n}\leq\ell_{n-1}\leq\ldots\leq\ell_{0}
be a sequence of horizontal lines such that
\ell_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}
and
\ell_{k+1}\in\{\ell_{k},\ell_{k}^{\uparrow}\}
. Then, by axioms (
X3
) and (
X5
) for
\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})(y_{j})-\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{0})(y_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|\dot{\tau}_{k+1}(\ell_{k+1})(y_{j})-\dot{\tau}_{k}(\ell_{k})(y_{j})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}2^{-k/2}\sim\delta\leq\frac{10\delta}{K}|y_{1}-y_{2}|,\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.

Since
K>1
in Proposition
8.3
is chosen small in a manner depending on both
\delta
and
\Sigma
, it follows that

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})|\leq|\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{0})(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell_{0})(y_{1})|+\tfrac{A\delta}{K}|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{2}-y_{1}|.

Here we also used the special definition of
\tau_{0}
, which implies, as seen in (
9.3
), that
\dot{\tau}_{0}(\ell)
is
\Sigma
-Lipschitz, not just
2\Sigma
-Lipschitz, for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
.

Recalling the case distinction (
9.29
), we next assume
\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}<|y_{1}-y_{2}|<\tfrac{1}{10}K
. Note that this forces
n\geq 2
. We then claim that there exists
0\leq\hat{n}\leq n-2
, and a rectangle
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}
with
\hat{Q}\supset Q
such that

(9.30)

|y_{1}-y_{2}|\geq 2^{-\hat{n}/2}\quad\text{and}\quad y_{1},y_{2}\in B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),K2^{-\hat{n}/2})

for some
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})\subset\mathcal{C}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}
. Let
n_{1}\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
be the greatest index with the property
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K2^{-n_{1}/2}
. Then
1\leq n_{1}<n
by our case assumption, so
\hat{n}:=n_{1}-1\in\{0,\ldots,n-2\}
, and

|y_{1}-y_{2}|>\tfrac{1}{5}K2^{-(n_{1}+1)/2}\geq 2^{-\hat{n}/2}.

So, the first assertion of (
9.30
) holds.

To prove the second assertion, let
Q_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n_{1}/2\rceil}
and
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}
be rectangles with
Q\subset Q_{1}\subset\hat{Q}
. Let
w_{1}\in\mathcal{C}(Q_{1})
be an arbitrary corner. We also choose a corner
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})
, but with some care: recall from Lemma
9.26
that one of the horizontal edges of
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}
lies on a line
\ell_{\hat{n}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}}
. We choose
\widehat{w}
to be one of the two corners of
\hat{Q}
on
\ell_{\hat{n}}
.

Certainly
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w_{1},\widehat{w})\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n_{1}/2}
. Then, (
9.28
) and Lemma
9.11
(applied with
w_{1}
and
w_{2}:=\widehat{w}
) first imply that

y_{1}\in B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{1}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n_{1}/2})\subset B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),\tfrac{4}{5}K2^{-\hat{n}/2}),

and next

|y_{2}-f_{2}(w_{1})|\leq|y_{1}-y_{2}|+\tfrac{4}{5}K2^{-\hat{n}/2}\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K2^{-\hat{n}/2}+\tfrac{4}{5}K2^{-\hat{n}/2}=K2^{-\hat{n}/2}.

This verifies (
9.30
) with
\widehat{w},\hat{Q}
, and
\hat{n}
, as above.

This information enables us to use the inductive assumption (
X4
) on the characteristics of generation
0\leq\hat{n}\leq n-2
. Let us construct a sequence of horizontal lines
\ell_{n},\ldots,\ell_{\hat{n}-1}
:

(a)

\ell_{n}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\}
is the line containing
w
, so
\ell_{n}
intersects the closures of
Q
and
\hat{Q}\supset Q
,

(b)

\ell_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}
for all
n\leq k\leq\hat{n}-1
, and
\ell_{k-1}\in\{\ell_{k},\ell_{k}^{\uparrow},\ell_{k\downarrow}\}
for
\hat{n}+2\leq k\leq n
(this notation refers to the
\mathcal{L}_{k}
-neighbours of
\ell_{k}
),

(c)

Every line
\ell_{k}
,
n\leq k\leq\hat{n}-1
, intersects the closure of
\hat{Q}
,

The line
\ell_{n}
is dictated by (a). Assume that
\ell_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}
has already been chosen for some
\hat{n}+2\leq k\leq n
. Then either
\ell_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k-1}
, and we simply set
\ell_{k-1}:=\ell_{k}
, or otherwise both
\mathcal{L}_{k}
-neighbours of
\ell_{k}
are in
\mathcal{L}_{k-1}
. Since the height of the rectangle
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}
is
4^{-\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}\in\{2^{-\hat{n}},2^{-(\hat{n}+1)}\}
, depending on the parity of
\hat{n}
, and the vertical separation of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
-neighbours is
2^{-k}\leq 2^{-\hat{n}+2}
, one of these neighbours meets the closure of
\hat{Q}
. So,
\ell_{k-1}\in\mathcal{L}_{k-1}
may be chosen as in (b) and (c) for
\hat{n}+2\leq k\leq n
.

We earlier defined
\ell_{\hat{n}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}}
to be a line containing the corner
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})
. According to (b)-(c), both
\ell_{\hat{n}+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}+1}
and
\ell_{\hat{n}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}+1}
intersect the closure of
\hat{Q}
. If the height of
\hat{Q}
is
2^{-\hat{n}+1}
, it follows that
\ell_{\hat{n}}\in\{\ell_{\hat{n}+1},\ell_{\hat{n}+1}^{\uparrow},\ell_{\hat{n}+1\downarrow}\}
. Otherwise, the height of
\hat{Q}
is
2^{-\hat{n}}
, and
\ell_{\hat{n}}
may be up to two steps away from
\ell_{\hat{n}+1}
in
\mathcal{L}_{\hat{n}+1}
. In both cases, axioms (
X3
)-(
X5
) yield

(9.31)

\|\dot{\tau}_{k}(\ell_{k})-\dot{\tau}_{k+1}(\ell_{k+1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\delta\cdot 2^{-k/2},\qquad\hat{n}\leq k\leq n-1.

From (
9.31
), we deduce that for both
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{j})-\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})(y_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=\hat{n}}^{n-1}|\dot{\tau}_{k+1}(\ell_{k+1})(y_{j})-\dot{\tau}_{k}(\ell_{k})(y_{j})|

(9.32)

\displaystyle\leq\delta\sum_{k=\hat{n}}^{n-1}2^{-k/2}\sim\delta\cdot 2^{-\hat{n}/2}\leq\delta|y_{1}-y_{2}|,

using the lower bound in (
9.30
) in the final inequality. Next, we estimate the difference
|\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})(y_{1})|
using the inclusion
y_{1},y_{2}\in B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),K2^{-\hat{n}/2})
from (
9.30
), and also
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil\hat{n}/2\rceil}(\ell_{\hat{n}})
. With these facts in hand, axiom (
X4
) lets us compare
\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})
to the
(\hat{Q},\ell_{\hat{n}})
-approximate quadric
q:=q_{\hat{Q},\ell_{\hat{n}}}
:

|\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{\hat{n}}(\ell_{\hat{n}})(y_{1})|\leq|\dot{q}(y_{2})-\dot{q}(y_{1})|+o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-\hat{n}/2}\leq|\dot{q}(y_{2})-\dot{q}(y_{1})|+o(\epsilon)\cdot|y_{1}-y_{2}|.

Since
\|\ddot{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Sigma
by the assumption that
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}
is an
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle, we combine the estimate above with (
9.32
) to deduce

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})(y_{1})|\leq\Sigma|y_{1}-y_{2}|+A(\delta+o(\epsilon))|y_{1}-y_{2}|,

where
A\geq 1
is an absolute constant. Finally, the same estimate holds for
|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})|
, since
\ell_{n}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\}
, and axiom (
X3
) implies
\|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{n})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}
. Choosing
\delta,\epsilon>0
small enough, depending on
\Sigma
, we see that
|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})|\leq 2\Sigma|y_{1}-y_{2}|
, as desired. This completes the proof of axiom (
X1
) in the case where
\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}<|y_{1}-y_{2}|<\tfrac{1}{10}K
.

Recalling the case distinction in (
9.29
), it remains to treat the case
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}
. Axioms (
X3
)-(
X5
) will not be used here. Recall from (
9.28
) that
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
is the corner of some rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
which satisfies
y_{1}\in B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})
. Here
m=\lceil n/2\rceil
, as always. We will next apply Corollary
9.25
to find a "parent" corner point
\widehat{w}
close to
w
. The details are slightly differently depending on whether
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
or
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
.

If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, let
\ell_{1}<\ldots<\ell_{5}
be the five consecutive lines of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\ell_{2}=\ell_{\downarrow}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
and
\ell_{4}=\ell^{\uparrow}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Then, recalling that
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
is the corner of
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
, Corollary
9.25
produces a corner
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
of the dyadic parent of
Q
which lies on
\ell^{\uparrow}\cup\ell_{\downarrow}
.

If, on the other hand,
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, then choose the five lines in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
so that
\ell\in\{\ell_{2},\ell_{4}\}
. Then
\ell=\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{4}
are consecutive in
\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, and Corollary
9.25
produces a corner
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
of the dyadic parent of
Q
which lies on
\ell\cup\ell_{4}
.

In both cases,
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w,\widehat{w})\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n/2}
, so Lemma
9.11
implies

y_{1}\in B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),\tfrac{4}{5}K\cdot 2^{-(n-1)/2}).

Consequently
|y_{2}-f_{2}(\widehat{w})|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}+\tfrac{4}{5}K\cdot 2^{-(n-1)/2}<K2^{-(n-1)/2}
, so in fact

(9.33)

[y_{1},y_{2}]\subset B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),K2^{-(n-1)/2}).

We are now in a position to apply the inductive hypothesis (
X4
) to the quadric
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
(in case
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
) or both quadrics
\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})
and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})
(in case
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
). We treat the cases separately for clarity. Let us first assume
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, so
\widehat{w}\in\ell\cup\ell_{4}
, where
\ell
and
\ell_{4}
are consecutive in
\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Let
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
be a rectangle with corner
\widehat{w}
. Let
q:=q_{\hat{Q},\ell}
be the
(\hat{Q},\ell)
-approximate quadric. Then, axiom (
X4
) applied inductively to
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
implies

(9.34)

\tau_{n-1}(y)\approx_{n-1}q(y),\qquad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].

Similar reasoning works if
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, so
\widehat{w}\in\ell^{\uparrow}\cup\ell_{\downarrow}
. Then, (
X4
) is applied inductively to both
\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})
and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})
, and to the
(\hat{Q},\ell^{\uparrow})
- and
(\hat{Q},\ell_{\downarrow})
-approximate quadrics
q^{\uparrow}
and
q_{\downarrow}
; the meaning of
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
is the same as before. The results are

(9.35)

\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y)\approx_{n-1}q^{\uparrow}(y)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})(y)\approx_{n-1}q_{\downarrow}(y),\qquad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].

To conclude the proof of axiom (
X1
), we will use (
9.34
)-(
9.35
), and additionally the following claim; in the two possible definitions

\begin{cases}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)q_{I,\ell}(y)+\varphi_{\ell}(y)\cdot\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y),\\
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)q_{I,\ell}(y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{\ell}(y)\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y)+\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})(y)]\end{cases}

of
\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)
, we have
q_{I,\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q(y)
for all
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]
and for all
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
such that
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}
. To accomplish this, we appeal to Proposition
9.16
. Note that if
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}
for some
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]
, then

y\in\kappa_{0}I_{n}(w_{I})=\bar{B}(f_{2}(w_{I}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{I}),100K2^{-n/2})

for some
w_{I}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})
, and
q_{I,\ell}=q_{Q_{I},\ell}
is the
(Q_{I},\ell)
-approximate quadric for some rectangle
Q_{I}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}
with corner
w_{I}
. On the other hand, from (
9.33
) we know that
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]\subset B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),100K2^{-n/2})
. Since the vertical separation between the lines containing
w_{I}
and
\widehat{w}
is certainly
\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
, Proposition
9.16
is applicable with
(Q_{1},w_{1}):=(Q_{I},w_{I})\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\times\mathcal{C}(Q_{I})
and
(Q_{2},w_{2}):=(\hat{Q},\widehat{w})\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}\times\mathcal{C}(\hat{Q})
, and implies

(9.36)

q_{I,\ell}(y)=q_{Q_{I},\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q_{\hat{Q},\ell}(y)=q(y),\qquad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].

Now, all the pieces are in place to prove (
X1
) in the final case
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}
. Assume, first, that
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, so

\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)q_{I,\ell}(y)+\varphi_{\ell}(y)\cdot\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y).

Combining (
9.34
) and (
9.36
), we see that
q_{I,\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q(y)\approx_{n-1}\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
such that
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}
. Now Corollary
9.15
implies

(9.37)

\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)\approx_{n-1}q(y),\qquad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].

In particular, since
\|\ddot{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Sigma
, we have
|\ddot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)|\leq\Sigma+o(\epsilon)
for
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]
, and (
X1
) then follows by choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, depending on
K,M,\Sigma
.

Assume finally that
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, so

\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)q_{I,\ell}(y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{\ell}(y)\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y)+\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})(y)].

We claim that

(9.38)

\begin{cases}|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{q}(y)|\lesssim_{M}\left(\tfrac{1}{K}+o(\epsilon)\right)2^{-n/2},\\
|\ddot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\ddot{q}(y)|\lesssim_{M}\tfrac{1}{K}+o(\epsilon),\end{cases}\quad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].

Only the latter estimate will be needed here, but the first one is useful later. Together with
\|\ddot{q}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\Sigma
, it implies
\|\ddot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)\|_{L^{\infty}([y_{1},y_{2}])}\leq\Sigma+C_{M}(o(\epsilon)+\tfrac{1}{K})
. Consequently
|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{2})-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y_{1})|\leq 2\Sigma
if we choose
K
large enough depending on
M,\Sigma
, and
\epsilon>0
small enough depending on
K,M,\Sigma
, as we assumed in Proposition
8.3
.

To prove (
9.38
), we will use Lemma
9.12
with
\{\varphi_{I}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}}:=\{\varphi_{I,\ell}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I(\ell)}}\cup\{\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{\ell}\}\cup\{\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{\ell}\}
,

p_{I}\in\{\varphi_{I,\ell},\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow}),\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})\}\quad\text{and}\quad q_{I}\equiv q.

We start by recapping from (
9.35
) that
\tau_{n-1}(\ell^{\uparrow})(y)\approx_{n-1}q^{\uparrow}(y)
and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{\downarrow})(y)\approx_{n-1}q{\downarrow}(y)
for all
y\in[y_{1},y_{2}]
, where
q^{\uparrow}
and
q_{\downarrow}
are the
(\hat{Q},\ell^{\uparrow})
and
(\hat{Q},\ell_{\downarrow})
-approximate quadrics, respectively. The quadrics
q,q^{\uparrow},q^{\downarrow}
are all associated with the common
(\epsilon,H,\Sigma)
-vertical rectangle
\hat{Q}
, so they only differ by a constant. In particular, the first and second derivatives of
q,q^{\uparrow},q^{\downarrow}
agree. Also, it is not hard to see that

\|q-q^{\uparrow}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})},\|q-q_{\downarrow}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{M}2^{-n},

because the graphs of
q,q^{\uparrow},q_{\downarrow}
are
\Pi
-projections of horizontal lines on
\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}}
which best approximate
f(\ell\cap H\hat{Q})
,
f(\ell^{\uparrow}\cap H\hat{Q})
, and
f(\ell_{\downarrow}\cap H\hat{Q})
respectively. We will derive much more precise information at (
9.55
), so we refer the reader there for more details. Consequently, for
\hat{\ell}\in\{\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\}
, and the appropriate choice of
\hat{q}\in\{q^{\uparrow},q_{\downarrow}\}
, we have

\begin{cases}|\tau_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-q(y)|\leq|\tau_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-\hat{q}(y)|+\|\hat{q}-q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{M}2^{-n},\\
|\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-\dot{q}(y)|=|\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-\dot{\hat{q}}(y)|=o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n/2},\\
|\ddot{\tau}_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-\ddot{q}(y)|=|\ddot{\tau}_{n-1}(\hat{\ell})(y)-\ddot{\hat{q}}(y)|=o(\epsilon),\qquad\qquad y\in[y_{1},y_{2}].\end{cases}

With this information (and of course (
9.36
)), and recalling the estimates (
9.8
) for the first and second derivatives of the functions
\varphi_{I,\ell}
and
\varphi_{\ell}
, we are in a position to apply Lemma
9.12
with constants
c_{1},c_{2}\sim K^{-1}
,
\epsilon_{1}\lesssim_{M}1
, and
\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}=o(\epsilon)
. The estimates (
9.38
) follow immediately, and the proof of axiom (
X1
) is complete.

9.2.3.
Verying axioms (
X2
)-(
X3
)

Recall that axiom (
X2
) asks to prove that if
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
satisfy
\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
, then

(9.39)

\theta(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})\leq\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\leq\theta^{-1}(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

By trivial induction, it suffices to prove these inequalities for any pair of
consecutive
lines
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
. Therefore
\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}=2^{-n}
(this notation meant that
\pi_{2}(\ell_{2})-\pi_{2}(\ell_{1})=2^{-n}
). So, fix consecutive lines
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
with
\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
. Then, either

(9.40)

\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1},

or the same holds with the roles of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
reversed. We assume with no loss of generality that (
9.40
) holds. Under these assumptions, we will, in addition to (
9.39
), prove that

(9.41)

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

This will establish axiom (
X3
).

To get started, we introduce separate notation for the upper
\mathcal{L}_{n}
-neighbour of
\ell_{2}
:

\ell_{3}:=\ell_{2}^{\uparrow}\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}.

Thus,
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ell_{3}
, where
\ell_{j}
is consecutive to
\ell_{j+1}
in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and
\ell_{1},\ell_{3}
are neighbours in
\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. For
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, we abbreviate

\varphi_{I,j}:=\varphi_{I,\ell_{j}},\quad\varphi_{j}:=\varphi_{\ell_{j}}=1-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{j})}\varphi_{I,j},\quad\text{and}\quad q_{I,j}:=q_{I,\ell_{j}}.

Now, to prove (
9.39
) and (
9.41
), fix
y\in\mathbb{R}
. An easy case occurs if
y\in\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,[U^{\sharp}(\ell_{1})\cup U^{\sharp}(\ell_{2})]
. Then
\varphi_{I,j}\equiv 0
and
\varphi_{j}\equiv 1
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{j})
, so

\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})+\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})]\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)=\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})

by the definitions (
9.9
)-(
9.10
), and recalling (
9.40
). Using the inductive hypothesis (
X2
) for
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})-\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})
, we find

\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})(y)-\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})(y)]\geq\tfrac{\theta}{2}(\ell_{3}-\ell_{1})=\theta(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}).

The upper bound
\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\leq\theta^{-1}(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})
is proved in the same manner. Similarly, we use the inductive hypothesis (
X3
) for
\tau_{n-1}
to prove (
9.41
):

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)|=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot|\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell_{3})(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell_{1})(y)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\delta\cdot 2^{-(n-1)/2}\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

We are now done in the case
y\in\mathbb{R}\,\setminus\,[U^{\sharp}(\ell_{1})\cup U^{\sharp}(\ell_{2})]
.

Assume next that
y\in U^{\sharp}(\ell_{1})\cup U^{\sharp}(\ell_{2})
. In this case, the inductive assumptions (
X2
)-(
X3
) on
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})
and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})
will not be used to verify (
9.39
) and (
9.41
). Instead, we will rely solely on (
X4
). By definition of the sets
U^{\sharp}(\ell_{j})
, the following is true for at least one index
j\in\{1,2\}
: there exists
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j}^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j\downarrow})
, where
m=\lceil n/2\rceil
, such that

(9.42)

|y-f_{2}(w)|\leq\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2}.

The possible lines
\ell_{j},\ell_{j}^{\uparrow},\ell_{j\downarrow}
occurring here, for
j\in\{1,2\}
, are
\ell_{1\downarrow},\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
, but to make the following discussion symmetric, we artificially add the possibility
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{3}^{\uparrow})
. In other words, we only use the information

(9.43)

w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{1\downarrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{1})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{2})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{3})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{3}^{\uparrow}),

see Figure
3
.

\begin{overpic}[Image4.pdf]
\put(56.0,29.5){$\hat{Q}$}
\put(74.0,30.0){$\widehat{w}$}
\put(56.0,6.0){$w$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3.
A possible configuration of the points
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
,
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{C}_{m}
and the rectangle
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be a rectangle with corner
w
. Assumption (
9.43
) enables us to use Corollary
9.25
much like we did while verifying axiom (
X1
). The conclusion is that there exists a corner
\widehat{w}
of the dyadic parent of
Q
which lies on
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{3}
, and is also the corner of some rectangle
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
(which is either the dyadic parent or sibling of
Q
, depending on the parity of
n
, as we saw in the proof of Corollary
9.25
). We write
\hat{\ell}\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\}
for the line such that
\widehat{w}\in\hat{\ell}
.

Recall from (
9.42
) that
|y-f_{2}(w)|\leq\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2}
. Since
d_{\mathrm{par}}(w,\widehat{w})\leq 100\cdot 2^{-m}=100\cdot 2^{-n/2}
, we deduce from Lemma
9.11
that

(9.44)

y\in B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),K2^{-(n-1)/2}).

This inclusion enables us to apply the inductive hypothesis (
X4
) on
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})(y)
and
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})(y)
. Namely, for both
\ell:=\ell_{1}
and
\ell:=\ell_{3}
, the line
\hat{\ell}\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\}
satisfies
\hat{\ell}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}\}
, where
\ell^{\uparrow},\ell_{\downarrow}
refer to the generation
n-1
neighbours of
\ell
. It follows that

\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}(\ell_{\downarrow}),\qquad\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\}.

Since also (
9.44
) holds, we may infer inductively from axiom (
X4
) that

(9.45)

\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)\approx_{n-1}q_{\hat{Q},\ell}(y),\qquad\ell\in\{\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\},

where
q_{\hat{Q},\ell}
is the
(\hat{Q},\ell)
-approximate quadric. Let us, at this point, recall the definitions

(9.46)

\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{1})}\varphi_{I,1}(y)\cdot q_{I,1}(y)+\varphi_{1}(y)\cdot\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})(y)

and

(9.47)

\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)\cdot q_{I,2}(y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(y)\cdot[\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{1})(y)+\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{3})(y)].

We just deduced in (
9.45
) that
\tau_{n-1}(\ell_{j})(y)\approx_{n-1}q_{\hat{Q},\ell_{j}}(y)
for
j\in\{1,3\}
. We would like to know, in addition, that whenever
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,j}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
, then
q_{I,j}(y)\approx_{n}q_{\hat{Q},\ell_{j}}(y)
. This can be deduced from Proposition
9.16
, essentially by the same argument we used at (
9.36
). We repeat the brief details.

Let
j\in\{1,2\}
, and let
I:=I_{n}(w_{I})\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{j})
be an interval such that

w_{I}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j}^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{j\downarrow})\subset\ell_{1\downarrow}\cup\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{3},

and

(9.48)

y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,j}\subset\kappa_{0}I=B(f_{2}(w_{I}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\subset B(f_{2}(w_{I}),100K2^{-n/2}).

Let
Q_{I}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be the rectangle with corner
w_{I}
such that
q_{I,j}
is the
(Q_{I},\ell_{j})
-approximate quadric, and recall that
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
was a rectangle with corner
\widehat{w}
. Combining (
9.44
) and (
9.48
), and noting that the lines containing
w_{I}
,
\widehat{w}
, and
\ell_{j}
, all lie in
\{\ell_{1\downarrow},\ldots,\ell_{3}\}
, we deduce from Proposition
9.16
that

(9.49)

q_{I,j}(y):=q_{Q_{I},\ell_{j}}(y)\approx_{n}q_{\hat{Q},\ell_{j}}(y)\text{ for }j\in\{1,2\}\text{ and }I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{j})\text{ with }y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,j}.

Let us abbreviate
q_{j}:=q_{\hat{Q},\ell_{j}}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. We are very close to completing the proof of (
9.41
), hence (
X3
). Combining (
9.49
) with (
9.45
)-(
9.46
), it first follows that

(9.50)

\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\approx_{n-1}q_{1}(y).

(This uses Corollary
9.15
in the same way as (
9.37
)). Similarly, combining (
9.49
) with (
9.45
), (
9.47
), one can deduce (using Lemma
9.12
) that

(9.51)

\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)\approx_{n-1}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)q_{2}(y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(y)\cdot[q_{1}(y)+q_{3}(y)],

and further

(9.52)

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\dot{q}_{2}(y)|\lesssim_{M}\left(\tfrac{1}{K}+o(\epsilon)\right)\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

This is proven in exactly the same way as the first assertion in (
9.38
), so we omit the details; the main point is, again, that the quadrics
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
only differ by a constant
\lesssim_{M}2^{-n}
. Combining (
9.50
) and (
9.52
), we estimate

|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)|\lesssim_{M}|\dot{q}_{1}(y)-\dot{q}_{2}(y)|+\left(o(\epsilon)+\tfrac{1}{K}\right)\cdot 2^{-n/2}=\left(o(\epsilon)+\tfrac{1}{K}\right)\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

Now (
9.41
) follows by choosing
K\geq 1
small enough depending on
\delta,M
, and
\epsilon>0
small enough depending on
K,M,\Sigma
. This completes the proof of (
X3
).

We turn to proving (
X2
). Using (
9.50
), and that
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}+\varphi_{2}\equiv 1
, we expand

\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\approx_{n-1}q_{1}(y)\left[\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)+\varphi_{2}(y)\right]=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)q_{1}(y)+\varphi_{2}(y)q_{1}(y).

To estimate the difference
\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)
we subtract the approximation (
9.51
) from the one above:

\displaystyle\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)

\displaystyle\approx_{n-1}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)q_{2}(y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(y)\cdot[q_{1}(y)+q_{3}(y)]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y)q_{1}(y)-\varphi_{2}(y)q_{1}(y)

(9.53)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}(y)[q_{3}(y)-q_{1}(y)]+[q_{2}(y)-q_{1}(y)]\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}(y).

To conclude the proof of (
X2
) from here, it suffices to show that

(9.54)

q_{3}(y)-q_{1}(y)\sim_{N}2^{-n}\sim_{N}q_{2}(y)-q_{1}(y),

recalling that
N\sim_{M,\Sigma}1
. Indeed, plugging these estimates to (
9.53
), and using
\varphi_{2}+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell_{2})}\varphi_{I,2}\equiv 1
, we find that
\eqref{form56}\sim_{N}2^{-n}
. Consequently, the computation preceding (
9.53
), and the definition of the "
\approx_{n}
" notation imply that
\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)
is within additive error of
o(\epsilon)\cdot 2^{-n}
, from the expression at (
9.53
), which is
\sim_{N}2^{-n}
. Choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, depending only on
K,M,\Sigma
, it follows that
\tau_{n}(\ell_{2})(y)-\tau_{n}(\ell_{1})(y)\sim_{N}2^{-n}
, as claimed in (
X2
). This completes the proof of (
X2
) in the case
y\in U^{\sharp}(\ell_{1})\cup U^{\sharp}(\ell_{2})
.

It remains to establish (
9.54
). This argument is very similar to the one we already saw at the end of case
n=0
. We first recall that the signature of
\mathcal{T}
, and hence
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}
is
+
, which meant that the
(N,\epsilon\ell(\hat{Q}))
-QIE

F:=F_{\hat{Q}}:=\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}}}\circ\iota_{\hat{Q}}=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}}}\circ\Pi\circ\iota_{\hat{Q}}\colon H\hat{Q}\to\mathbb{W}

is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(\hat{Q})
-increasing on the rectangle
B\hat{Q}\subset H\hat{Q}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}=2N\epsilon
. This fact was established in Lemma
4.45
, assuming
H\geq A(1+\Sigma)^{2}MB
. The quadric straightening map
\psi:=\psi_{\mathbb{V}_{\hat{Q}}}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{W}
appearing in the definition of
F
was introduced just above (
4.31
). It had the form

\psi(y,t)=(y,t-\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}-by),

where
a
and
b
are the common coefficients of approximate
(\hat{Q},\ell)
-quadrics, all of which have the form
q_{\ell}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}ay^{2}+by+c_{\ell}
. In particular, the quadrics
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
appearing in (
9.54
) can be written in this general form for certain coefficients
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q_{i}-q_{j}\equiv c_{i}-c_{j}
for
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. So, proving (
9.54
) is equivalent to showing that

(9.55)

c_{2}-c_{1}\sim_{N}\sim 2^{-n}\sim_{N}c_{3}-c_{1}.

To prove (
9.55
), we pick (any)
3
vertically aligned points

(y_{0},t_{1}),(y_{0},t_{2}),(y_{0},t_{3})\in B\hat{Q}\quad\text{with}\quad(y_{0},t_{j})\in\ell_{j},\,j\in\{1,2,3\}.

This can be done for
B\geq 5
, since
\hat{Q}
had a corner
\widehat{w}\in\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{3}
, and
\operatorname{dist}(\ell_{i},\ell_{j})\leq 2\ell(\hat{Q})
for
1\leq i,j\leq 3
. Evidently
t_{j+1}=t_{j}+2^{-n}
, so in particular
\|t_{j+1}-t_{j}\|=2^{-n/2}
, for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Since
F
is vertically
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(\hat{Q})
-increasing, in particular the map
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t))
is
\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(\hat{Q})
-increasing on
([t_{1},t_{2}],\|\cdot\|)
. Since
\|t_{j+1}-t_{j}\|>\epsilon^{\prime}\ell(\hat{Q})
(recall from (
4.43
) that
\epsilon\ll N^{-10}
), this implies

c_{3}=\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{3}))>c_{2}=\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{2}))>c_{1}=\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{1})).

Here we also used formula (
4.35
), which says that
(\pi_{2}\circ F)|_{\ell}\equiv c_{\ell}
. To establish the more quantitative estimates (
9.55
), we use Lemma
3.30
, which implies that the map
t\mapsto\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t))
is an
(N,2\epsilon\ell(\hat{Q}))
-QIE on
([t_{1},t_{3}],\|\cdot\|)
. Hence,

\displaystyle(2N)^{-1}2^{-n/2}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}2^{-n/2}-2\epsilon\ell(\hat{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{j+1}))-\pi_{2}(F(y_{0},t_{j}))\|=\|c_{j+1}-c_{j}\|

\displaystyle\leq N2^{-n/2}+2\epsilon\ell(\hat{Q})\leq 2N\cdot 2^{-n/2},\quad j\in\{1,2\}.

This completes the proof of (
9.55
), and consequently the proofs of (
9.54
) and (
X2
).

9.2.4.
Verifying axiom (
X4
)

The definition of
\tau_{n}(\ell)
has been set up so that axiom (
X4
) is easy to verify. Write
m:=\lceil n/2\rceil
,
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, and let
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be a rectangle with corner
w
, and let
q:=q_{Q,\ell}
be the
(Q,\ell)
-approximate quadric. The claim is, then, that

(9.56)

\tau_{n}(y)\approx_{n}q(y),\qquad y\in B(f_{2}(w),K2^{-n/2})=:I_{0}.

Fix
y\in I_{0}
. Recall from the definition of the functions
\{\varphi_{I,\ell}\}_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}
that

\mathbf{1}_{U(\ell)}\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}\leq 1,

so consequently
\varphi_{\ell}=1-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}
vanishes on
U(\ell)
. Since
I_{0}\subset U(\ell)
, we have

\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)\cdot q_{I,\ell}(y),\qquad y\in I_{0},

regardless of whether
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
or
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n}\,\setminus\,\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
. Now, all that remains to be verified is:

(9.57)

q_{I,\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q(y)\qquad\text{whenever}\quad I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)\text{ and }y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}.

Indeed, once (
9.57
) is settled, the approximation (
9.56
) follows from Corollary
9.15
.

The verification of (
9.57
) is similar to arguments we have already recorded multiple times, but let us record them once more, since (
X4
) is perhaps the most fundamental axiom. Let
I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)
be such that
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}
. Thus, there exists
w_{I}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
such that

y\in B(f_{2}(w_{I}),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})\cap B(f_{2}(w),K2^{-n/2}).

Let
Q_{I}\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be a rectangle with corner
w_{I}
such that
q_{I,\ell}=q_{Q_{I},\ell}
is the
(Q_{I},\ell)
-approximate quadric. Since
w,w_{I}\in\ell_{\downarrow}\cup\ell\cup\ell^{\uparrow}
, and the separation of these lines is bounded by
\leq 100\cdot 2^{-n}
, Proposition
9.16
is applicable and yields (
9.57
) directly. This completes the verification of axiom (
X4
).

9.2.5.
Verifying axiom (
X5
)

Let
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The claim is that

(9.58)

|\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)-\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)-\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell)(y)|\leq\delta\cdot 2^{-n/2}.

Since
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
, the definition of
\tau_{n}(\ell)
is

(9.59)

\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)}\varphi_{I,\ell}(y)\cdot q_{I,\ell}(y)+\varphi_{\ell}(y)\cdot\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

If
y\notin U^{\sharp}(\ell)
, then simply
\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)=\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)
and
\dot{\tau}_{n}(\ell)(y)=\dot{\tau}_{n-1}(\ell)(y)
, and there is nothing to prove. So, let us assume that
y\in U^{\sharp}(\ell)
. In other words, with
m:=\lceil n/2\rceil
, there exists
w\in\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell)\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell^{\uparrow})\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}(\ell_{\downarrow})
such that
y\in B(f_{2}(w),\kappa_{0}K2^{-n/2})
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{m}
be a rectangle with corner
w
. In this case, the verification of (
9.58
) will only be based on the inductive information from axiom (
X4
), which implies that
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)
is close to a certain quadric around
y
. The details are extremely similar to those already seen during the proof of axiom (
X1
) (more precisely the final case where
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{5}K\cdot 2^{-n/2}
), so we just recall the skeleton of the argument: one begins by finding a corner point
\widehat{w}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
on either
\ell
, or its
\mathcal{L}_{n-1}
-neighbour, with the property

(9.60)

y\in B(f_{2}(\widehat{w}),K2^{-(n-1)/2}).

Then, one selects a rectangle
\hat{Q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil}
with corner
\widehat{w}
, and uses (
9.60
) and (
X4
) inductively to show that
\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)\approx_{n-1}q(y)
, where
q:=q_{\hat{Q},\ell}
is the
(\hat{Q},\ell)
-approximate quadric; see (
9.34
) for comparison. Next, one proves that all the quadrics
q_{I,\ell}
appearing in (
9.59
) with
y\in\operatorname{spt}\varphi_{I,\ell}
must also satisfy
q_{I,\ell}(y)\approx_{n}q(y)
. For the details, see the proof of (
9.36
). From this, it follows from Corollary
9.15
that
\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)\approx_{n-1}q(y)
(compare with (
9.37
)), and finally
\tau_{n}(\ell)(y)\approx_{n-1}\tau_{n-1}(\ell)(y)
. This implies (
9.58
) if
\epsilon>0
is chosen sufficiently small, depending only on
\delta,K,M,\Sigma
.

The axioms (
X1
)-(
X5
) have been verified, and the proof of Proposition
8.3
is complete. Since the proof of Theorem
1.3
had previously been reduced to establishing Proposition
8.3
, also the proof of Theorem
1.3
is complete.

Appendix A
Proof of the quadric comparison lemma

Here we prove Lemma
9.22
, whose statement we now recall:

Lemma A.1
.

Let
q\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a quadratic function, and let
I=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval. Write

m:=\max\{|q(a)|,|q(b)|\}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{m}:=\max\{|\dot{q}(a)|,|\dot{q}(b)|\}.

Then, for all
r\geq 1
and
t\in rI
,

\ |q(t)|\lesssim(m+|I|\dot{m})r^{2},\quad|\dot{q}(t)|\lesssim(\dot{m}+\tfrac{m}{|I|})r\quad\text{and}\quad|\ddot{q}(t)|\lesssim\tfrac{m}{|I|^{2}}+\tfrac{\dot{m}}{|I|}.

Proof.

We first prove the case
[a,b]=[0,1]
. The quadric
q
can be written as

q(t)=q(0)+\dot{q}(0)t+\tfrac{1}{2}\ddot{q}(0)t^{2},

where
|q(0)|\leq m
,
|\dot{q}(0)|\leq\dot{m}
, and

(A.2)

|\ddot{q}(t)|\equiv|\ddot{q}(0)|\leq 2|q(1)|+2|q(0)|+2|\dot{q}(0)|\leq 4m+2\dot{m}.

Consequently,

(A.3)

|q(t)|\leq m+\dot{m}|t|+(4m+2\dot{m})t^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\dot{q}(t)|\leq\dot{m}+(8m+4\dot{m})|t|.

Next, consider the general case, and define
\bar{q}(t):=q(t(b-a)+a)
. Then,

\bar{m}:=\max\{|\bar{q}(0)|,|\bar{q}(1)|\}=\max\{|q(a)|,|q(b)|\}=m,

and

\dot{\bar{m}}:=\max\{|\dot{\bar{q}}(0)|,|\dot{\bar{q}}(1)|\}=|I|\cdot\max\{|\dot{q}(a)|,|\dot{q}(b)|\}=|I|\dot{m},

and
|\ddot{\bar{q}}(t)|\leq 4\bar{m}+2\dot{\bar{m}}=4m+2|I|\dot{m}
by (
A.2
). Using (
A.3
), we infer that for all
t\in rI
,

|q(t)|=\left|\bar{q}\left(\tfrac{t-a}{b-a}\right)\right|\leq m+|I|\dot{m}\tfrac{|t-a|}{|b-a|}+(4m+2|I|\dot{m})\left(\tfrac{t-a}{b-a}\right)^{2}\lesssim(m+|I|\dot{m})r^{2}

and

|\dot{q}(t)|=\tfrac{1}{|I|}\left|\dot{\bar{q}}\left(\tfrac{t-a}{b-a}\right)\right|\leq\dot{m}+\tfrac{1}{|I|}(8m+4|I|\dot{m})\tfrac{|t-a|}{|b-a|}\lesssim(\dot{m}+\tfrac{m}{|I|})r

and

|\ddot{q}(t)|=\tfrac{1}{|I|^{2}}\left|\ddot{\bar{q}}\left(\tfrac{t-a}{b-a}\right)\right|\lesssim\tfrac{m}{|I|^{2}}+\tfrac{\dot{m}}{|I|},

as claimed.
∎
